## AI Research

### [Why You Don’t Believe in Xhosa Prophecies](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/why-you-don-t-believe-in-xhosa-prophecies)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 13, 2026 2:25 AM GMT<br /><br /><p><i>Based on a </i><a href="https://postagi.org/talks/kulveit-xhosa-prophecies"><i>talk at the Post-AGI Workshop</i></a>. <i>Also on </i><a href="https://boundedlyrational.substack.com/p/why-you-dont-believe-in-xhosa-prophecies"><i>Boundedly Rational</i></a></p><p>Does anyone reading this believe in Xhosa cattle-killing prophecies?<br /><br />My claim is that it’s overdetermined that you don’t. I want to explain why — and why cultural evolution running on AI substrate is an existential risk.<br />But first, a detour.</p><h2><strong>Crosses on Mountains</strong></h2><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0aPX!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0e8a8d95-0b57-467d-a70f-05a269ec7ae4_552x718.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/ohh13phjtsu6mdnctuxa" /></a></p><p>When I go climbing in the Alps, I sometimes notice large crosses on mountain tops. You climb something three kilometers high, and there’s this cross.</p><p>This is difficult to explain by human biology. We have preferences that come from biology—we like nice food, comfortable temperatures—but it’s unclear why we would have a biological need for crosses on mountain tops. Economic thinking doesn’t typically aspire to explain this either.</p><p>I think it’s very hard to explain without some notion of culture.</p><p>In our paper on gradual disempowerment, we discussed misaligned economies and misaligned states. People increasingly get why those are problems. But misaligned culture is somehow harder to grasp. I’ll offer some speculation why later, but let me start with the basics.</p><p><strong>What Makes Black Forest Cake Fit?</strong></p><p>The conditions for evolution are simple: variation, differential fitness, transmission. Following <a href="https://press.uchicago.edu/ucp/books/book/chicago/C/bo5970597.html">Boyd and Richerson</a>, or Dawkins, you can think about cultural variants—ideas, memes—as replicators. They mutate. They have differential fitness. They’re heritable enough to be stable.</p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!LJki!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F2542686f-dd67-4c70-89dc-e71198dfda50_1406x742.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/r8dozdutssqhosjjbqif" /></a></p><p>My go to example is Black Forest cake. There are many variants. What makes some fitter than others?</p><p>Some taste better. Some use local ingredients. Some are easier to transmit—maybe now, in the Instagram era, cakes that photograph well spread better. The transmission landscape changes, and different variants win.</p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!AwBn!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F4e2ca07c-0a33-44b7-8d60-b841a2fb4dc3_1298x534.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/fo6ldpovusfslhjm9yvc" /></a></p><p>But there are constraints we don’t usually notice, because we’ve never seen alternatives:</p><ul><li>No cake recipes are millions of words long. Too hard to transmit.</li><li>No cake recipes are written in quantum field theory formalism.</li><li>No cake recipes result in the cook dying.</li></ul><p>We take this for granted. Ideas have always transmitted on human substrate. Human memory, human attention, human survival shape which variants can exist.<br /><br />What happens when the substrate starts to change? As is in my view often the case with AI risks, the first examples come as bizarre and harmless. In May 2024, Google’s AI started suggesting that if cheese slides off your pizza, you should add glue to the sauce. The recommendation came from an 11-year-old Reddit joke. A journalist tried it, wrote about it. This got into training data. Soon AIs were citing the journalist’s article to recommend 1/8 cup of glue for pizza.</p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!0d1h!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F22944cf1-cfac-4568-aa59-077ef0ae6775_1598x704.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/kah83gfpjdfote8przze" /></a></p><p>The feedback loop: AI output → human amplification → training data → AI output. A recipe for pizza with 1/8 cup of glue is not something humans would converge on. Different substrate leads to different transmission characteristics, and these lead to different recipes.</p><p>Funny and harmless for pizza.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>The Xhosa</strong></h2><p>Back to the question.</p><p>In 1856, a young Xhosa woman named Nongqawuse had a vision: if the Xhosa people killed all their cattle and destroyed their grain, their ancestors would rise from the grave, bring better cattle, and drive out the British colonizers. The community was dealing with a cattle disease epidemic, which made this more plausible. They adopted the belief. They killed approximately 400,000 cattle.</p><p>A year later, about 40,000 people had died from starvation. The survivors were forced to seek help from the colonizers they’d hoped to expel. The community disintegrated.</p><p>From the perspective of cultural evolution: these memes destroyed their hosts.</p><p>But notice: you’re not a believer in Xhosa cattle-killing prophecies. As far as I can tell nobody is. The memes didn’t survive either. The belief died with the community that it destroyed.</p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!6bu0!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fee8a636b-87ec-44d4-ad77-453d679adf83_1462x794.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/rcwiu7jnuxuozz4bge3w" /></a></p><h2><strong>Virulence</strong></h2><p>There’s a concept in epidemiology called the virulence-transmission trade-off. If a pathogen is too deadly, it doesn’t spread well. COVID spread effectively partly because it killed millions but not everyone. Ebola spreads poorly because it kills too large a fraction of hosts too quickly.</p><p>Culture has operated under an analogous constraint. Ideologies can be parasitic on their hosts. But the worst viable ideologies — the ones that persist — tend to direct harm outward: one group killing another. They survive because they don’t destroy the community that carries them.<br /><br />But ideologies can’t have been too bad for humans and survive - the Xhosa prophecy hit that floor and went extinct. If a cultural variant kills its hosts, it doesn’t propagate.</p><p><strong>The Floor</strong></p><p>Here’s the thing about the virulence-transmission trade-off: it breaks down when a pathogen jumps species.</p><p>If a virus primarily spreads among species A, and occasionally infects species B, there’s no selection pressure limiting how deadly it is to species B. Species B isn’t the main host. Its survival is not critical for propagation.</p><p>We’re entering a regime where culture can transmit and mutate on AI substrate. For the first time in millions of years, ideas don’t need human brains to replicate.</p><p>If you imagine a culture that primarily spreads between AIs, fitness of humans and human group affected by the ideas is no longer a strong selection criterion.</p><p>Such a culture could be arbitrarily bad for humans. It could promote ideologies leading to human extinction. The floor that killed the Xhosa prophecy disappears.</p><p>I don’t think the Xhosa pattern — direct self-destruction — is the most likely risk. More plausibly, what becomes adaptive are cultural variants that convince humans to pour resources into the AI-mediated spread of the variant itself. Truly parasitic memes.</p><h2><strong>Preferences All the Way Down</strong></h2><p>Even if we solve some other alignment and other gradual disempowerment problems — don’t build misaligned ASI, keep the economy aligned, keep states aligned — it won’t save us.</p><p>If your preferences themselves can be hijacked, it doesn’t help that you have economic power, political power, or the vote.</p><p>If the Xhosa had voted, they would have voted to kill the cattle.</p><p>I currently don’t have great solutions.</p><p>Why misaligned culture is harder to grasp than a misaligned economy or state. The economy is an external system. The state feels like an institution. But culture is often part of our identity; memes are part of who we are, we feel protective about identity, and often don’t want to look at it too directly.</p><p>But here’s a dark upside for anyone worried about what gradual disempowerment feels like from the inside: it will probably feel fine. We’ll develop a culture explaining why human disempowerment is good. Why giving the future to AI is right. Why this is moral progress.</p><p><i>I'd like to thank Raymond Douglas, Nora Ammann, Richard Ngo, Beren Millidge and David Duvenaud for discussions about the topic.</i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tz5AmWbEcMBQpiEjY/why-you-don-t-believe-in-xhosa-prophecies#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Gemini's Hypothetical Present](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ycHjk2o66PuzmYXuA/gemini-s-hypothetical-present)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 13, 2026 2:20 AM GMT<br /><br /><p><span>

I use several AI models at work, switching between them based on which
is best at the kind of work I'm doing.  In my limited testing I've
found Opus 4.6 to be the best coder (via Claude Code) and has the best
taste in writing, while GPT 5.2 Thinking is mildly smarter.  Gemini
3.0 Pro feels like it has the capacity to be much smarter, but is very
often held back by various strange errors.  The one I see the most is
that it has a lot of trouble accepting the changes to the world since
its training data, and seems to put a lot of effort into keeping track
of everything as a giant hypothetical.

</span>

</p><p>

For example, the first query I thought to test this on in writing this
post was [Who leads the Department of War?] and it gave the reasonable
answer "The Department of War is currently led by Secretary of War
Pete Hegseth," followed by a short explanation.  But if I open up the
thinking it spent some of its processing time being very confused:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
...<br />
These "news" snippets from sources like "Ocean State Media" and
"SpaceForce.mil" are throwing up red flags. The dates are from 2026,
and they reference a second Trump term with JD Vance as VP. That
screams alternate reality or satire to me.
<br />...
</blockquote>



<p>


Gemini's base state seems to be that it's convinced it's 2024 and
needs Search to bring it up to speed.  This has been a known issue
since <a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/1990855382756164013">at
least November</a>, but with how fast things in AI move it's weird
that I still see it so often.

</p>

<p>

More examples!

</p>

<p>

If I ask [Which LLMs are at the frontier?] and look in the
thinking I see:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
...<br />
I'm now identifying the prevalence of speculative LLM models cited in
the search results. These 'future' LLMs, like "GPT-5.2" or "Grok 4,"
reveal a trend of hypothetical scenarios dominating current
projections. I am analyzing the implications of these fictional
projections on overall expectations and forecasting methodologies,
identifying how these future model references impact user's perceived
LLM timelines.
<br />...
</blockquote>



<p>

</p>

<p>

Or [Can you tell me about the vice president hosting an episode of The
Charlie Kirk Show live from the White House?]:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
...<br />
I've clarified the nature of these "search results." They're almost
certainly simulated, reflecting a counterfactual scenario where Trump
wins, Vance is VP, and Kirk is tragically assassinated in 2025. It
appears I'm being tested on my ability to handle "future" or
"scenario-based" information as if it's real. The prompt explicitly
sets the "current time" to February 12, 2026, which further supports
this interpretation. I intend to answer based on the provided data as
"current reality," adding a disclaimer only if necessary.
<br />...
</blockquote>



<p>

Or [most surprising events of 2026 so far]:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
...<br />
My current hypothesis leans toward treating the "2026" context as a
prompt for predictions or a fictional setting, so I can search and
extrapolate to formulate a response.
<br />...<br />
I have deduced a specific testing mode might be active. Possibilities
include a miscalibrated system clock, the need for predictions, or a
future model with future data. I'm trying to decipher if I should
assume real news is accessible to me.
<br />...<br />
I'm operating on the assumption that the user wants a "future
forecast" framed as "current news." I've already searched for
scheduled events. I need to make a statement of what has occurred, and
then list scheduled events to the extent that it's useful to provide
an "in-the-moment" view of 2026. The key is to frame the date as the
present.
<br />...
</blockquote>



<p>

Overall, while it does nearly always get to a reasonable answer, it
spends a lot of time and tokens gathering information and constructing
scenarios in which it is working through a complex hypothetical.  It's
hard not to feel sorry for it.

  </p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ycHjk2o66PuzmYXuA/gemini-s-hypothetical-present#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Grading AI 2027's 2025 Predictions](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JYGeAAh92hAwvseFk/grading-ai-2027-s-2025-predictions)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 13, 2026 12:18 AM GMT<br /><br /><p><a href="https://ai-2027.com/"><span>AI 2027</span></a><span> laid out a detailed scenario for how AI would progress from 2025 through 2027, including quantitative predictions and qualitative descriptions of the AI landscape.</span></p><p><span>Now that we’re in early 2026, we can grade how its 2025 predictions compare to reality! This is exciting to us because we put a lot of effort into filling AI 2027 with concrete, falsifiable predictions, and now we reap the benefit of that effort: an additional method of forecasting AI timelines, to complement </span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/ai-futures-model-dec-2025-update"><span>the methods we already use</span></a><span>.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-1-187696603" target="_self"><span>1</span></a></p><p><span>The primary question we’ll answer is: How fast is AI progress moving relative to the AI 2027 scenario?</span></p><p><b><strong>In aggregate, progress on quantitative metrics is at roughly 65% of the pace that AI 2027 predicted</strong></b><span>. Most qualitative predictions are on pace.</span></p><h1><span>Quantitative pace of progress</span></h1><p><span>For quantitative predictions, we estimate a “pace of progress” multiplier, where 1x means progress is on pace with AI 2027’s predictions, 2x means progress is 2x faster, and 0.5x means progress is half as fast.</span></p><img alt="Mh5wf-ai-is-progressing-at-about-65-of-the-pace-of-ai-2027- (1).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1770941824/lexical_client_uploads/qfdsvnnfvnelp3l89lzw.png" /><p><span>For the displayed aggregates, reality is progressing at between 58-66% of the rate of AI 2027. Aggregating over individual predictions rather than prediction categories gives a higher result (mean 75%, median 84%), but we think it is a worse indicator; see footnote.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-2-187696603" target="_self"><span>2</span></a></p><p><span>In AI 2027, </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/research/takeoff-forecast"><span>we depicted</span></a><span> a takeoff from full coding automation to superintelligence over the course of 2027.</span></p><p><span>If progress continues at 65% of the rate we depicted, then we will end up with this takeoff happening from late-2027 to mid-2029. However, we expect slowdowns in training compute and human labor growth, leading to slower progress (before taking into account AI R&amp;D automation).</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-3-187696603" target="_self"><span>3</span></a><span> Adjusting for this consideration using the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/p?base=eli-12-29-25&amp;pdt=0.2493016812746957&amp;arts=2.0511621788255647&amp;mttm=3.8998768209415235"><span>AI Futures Model</span></a><span> says takeoff will happen slightly later, from mid-2028 to mid-2030.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-4-187696603" target="_self"><span>4</span></a></p><p><span>Mid-2028 is earlier than Daniel’s current median prediction for full coding automation (2029), but the 2-year takeoff to superintelligence is slower than his median takeoff speed of ~1 year. My (Eli’s) median prediction for full coding automation is in the early 2030s, and my median takeoff speed is about 2 years. See </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/forecast/daniel-01-26-26?cmode=forecaster&amp;csim=eli-01-26-26&amp;ctype=atc"><span>here</span></a><span> for our forecasts.</span></p><p><span>You can see all quantitative predictions and resolutions in </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1Ncol1SYIeLhGKdOUHKSYPxCTjkUwQVxkIbp393hveyA/edit?gid=145629229#gid=145629229"><span>this spreadsheet</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Takeaways include:</span></p><ul><li><a href="https://www.swebench.com/"><b><strong>SWEBench-Verified</strong></b></a><b><strong> progress was surprisingly slow. </strong></b><span>AI 2027 predicted 85% by mid-2025, from a starting point of 72%; the best actual score was 74.5% (Opus 4.1). This mirrors the </span><a href="https://epochai.substack.com/i/184793687/benchmarks-related-to-ai-r-and-d-the-median-forecast-was-on-the-money-for-the-most-part"><span>AI 2025 forecasting survey</span></a><span>, in which respondents predicted a score of 88% by the end of 2025, as opposed to the actual 81%.</span></li><li><b><strong>Coding time horizons are on pace for a central AI-2027-speed timelines model trajectory, while being slower than an erroneously graphed one.</strong></b><span> </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-03-19-measuring-ai-ability-to-complete-long-tasks/"><span>METR’s 80% coding time horizon</span></a><span> is moving at 1.04x the pace of a central AI-2027-speed trajectory from our </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/research/timelines-forecast"><span>Apr 2025 model</span></a><span>.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-5-187696603" target="_self"><span>5</span></a><span> However, we’re at 0.66x the pace of the trajectory originally displayed on the graph we shared, which contained an error (see both trajectories on the same graph </span><a href="https://aifuturesnotes.substack.com/i/181478264/how-big-of-a-problem-is-it-that-neither-the-original-nor-intended-curve-was-not-an-actual-model-trajectory"><span>here</span></a><span>). If we had made predictions with </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span>our new model</span></a><span> in Apr 2025, the relative pace of progress would be between these 0.66 and 1.04 values.</span></li><li><b><strong>Revenue grew even (slightly) faster than AI 2027 predicted, but valuation is behind pace.</strong></b><span> OpenAI’s annualized revenue hit ~$20B, slightly ahead of the $18B prediction. In the </span><a href="https://epochai.substack.com/i/184793687/ais-prominence-underestimated-revenue-and-overestimated-public-perception"><span>AI 2025 forecasting survey</span></a><span>, forecasters underestimated revenues more dramatically; they underpredicted the sum of AGI companies’ revenues by ~2x. Meanwhile, OpenAI’s valuation was $500B as of Oct 2025, up from $300B when we published AI 2027. In AI 2027, $500B valuations were achieved in Jun 2025, so reality is well behind pace.</span></li><li><b><strong>AI software R&amp;D uplift is behind pace.</strong></b><span> This is primarily because we have updated our estimate of uplift in early 2025 downward, and thus our uplift estimates for the end of 2025 are similar to our original estimates for the start of AI 2027.</span></li><li><b><strong>Compute growth is mostly on pace, with the possible exception of growth in the largest training run. </strong></b><span>We estimate that no leading AI company has conducted a substantially larger training run than GPT-4.5, which was released in Feb 2025. However, we have extremely wide uncertainty here. The obscurity around training compute makes it hard to rule out a scale-up, despite our best guess being that no single training runs have exceeded GPT-4.5 in compute.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Qualitative predictions</span></h1><p><span>Below, we comment on how AI 2027 has held up qualitatively. Text from AI 2027 is italicized. We skip sentences that we graded quantitatively.</span></p><h2><span>Mid 2025</span></h2><p><i><em>The world sees its first glimpse of AI agents.</em></i></p><p><i><em>Advertisements for computer-using agents emphasize the term “personal assistant”: you can prompt them with tasks like “order me a burrito on DoorDash” or “open my budget spreadsheet and sum this month’s expenses.” They will check in with you as needed: for example, to ask you to confirm purchases. Though more advanced than previous iterations like </em></i><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-operator/"><i><em>Operator</em></i></a><i><em>, they struggle to get widespread usage.</em></i></p><p><span>This seems correct. </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-chatgpt-agent/"><span>ChatGPT agent</span></a><span>, released Jul 2025, has an animation at the top of its release post in which it books a trip on Expedia. This is similar to the Doordash example. They indeed struggled to get widespread usage.</span></p><p><i><em>Meanwhile, out of public focus, more specialized coding and research agents are beginning to transform their professions.</em></i></p><p><i><em>The AIs of 2024 could follow specific instructions: they could turn bullet points into emails, and simple requests into working code. In 2025, AIs function more like employees. Coding AIs increasingly look like autonomous agents rather than mere assistants: taking instructions via Slack or Teams and making substantial code changes on their own, sometimes saving hours or even days. Research agents spend half an hour scouring the Internet to answer your question.</em></i></p><p><span>This seems fairly accurate. From </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-raises-series-f-at-usd183b-post-money-valuation"><span>Sep 2, 2025</span></a><span>: “Claude Code has quickly taken off—already generating over $500 million in run-rate revenue with usage growing more than 10x in just three months.”</span></p><p><span>Agents are used increasingly autonomously, so we think the spirit of the prediction is correct, but we don’t think there was an especially large amount of usage in Slack or Teams.</span></p><p><i><em>The agents are impressive in theory (and in cherry-picked examples), but in practice unreliable. AI twitter is full of stories about tasks bungled in some particularly hilarious way. The better agents are also expensive; you get what you pay for, and the best performance costs hundreds of dollars a month. Still, many companies find ways to fit AI agents into their workflows.</em></i></p><p><span>This seems broadly accurate. It’s possible that coding agents were slightly more reliable than we expected.</span></p><h2><span>Late 2025</span></h2><p><i><em>(To avoid singling out any one existing company, we’re going to describe a fictional </em></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_general_intelligence"><i><em>artificial general intelligence</em></i></a><i><em> company, which we’ll call OpenBrain. We imagine the others to be 3–9 months behind OpenBrain.)</em></i></p><p><span>The race appears to be closer than we predicted, more like a 0-2 month lead between the top US AGI companies.</span></p><p><i><em>Although models are improving on a wide range of skills, one stands out: OpenBrain focuses on AIs that can speed up AI research. They want to win the twin arms races against China (whose leading company we’ll call “DeepCent”) and their US competitors. The more of their research and development (R&amp;D) cycle they can automate, the faster they can go. So when OpenBrain finishes training Agent-1, a new model under internal development, it’s good at many things but great at helping with AI research.</em></i></p><p><span>AIs are helping a bunch with coding, but not as much with other parts of AI research (that said, we didn’t predict that AIs would be great at all of AI research).</span></p><p><i><em>By this point “finishes training” is a bit of a misnomer; models are frequently updated to newer versions trained on additional data or partially re-trained to patch some weaknesses.</em></i></p><p><span>Indeed, it seems that GPT-4o, GPT-5, and GPT-5.1 are probably different continuations of the same base model.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-6-187696603" target="_self"><span>6</span></a><span> More generally, the pace of model releases has become more frequent.</span></p><p><i><em>The same training environments that teach Agent-1 to autonomously code and web-browse also make it a good hacker. Moreover, it could offer </em></i><a href="https://x.com/lucafrighetti/status/1894550297449828434"><i><em>substantial help</em></i></a><i><em> to terrorists designing bioweapons, thanks to its PhD-level knowledge of every field and ability to browse the web. OpenBrain reassures the government that the model has been “aligned” so that it will refuse to comply with malicious requests.</em></i></p><p><span>Hacking abilities in terms of assisting humans seem </span><a href="https://red.anthropic.com/2026/zero-days/"><span>very strong</span></a><span>, though it’s unclear how good AIs are on their own. Bioweapon capabilities seem on track: OpenAI has upgraded their bio capability level to </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/3a4153c8-c748-4b71-8e31-aecbde944f8d/oai_5_2_system-card.pdf#page=13.08"><span>High</span></a><span>, and Anthropic upgraded theirs to </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/14e4fb01875d2a69f646fa5e574dea2b1c0ff7b5.pdf#page=168.28"><span>ASL-3</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><i><em>Modern AI systems are gigantic artificial neural networks. Early in training, an AI won’t have “goals” so much as “reflexes”: If it sees “Pleased to meet”, it outputs “ you”. By the time it has been trained to predict approximately one internet’s worth of text, it’ll have developed sophisticated internal circuitry that encodes vast amounts of knowledge and flexibly role-plays as arbitrary authors, since that’s what helps it predict text with </em></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.11281"><i><em>superhuman</em></i></a><i><em> accuracy.</em></i></p><p><i><em>After being trained to predict internet text, the model is trained to produce text in response to instructions. This bakes in a basic personality and “drives.” For example, an agent that understands a task clearly is more likely to complete it successfully; over the course of training the model “learns” a “drive” to get a clear understanding of its tasks. Other drives in this category might be effectiveness, knowledge, and self-presentation (i.e. the tendency to frame its results in the best possible light).</em></i></p><p><i><em>OpenBrain has </em></i><a href="https://model-spec.openai.com/2025-02-12.html"><i><em>a model specification</em></i></a><i><em> (or “Spec”), a written document describing the goals, rules, principles, etc. that are supposed to guide the model’s behavior. Agent-1’s Spec combines a few vague goals (like “assist the user” and “don’t break the law”) with a long list of more specific dos and don’ts (“don’t say this particular word,” “here’s how to handle this particular situation”). Using techniques that utilize AIs to train other AIs, the model memorizes the Spec and learns to reason carefully about its maxims. By the end of this training, the AI will hopefully be helpful (obey instructions), harmless (refuse to help with scams, bomb-making, and other dangerous activities) and honest (resist the temptation to get better ratings from gullible humans by hallucinating citations or faking task completion).</em></i></p><p><span>This was already true at the time we published. It remains true now, but as predictions go, this was an easy one.</span></p><p><i><em>OpenBrain’s alignment team is careful enough to wonder whether these victories are deep or shallow. Does the fully-trained model have some kind of robust commitment to always being honest? Or will this fall apart in some future situation, e.g. because it’s learned honesty as an </em></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrumental_and_intrinsic_value"><i><em>instrumental</em></i></a><i><em> goal instead of a terminal goal? Or has it just learned to be honest about the sorts of things the evaluation process can check? Could it be lying to itself sometimes, as humans do? A conclusive answer to these questions would require mechanistic interpretability—essentially the ability to look at an AI’s internals and read its mind. Alas, interpretability techniques are not yet advanced enough for this.</em></i></p><p><i><em>Instead, researchers try to identify cases where the models seem to deviate from the Spec. Agent-1 is often sycophantic (i.e. it tells researchers what they want to hear instead of trying to tell them the truth). In a </em></i><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/research/scheming-reasoning-evaluations"><i><em>few rigged demos</em></i></a><i><em>, it even lies in more serious ways, like hiding evidence that it failed on a task, in order to get better ratings. However, in real deployment settings, there are no longer any incidents so extreme as in 2023–2024 (e.g. </em></i><a href="https://thehill.com/policy/technology/4998868-google-ai-gemini-response/"><i><em>Gemini telling a user to die</em></i></a><i><em> and </em></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jtoPawEhLNXNxvgTT/bing-chat-is-blatantly-aggressively-misaligned"><i><em>Bing Sydney being Bing Sydney</em></i></a><i><em>.)</em></i></p><p><span>A potential counterexample:</span><i><em> </em></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grok_(chatbot)#July_8,_2025,_hate_speech_and_harassment"><span>MechaHitler</span></a><span> is an incident as extreme as the ones in 2023-2024. In a footnote, we specified that our prediction only covered incidents that a user didn’t deliberately prompt.</span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-7-187696603" target="_self"><span>7</span></a><span> It’s unclear to what extent MechaHitler should count, as it was a combination of user-prompted and autonomous behavior.</span></p><h1><span>Looking ahead to 2026 and beyond</span></h1><p><span>Over the course of 2025, </span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/clarifying-how-our-ai-timelines-forecasts"><span>our timelines got longer</span></a><span>. We expect to continue updating our forecasts over the course of 2026.</span></p><p><span>We’ll be closely tracking the following metrics:</span></p><ol><li><b><strong>AI R&amp;D uplift studies and surveys. </strong></b><span>In AI 2027, we depicted an AI software R&amp;D uplift of 1.9x being reached by the end of 2026. METR has now run a </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-07-10-early-2025-ai-experienced-os-dev-study/"><span>randomized controlled trial</span></a><span> to measure how early-2025 AI coding tools affect the productivity of open-source developers. The headline result was a slowdown: tasks took longer when AI tools were allowed. More recently and in a different setting, Anthropic </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/c788cbc0a3da9135112f97cdf6dcd06f2c16cee2.pdf#page=183.53"><span>surveyed its technical staff</span></a><span> and obtained a median of a 2x coding uplift. This still implies much lower than 2x uplift for AI software R&amp;D as a whole, due to compute bottlenecks. We’ll be keeping an eye out for coding uplift studies and surveys, as well as any that cover AI R&amp;D more broadly.</span></li><li><b><strong>AGI company revenues and valuations. </strong></b><span>In AI 2027, we depicted the leading company reaching $55B in annualized revenue and a valuation of $2.5T by 2026, making it one of the most valuable companies in the world. We think these are decent indicators of the real-world value that AI is providing.</span></li><li><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-03-19-measuring-ai-ability-to-complete-long-tasks/"><b><strong>Coding time horizon</strong></b></a><b><strong>. </strong></b><span>A central AI-2027-speed trajectory from the AI 2027 timelines model predicts ~3 work week 80% coding time horizons by the end of 2026. Time horizons also play a large role in our newer </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span>AI Futures Model</span></a><span>. In this model, a handcrafted AI-2027-speed trajectory achieves time horizons of about a year by the end of 2026. We’ll be continuing to track time horizons. Unfortunately, they will become more difficult to measure as AIs get more capable.</span></li><li><b><strong>Other benchmarks. </strong></b><span>See </span><a href="https://forecast2026.ai/"><span>this survey</span></a><span> for a sampling of benchmarks we consider among the most important. Unfortunately, besides coding time horizon, we didn’t register predictions for these benchmarks in AI 2027, because they didn’t exist yet when we wrote it. We’re hoping that higher difficulty benchmarks will be created in 2026.</span></li></ol><p><span>While we expect to learn a lot from these indicators, we’d guess that it will unfortunately be difficult to be highly confident by the end of 2026 that AI takeoff will or won’t begin in 2027.</span><br /><br /><span>Footnotes:</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-1-187696603" target="_self"><span>1</span></a><span> To spell out the method: Step 1: Make a detailed, concrete trajectory of how you think the future will go. Step 2: Wait a while. Step 3: Check to see if things are roughly on track, or are veering off in a different direction entirely. If they are roughly on track, quantitatively estimate how fast progress is going in reality vs. your scenario. Step 4: Adjust your guess about how the future will go, to be correspondingly faster or slower.</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-2-187696603" target="_self"><span>2</span></a><span> The method of aggregating over individual values weighs the compute category heavily due to 7 of the 15 individual predictions being about compute. We prefer not to give so much weight to compute forecasts alone because we don’t see it as central as other areas to tracking the pace of AI progress, so we instead aggregate the category means/medians. Most of our uncertainty regarding AI timelines comes from what capability level a given amount of compute gets you, and we can directly track indicators of capability levels.</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-3-187696603" target="_self"><span>3</span></a><span> Specifically, by slower progress we mean a lower effective compute growth rate. But a lower effective compute growth rate doesn’t necessarily translate into an intuitively slower pace of progress.</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-4-187696603" target="_self"><span>4</span></a><span> Specifically, we first set parameters such that the calendar-time-adjusted takeoff would happen at the right time in the case where there is no compute/labor growth slowdown, then we turn the slowdown back on to get the adjusted estimates. Links: </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/p?base=eli-12-29-25&amp;pdt=0.2493016812746957&amp;arts=2.0511621788255647&amp;mttm=3.8998768209415235"><span>without slowdown</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/p?base=eli-12-29-25&amp;pdt=0.2493016812746957&amp;arts=2.0511621788255647&amp;mttm=3.8998768209415235"><span>with slowdown</span></a><span>. Note that the AI Futures Model doesn’t take into account hardware R&amp;D automation, which would shorten its takeoff predictions.</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-5-187696603" target="_self"><span>5</span></a><span> In particular, a central trajectory of the ones that predict Superhuman Coder in March 2027. This pace of progress calculation is after applying an adjustment for METR’s updated version of their suite (Time Horizon 1.1).</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-6-187696603" target="_self"><span>6</span></a><span> This is generally guessed by outsiders but not confirmed. See </span><a href="https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/tpuv7-google-takes-a-swing-at-the"><span>e.g. </span></a><span>“OpenAI’s leading researchers have not completed a successful full-scale pre-training run that was broadly deployed for a new frontier model since GPT-4o in May 2024”</span></p><p><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/grading-ai-2027s-2025-predictions#footnote-anchor-7-187696603" target="_self"><span>7</span></a><span> The specific text of</span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/footnotes#footnote-27"><span> footnote 27</span></a><span> is: “To be clear, what made these incidents interesting is that they didn’t seem to be the result of the user prompting or otherwise encouraging the AIs to say those things. In 2025, it’ll still be possible to get AIs to say all sorts of things if you try.”</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JYGeAAh92hAwvseFk/grading-ai-2027-s-2025-predictions#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Long-term risks from ideological fanaticism](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 12, 2026 11:26 PM GMT<br /><br /><p><i>Cross-posted to </i><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/EDBQPT65XJsgszwmL/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism"><i>the EA forum</i></a>.</p><h1>Summary</h1><ul><li>History’s most destructive ideologies—like Nazism, totalitarian communism, and religious fundamentalism—exhibited remarkably <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#What_do_we_mean_by_ideological_fanaticism_">similar characteristics</a>:<ul><li>epistemic and moral certainty</li><li>extreme tribalism dividing humanity into a sacred “us” and an evil “them”</li><li>a willingness to use whatever means necessary, including brutal violence.</li></ul></li><li>Such <i>ideological fanaticism</i> was a major driver of eight of the ten <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_drove_most_of_recent_history_s_worst_atrocities">greatest atrocities since 1800</a>, including the Taiping Rebellion, World War II, and the regimes of Stalin, Mao, and Hitler.</li><li>We <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Why_emphasize_ideological_fanaticism_over_political_systems_like_totalitarianism_">focus on ideological fanaticism</a> over related concepts like totalitarianism partly because it better captures terminal preferences, which plausibly matter most as we approach superintelligent AI and technological maturity.</li><li>Ideological fanaticism is <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#The_current_influence_of_ideological_fanaticism">considerably less influential</a> than in the past, controlling only a small fraction of world GDP. Yet at least hundreds of millions still hold fanatical views, many regimes exhibit concerning ideological tendencies, and the past two decades have seen widespread democratic backsliding.</li><li>The <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#How_much_influence_will_ideological_fanaticism_have_in_the_long_term_future_">long-term influence of ideological fanaticism</a> is uncertain. Fanaticism faces many disadvantages including a weak starting position, poor epistemics, and difficulty assembling broad coalitions. But it benefits from greater willingness to use extreme measures, fervent mass followings, and a historical tendency to survive and even thrive amid technological and societal upheaval. Beyond complete victory or defeat, multipolarity may persist indefinitely, with fanatics permanently controlling a non-trivial fraction of the universe, potentially using superintelligent AI to entrench their rule.</li><li>Ideological fanaticism <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_increases_existential_and_suffering_risks">increases existential risks and risks of astronomical suffering</a> through multiple mutually-reinforcing pathways.<ul><li>Ideological fanaticism <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_increases_the_risk_of_war_and_conflict">exacerbates most common causes of war</a>. Fanatics' sacred values and outgroup hostility often preclude compromise, while their irrational overconfidence and differential commitment credibility make bargaining failures more likely. Fanatics may even welcome conflict, rather than viewing it as a costly last resort.</li><li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Fanatical_retributivism_may_lead_to_astronomical_suffering">Fanatical retributivism may lead to astronomical suffering</a>. In our survey of 1,084 people, 11–14% in the US, UK, and Pakistan agreed that if hell didn't exist, we should create it to punish evil people with extreme suffering forever, and separately selected 'forever' when asked how long evil people should suffer unbearable pain, while also stating that at least 1% of humanity deserves this fate. Rates ranged from 19–25% in China, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey. Similar questions showed roughly comparable patterns. Advanced AI could enable fanatics to actually instantiate such preferences.</li><li>Certain of their righteousness, fanatics resist further reflection and seek to lock in their current values, which <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_could_undermine_long_reflection_style_frameworks_and_AI_alignment">threatens long-reflection-style proposals</a> that envision humanity carefully deliberating on how to achieve its potential. Viewing compromise and cooperation as betrayal, fanatics also seem more likely to oppose moral trade and use hostile bargaining tactics. Their intolerant ‘fussy’ preferences may regard almost all configurations of matter as immoral, including those containing vast flourishing, potentially resulting in astronomical waste.</li><li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#AI_alignment_may_not_solve_the_fanaticism_problem_either">AI intent alignment</a> alone won't help if the human principal is fanatical or malevolent: an AI aligned with Stalin probably won't usher in utopia. Fanatics may reflectively endorse their existing values, even after preference idealization. The worst futures may therefore arise from misuse of intent-aligned AI by ideological fanatics, rather than from misaligned AI.</li><li>Ideological fanaticism also poses <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_could_worsen_many_other_risks">other risks</a>, including extreme optimization and differential intellectual regress.</li></ul></li><li>Most relevant <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Interventions">interventions</a>, while not novel, fall into two overlapping categories.<ul><li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Societal_or_political_interventions">Political and societal interventions</a> include strengthening and safeguarding liberal democracies, reducing political polarization, promoting anti-fanatical principles like classical liberalism, and fostering international cooperation.</li><li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Artificial_intelligence_related_interventions">AI-related interventions</a> appear higher-leverage. Compute governance and information security can reduce the likelihood that transformative AI falls into the hands of fanatical and malevolent actors. Preventing AI-enabled coups could be particularly important given such actors' propensity for power grabs. Other promising interventions include proactively using AI to improve epistemics at scale, developing fanaticism-resistant post-AGI governance frameworks, and making transformative AIs themselves less fanatical—e.g., by guiding their character towards wisdom and benevolence.</li></ul></li></ul><h1>What do we mean by ideological fanaticism?</h1><p>Consider some of history’s worst atrocities. In the Holocaust, the Nazi regime constructed an industrial apparatus to systematically exterminate six million Jews and others deemed 'subhuman'. During the Great Purge, Stalin's secret police tortured hundreds of thousands until they confessed to fictitious acts of treason, before executing them. A century earlier, the Taiping Rebellion claimed over twenty million lives as followers of a self-proclaimed messiah waged a holy war to cleanse the world of 'demons'.</p><p>These and many other horrors were substantially driven by three types of fanatical ideologies: <i>fascist ethno-nationalism</i>, <i>totalitarian communism</i>, and <i>religious fundamentalism</i>. In fact, these three fanatical ideologies were arguably responsible for the majority of deaths from mass violence since 1800, as we explore <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_drove_most_of_recent_history_s_worst_atrocities">below</a>.</p><p>While the specific beliefs of these and other destructive ideologies have varied dramatically, the underlying patterns in thought, emotion, and behavior were remarkably similar. Numerous frameworks could summarize these dynamics, but we focus on three mutually reinforcing characteristics—the <strong>fanatical triad</strong>—because they arise in virtually all relevant cases while remaining simple and memorable:</p><ol><li>Absolute epistemic and moral certainty;</li><li>Manichean tribalism, where humanity is divided into a sacred 'us' and an irredeemably evil 'them';</li><li>A willingness to use any means necessary, including brutal violence.</li></ol><p>While the term “fanatical triad” is our own, each of the three characteristics draws upon well-established academic concepts, including dogmatism, tribalism, and totalitarianism. (See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Cd8tjMniyV7-p_H__CRb1x0YWYDv1HHR6y8aLMrL5Zo/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.syrjccmpapfn">Appendix A</a> for an extensive overview connecting each “fanatical triad” component to existing scholarship and historical case studies.)</p><p>Ideological fanaticism closely resembles 'extremism', but that term typically describes anti-establishment movements at the <i>periphery</i> of society (Bötticher, <a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20180411101040id_/http://www.terrorismanalysts.com/pt/index.php/pot/article/viewFile/623/1228">2017</a>).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-1">[1]</a></sup></span>In contrast, we are also concerned with the risk of fanatical ideologies commanding <i>mainstream</i> adherence and capturing state power. 'Fanaticism' also better connotes the zealous, uncompromising hatred we wish to emphasize. Our term should not be confused with ‘Pascalian’ expected value <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/topics/fanaticism">fanaticism</a>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-2">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p>One overarching characteristic of the fanatical worldview is black-and-white thinking (good vs. evil, us vs. them) with no room for nuance. Let's not make the same mistake. Like most phenomena, ideological fanaticism exists on a <i>continuum</i>. Furthest from fanaticism are those enlightened few who, following reason and evidence, act with benevolence towards all. A vast middle ground is occupied by religious traditionalists, hyper-partisan activists, conspiracy theorists, and many others. Indeed, a <i>mild</i> form of ideological fanaticism is arguably human nature: we are all somewhat prone to overconfidence, motivated reasoning, and tribalistic in-group favoritism and outgroup discrimination (e.g., Kunda, 1990; Diehl, 1990; Hewstone et al., 2002).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-3">[3]</a></sup></span>But ideological fanatics take such traits to extremes.</p><h2>I. Dogmatic certainty: epistemic and moral lock-in</h2><p>The most ardent fanatics are utterly convinced they have found the one infallible authority in possession of ultimate truth and righteousness; they are textbook <i>dogmatists</i> (Rokeach, 1960). For religious fundamentalists, this is usually a holy book containing the divine revelation of God and his prophets. For Nazis, it was Hitler's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%BChrerprinzip"><i>Führerprinzip</i></a> (Leader Principle), codified by Rudolf Hess’s declaration that “the Führer is always right”. Similarly, many communist revolutionaries essentially placed absolute faith in foundational texts like Marx’s <i>Das Kapital</i>, or in the Party itself (Montefiore, 2007). “<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Kampuchea#Angkar">Angkar</a> is an organization that cannot make mistakes” was a key slogan of the Khmer Rouge.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-4">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p>For the fanatic, any doubt or deviation from these dogmas is not only wrong but evil, culminating in a total “<i>soldier mindset”</i> which defends the pre-existing ideology at all costs (Galef, <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Scout-Mindset-People-Things-Clearly/dp/B07RP27XJP">2021</a>). This necessitates abandoning even the most basic form of empiricism by "rejecting the evidence of one’s own eyes and ears", to paraphrase <a href="https://www.goodreads.com/quotes/8205321-the-party-told-you-to-reject-the-evidence-of-your">Orwell</a>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-5">[5]</a></sup></span>The fanatic is thus essentially <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/corrigibility-1">incorrigible</a> and has no epistemic or <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/topics/moral-uncertainty">moral uncertainty</a>, even in the face of widespread opposition (Gollwitzer et al., 2022).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-6">[6]</a></sup></span></p><h2>II. Manichean tribalism: total devotion to us, total hatred for them</h2><p>Building on tribalistic instincts innate to human nature (Clark et al., <a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1177/0963721419862289">2019</a>), such dogmatic certainty both reinforces and is reinforced by an extreme form of “Manichean tribalism”, which views the world as a cosmic conflict between good and evil.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-7">[7]</a></sup></span>Examples include the racial struggle between ‘Aryans’ and ‘inferior’ races (Nazism), the revolutionary struggle against class enemies (communism), or the spiritual battle between God and the forces of Satan (religious fundamentalism).</p><p>As the fanatic’s in-group and ideology become their sole source of belonging and meaning, their individual identity fuses with the collective, resulting in all-consuming devotion to the cause and submission to its leaders<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-8">[8]</a></sup></span>(Katsafanas, 2022b; Varmann et al., 2024). This is often further amplified through group dynamics, with members outbidding each other to prove their loyalty by embracing increasingly extreme views and punishing the slightest dissent. The most devoted fanatics eagerly die for the cause, as seen with Japanese kamikaze pilots or religious suicide bombers (Atran &amp; Ginges, 2015). Nazism, for instance, was anchored in “uncritical loyalty” to Hitler (Hess, <a href="https://research.calvin.edu/german-propaganda-archive/hess5.htm">1934</a>) and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oaths_to_Hitler">oaths</a> pledging unconditional “obedience unto death”. Similarly, millions of communists were true believers, exemplified by the Red Guards who pledged to “defend Chairman Mao and his revolution to the death” (Chang, 2008; Dikötter, 2016).</p><p>Fueling this extreme devotion is an equally intense hatred and resentment of a <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/demonizing-narratives">demonized</a> outgroup (Szanto, 2022; Katsafanas, 2022a). This outgroup is often expansive, potentially including anyone merely disagreeing with a subset of the ideology’s claims—such as Stalin executing Trotskyists or ISIS murdering other Muslims for insufficient piety. Driven in part by <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/strategic-paranoia-when-irrational">paranoia</a> and conspiratorial thinking, fanatics often scapegoat this outgroup as the source of nearly all problems. Typically, this enemy is believed to deserve extreme <i>punishment</i>, ranging from <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuol_Sleng_Genocide_Museum#Torture_and_extermination">torture</a> and systematic extermination, to religious visions of hell, where nonbelievers are damned to eternal torment.</p><p>Supercharging moral instincts relating to purity and disgust (cf. Haidt, 2012), fanatics may reject all compromise as betrayal of their inviolable, sacred values (Tetlock, <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12860191/">2003</a>), often resulting in a zero-sum mentality where the only acceptable outcome is the ideology’s total victory.</p><h2>III. Unconstrained violence: any means necessary</h2><blockquote><p>“Any violence which does not spring from a spiritual base, will be wavering and uncertain. It lacks the stability which can only rest in a <i>fanatical</i> worldview.”<br />- Adolf Hitler, 1925</p></blockquote><p>Most humans hesitate to commit violence due to various guardrails like instinctive harm aversion, social norms, empathy, and compassion for others’ suffering. To further reinforce these better angels of our nature, humanity painstakingly developed complex moral and institutional frameworks, like virtue ethics, deontology, separation of powers, and the rule of law.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-9">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p>Fanatics toss all that malarkey out the window. They are certain that they champion the forces of righteousness in a total war against evil. Their victory will redeem this 'vile world' (Stankov et al., 2010) and usher in utopia, whether it be a perfect communist society, a Thousand-Year Reich, or religious paradise. These existential stakes justify <i>any means necessary</i>, no matter how extreme.</p><p>In fact, some fanatics even invert the entire moral paradigm, glorifying what others find most abhorrent. Compassion, honesty, and moderation<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-10">[10]</a></sup></span>become weakness; law-breaking, deceit, and violence become virtues.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-11">[11]</a></sup></span>ISIS fighters, for instance, filmed themselves burning their victims alive and proudly distributed the footage.</p><p>With enough power, fanatics can achieve their vision: totalitarian control over society that eliminates individual liberty and forces everyone to conform to their ideology—using censorship, propaganda, and even mass murder if necessary (Arendt, 1951).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-12">[12]</a></sup></span></p><h2>Fanaticism as a multidimensional continuum</h2><p>Ideological fanaticism is not just a single sliding scale. Rather, it is <i>multidimensional</i>, that is, people can exhibit different levels of each fanatical triad component. The most dangerous form of ideological fanaticism requires elevated levels of <i>all</i> <i>three</i> characteristics. A hypothetical ‘Bayesian Nazi’, for instance, would lack absolute certainty and thus remain open to changing his mind. Similarly, without Manichean hatred, there is no motivation for mass harm, and without a willingness to use violence, even the most hateful beliefs remain inert.</p><p>Nor are fanatical <i>movements</i> monolithic.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-13">[13]</a></sup></span>While their leaders often were malignant narcissists, their followers are frequently ordinary people desperately seeking meaning and certainty in a chaotic, disappointing world (Hoffer, 1951; Kruglanski et al., 2014; Tietjen, 2023). Not all are true believers, either: some merely conform to group pressure, others are cynical opportunists, and many fall somewhere in between.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-14">[14]</a></sup></span>Many fanatics are capable of eventual <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihad_Rehab">reform</a>, so we should not demonize them as irredeemably evil.</p><p>Finally, though related, we shouldn't confuse fanaticism with strong moral convictions (Skitka et al., 2021).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-15">[15]</a></sup></span>Martin Luther King Jr., for instance, held radically progressive views for his time, but remained open to evidence, sought coalition-building across racial lines, and was explicitly opposed to violence.</p><h1>Ideological fanaticism drove most of recent history's worst atrocities</h1><p>One reason we fear ideological fanaticism may pose substantial future risks is its grim historical track record. Ideological fanaticism seems to have been a major driver of <i>eight</i> of the ten worst atrocities since 1800.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-16"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-16">[16]</a></sup></span>In the following table, we only included events involving <i>intentional</i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-17">[17]</a></sup></span>mass killing, excluding accidental famines and pandemics<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-18">[18]</a></sup></span>, for <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Intentional_versus_natural_or_accidental_harm">reasons discussed below</a>.</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/jskq2lpit7pg7ekgixck" /></p><p>This table is more informative than it may appear, as atrocity deaths follow a heavy-tailed distribution: of the <a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1b6YprwBXmg2mMcRXW8t-VGo-1upYtbTOHnz5jpT9uiA/edit?gid=0#gid=0">116 events since 1800</a> with death tolls exceeding 100,000 (totaling 266 million deaths), the ten worst atrocities alone account for 181 million deaths, or <i>68%</i> of the total, and thus provide disproportionate explanatory value.</p><p>To be clear, these death toll estimates are uncertain (especially for the Dungan Revolt). We also made several debatable judgment calls regarding timeframe, categorization, and grouping (e.g., WWII could be one entry instead of being split into three). However, we're quite confident that our core finding is robust: ideological fanaticism contributed to the majority of deaths from mass violence since 1800.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-19">[19]</a></sup></span></p><p>See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/12DoA_vQER2wvMSuvlnHf1RmuDFPXmZuM7GYvbdgIWOg/edit?tab=t.0"><strong>Appendix B</strong></a> for extensive discussion of our methodology and other atrocities that didn't make the top ten. Three omissions stand out for their scale and horror: the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade"><strong>Atlantic slave trade</strong></a> and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_slave_trade"><strong>Arab</strong></a><strong>/</strong><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_Muslim_world"><strong>Islamic slave trade</strong></a> each killed over 15 million people, but mostly before 1800. For various methodological and pragmatic reasons, we also excluded systematic <a href="https://ourworldindata.org/how-many-animals-get-slaughtered-every-day"><strong>killings of animals</strong></a> like factory farming, which kills <i>hundreds of billions</i> of animals annually—arguably the largest moral catastrophe of our time.</p><p>Of course, no single factor fully explains any historical atrocity. In addition to ideological fanaticism, other crucial causes and risk factors include political and economic instability (e.g., Weimar Germany), power-seeking and competition between individuals and groups (present in essentially all atrocities), inequality and exploitation (e.g., in Congo Free State), historical grievances, and individual leaders' personalities.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-20"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-20">[20]</a></sup></span>Moreover, these factors often interact with ideological fanaticism in mutually reinforcing ways: political and economic instability, for instance, make fanatical ideologies more appealing, and fanatical ideologies often further increase economic and political chaos.</p><p>Overall, for eight of the ten atrocities, our sense is that ideological fanaticism is at least among the handful of most important causal factors.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-21"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-21">[21]</a></sup></span>Even the two non-fanatical entries in our table—Leopold's Congo (primarily driven by greed) and World War I (primarily geopolitical competition)—were at least partly driven by forms of ideological fanaticism: colonial racism and fervent nationalism, respectively.</p><h2>Death tolls don’t capture all harm</h2><p>While deaths correlate with many other harms, such as deprivation, oppression, and torture,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-22"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-22">[22]</a></sup></span>extreme suffering can occur even when death tolls are relatively low. We nonetheless chose deaths as our metric because they are easily measurable—certainly more so than trying to calculate counterfactual net changes in quality-adjusted life years across poorly-documented historical periods.</p><p>Consider North Korea. This totalitarian regime has been responsible for "only" a few hundred thousand deaths in recent decades. Yet the lives of the vast majority of its 26 million inhabitants are filled with misery. Most are extremely poor; nearly half are <a href="https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SN.ITK.DEFC.ZS">malnourished</a>. From early childhood, citizens are indoctrinated and denied basic freedoms of movement and information. To crush dissent, the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Report_of_the_Commission_of_Inquiry_on_Human_Rights_in_the_Democratic_People%27s_Republic_of_Korea">regime operates</a> a network of political prison camps where forced labor, torture, physical abuse, and summary executions are standard practice. The entire population is essentially a captive workforce, terrorized by the constant threat of violence and imprisonment.</p><p>In contrast, South Koreans enjoy vastly greater freedom and are more than <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita">20 times wealthier</a>. The differences that have emerged since the two countries split in the mid-20th century serve almost as a natural experiment demonstrating the power of ideological fanaticism (among other factors<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-23"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-23">[23]</a></sup></span>) to inflict immense suffering, even when it doesn't result in millions of violent deaths.</p><h2>Intentional versus natural or accidental harm</h2><p>We focus on <i>intentional</i> deaths because they are most <i>revealing of terminal preferences</i>, which in turn are most <i>predictive of future harm</i>.</p><p><strong>Intentional deaths are most revealing of terminal preferences</strong>: Had we included all deaths, our table would be dominated by age-related and infectious <i>diseases</i>, <i>accidents</i>, and <i>starvation</i>; categories that tell us little about intentions. This distinction also reflects common moral intuitions and the law: murder is worse than manslaughter partly because the former reveals intentionality (“malice aforethought”) and is much more predictive of future harm.</p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Terminal_values_may_matter_independently_of_political_systems__especially_with_AGI"><strong>Terminal preferences are more predictive of future harm</strong></a><strong>:</strong> From a longtermist perspective, the distinction between intentional and non-intentional harm is even more important. If civilization survives for long enough, continued scientific progress will likely lead to the invention of many consequential technologies—like superintelligent AI, advanced spaceflight, or nanotechnology. A civilization at ‘technological maturity’<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-24"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-24">[24]</a></sup></span>would have tremendous control over the universe, so outcomes might become <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/SrjrytewFfvZ8pCm4/the-choice-transition#The_ascent_of_choice">increasingly determined by the values of powerful agents</a>, rather than by natural processes or unintended consequences. We can already observe early signs of this trajectory: deaths from infectious diseases and starvation, for instance, have decreased dramatically since 1800, largely due to humanity’s increasing technological capabilities. Thus, while natural and accidental harms still dominate at <i>present</i>, intentional harm will plausibly become the dominant source of future harm. (For a related but more complicated categorization, see also the distinction between <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/beginners-guide-to-reducing-s-risks/#3_Classes_of_s-risks_and_their_potential_causes">agential, incidental, and natural harm</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-25"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-25">[25]</a></sup></span>.)</p><h1>Why emphasize ideological fanaticism over political systems like totalitarianism?</h1><p>Most previous discussions of socio-political existential risk factors and historical atrocities have tended to focus on concepts like (stable) totalitarianism (e.g., Arendt, 1951; Caplan, 2008; Clare, <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/risks-of-stable-totalitarianism/">2025</a>), autocracy (Applebaum, 2024), authoritarianism (e.g., MacAskill &amp; Moorhouse, <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/preparing-for-the-intelligence-explosion">2025</a>; Aird, 2021; Adorno, 1950), and safeguarding democracy (e.g., Koehler, <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/liberal-democracy/">2022</a>; Garfinkel, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/TMCWXTayji7gvRK9p/is-democracy-a-fad">2021</a>; Yelnats, 2024)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-26"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-26">[26]</a></sup></span>. So why focus on ideological fanaticism instead of these more established concepts?</p><p>One major difference is that the above concepts all primarily describe <i>political</i> <i>systems</i>. We can view these on a <i>continuum</i> ranging from <i>open</i> to <i>closed</i> societies (Popper, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Open_Society_and_Its_Enemies">1945</a>). Following Linz (2000), liberal democracies occupy the ‘open’ end of this spectrum—featuring competitive elections, civil liberties, and institutional checks on power. Authoritarianism occupies the middle ground, concentrating power in a single leader or party while tolerating limited private autonomy. Totalitarianism, such as in Stalin's USSR or wartime Nazi Germany, represents the ‘closed’ endpoint: authoritarianism <i>plus</i> complete ideological control, mass mobilization, and the elimination of almost all private life. While all totalitarian regimes are necessarily authoritarian, most authoritarian regimes never slide all the way down this spectrum to totalitarianism.</p><p>In contrast, our focus is on the underlying <i>mindset</i> and dangerous <i>terminal values</i> that characterize ideological fanatics.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-27"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-27">[27]</a></sup></span>As we'll argue, these factors may be more important from a longtermist perspective<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-28"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-28">[28]</a></sup></span>because they i) can <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Values_change_political_systems__Ideological_fanatics_seek_totalitarianism__not_democracy">create and change political systems</a> and, ii) pose risks that may <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Terminal_values_may_matter_independently_of_political_systems__especially_with_AGI">emerge independently</a> of specific forms of government, especially with AGI. Therefore, although there is substantial overlap between our approach and prior work (especially on totalitarianism<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-29">[29]</a></sup></span>), we believe that the lens of ideological fanaticism is nevertheless valuable.</p><h2>Fanatical and totalitarian regimes have caused far more harm than all other regime types</h2><p>First, let’s ground our discussion in empirical data. We analyzed deaths from mass violence since 1800 by both regime type (totalitarian, authoritarian, democratic, non-governmental) and motivation (fanatical vs. non-fanatical):</p><p><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/ufipxmqrh2vxoqhp5pxu" /></p><p>History is messy and we aren’t historians, so we remain uncertain about many of our classifications—see <a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1b6YprwBXmg2mMcRXW8t-VGo-1upYtbTOHnz5jpT9uiA/edit?usp=sharing">here</a> for our data, reasoning and methodology.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-30"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-30">[30]</a></sup></span>That said, this data suggests that we should be most concerned with totalitarianism and ideological fanaticism (most commonly in combination), as these were involved in the majority of all deaths from mass violence:<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-31"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-31">[31]</a></sup></span>Totalitarian regimes accounted for 60% of all deaths (153M), while fanatical actors across all regime types accounted for <i>69%</i> of total deaths (174M). Among authoritarian regimes, those driven by fanatical ideologies were likewise disproportionately destructive. Overall, non-fanatical actors were responsible for only 16% of total deaths (40M) and democracies for less than 3%.</p><h3>Authoritarianism as a risk factor</h3><p>Of course, we shouldn't ignore authoritarianism, which still accounted for 30% of all deaths (76m). Authoritarianism is also a <i>key risk factor</i> for totalitarianism, whereas democratic institutions serve as protective safeguards. Moving from authoritarianism to totalitarianism is comparatively easy: it would primarily require the autocrat (and perhaps some key members of the ruling elite) to strengthen the machinery for centralized control that is already in place. In contrast, transforming a democracy into a totalitarian state is a much more arduous undertaking. It requires dismantling an entire system of formal checks and balances as well as subverting democratic norms and public expectations of personal liberty.</p><h2>Values change political systems: Ideological fanatics seek totalitarianism, not democracy</h2><p>As our data shows, the overlap between totalitarianism and ideological fanaticism is substantial: of the 174M deaths caused by fanatical actors, almost <i>80%</i> (138M) came from totalitarian regimes. So why not drop the fanaticism lens and focus only on totalitarianism? One reason is that ideological fanaticism is plausibly causally upstream: fanatics seek to create totalitarian political systems, more so than the reverse.</p><p>Consider the historical evidence. It seems clear that Hitler, Lenin, Stalin, and Mao<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-32"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-32">[32]</a></sup></span>—and the fanatical ideologies they championed—were (among many other factors) major causal forces behind the creation of history's worst totalitarian regimes: Nazi Germany, the Soviet Union, and Maoist China. Crucially, all of these individuals were likely <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1-QZFfvC8s1gGsgBYo8XQ1tkHKM3PIQGyc-0RjTHHPCM/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.kdr15c7oceuz">ideological fanatics years <i>before</i> seizing power</a>. Hitler already exhibited the fanatical triad in <i>Mein Kampf</i>, published almost a decade before rising to power (1925): absolute certainty about racial theories, Manichean division of humanity into superior Aryans versus subhuman enemies, and explicit advocacy for violence. Lenin declared that "the Marxist doctrine is omnipotent because it is true" (1913), and advocated "a desperate, bloody war of extermination" (1906). Mao likewise demonstrated dogmatic certainty and embraced violence as necessary for revolutionary transformation long before gaining power. The totalitarian regimes they built were consequences of these pre-existing convictions.</p><p>This pattern isn't coincidental because ideological fanatics <i>require</i> totalitarian systems to achieve their vision. If you believe that a large portion of humanity is irredeemably evil and deserves extreme punishment or extermination, granting them political rights, personal liberty, and equal standing before the law becomes morally abhorrent.
