And <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/anders-sandberg-best-things-possible-in-our-universe/#the-far-future-of-war-002658">wars in outer space</a> could last for an extraordinarily long time.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-91"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-91">[91]</a></sup></span></p><h2>Fanatical retributivism may lead to astronomical suffering</h2><p>Throughout history, humans have inflicted extreme suffering on those they deemed deserving of punishment. Pre-modern judicial systems employed methods like <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingchi">Lingchi</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanged,_drawn_and_quartered">hanging, drawing, and quartering</a>, and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_by_burning">burning alive</a>—designed not merely to kill but to maximize agony. When fanatical ideologies seized state power, they often implemented such cruelty on an industrial scale: the Nazi concentration camps, Stalin's Gulag, Mao's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laogai">laogai</a> (Chang &amp; Halliday, Ch. 8 &amp; Ch. 23, 2005), and the Khmer Rouge's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuol_Sleng_Genocide_Museum">Tuol Sleng</a> all featured systematic torture. While instrumental purposes like deterrence, information extraction, and social control played a key role, the intensity and scope of suffering in these systems often seemed to exceed what these purposes would require, suggesting that <i>retributive</i> motivations were also at work.</p><p><a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/justice-retributive">Retributivism</a> holds that wrongdoers <i>deserve</i> punishment—that their suffering is intrinsically morally good.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-92"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-92">[92]</a></sup></span>Most people's punishment intuitions are at least partly retributive (Carlsmith, 2006; Goodwin &amp; Gromet, 2014). Fortunately, ordinary retributive attitudes seem usually limited in scope: most people only want to punish a relatively small set of serious wrongdoers, and they don't endorse truly extreme or permanent suffering.</p><p>Some forms of ideological fanaticism, however, could remove both constraints.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-93"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-93">[93]</a></sup></span>Fanatics often view a much <i>larger proportion</i> of people as deserving punishment, sometimes everyone who merely disagrees with their ideology. Moreover, fanatics often view their ideological enemies as irredeemably evil and as deserving of more prolonged and intense suffering (e.g., <a href="https://www.npr.org/sections/thetwo-way/2015/02/04/383742600/jordan-executes-2-militants-after-isis-burns-captured-pilot-alive">ISIS burning enemies alive</a>).</p><p>In the future, such preferences could prove catastrophic. Advanced AI could enable fanatics to inflict extreme suffering on their perceived enemies at unprecedented scale, potentially forever, at relatively small cost.</p><h3>Empirical evidence: how many people endorse eternal extreme punishment?</h3><p>Such scenarios originally struck us as extremely far-fetched. But recent empirical evidence may suggest otherwise.</p><p>We surveyed 3,082 adults across six countries—the United States (majority Christian), the UK (majority atheist/agnostic), China (majority atheist/agnostic), and Turkey, Pakistan, and Saudi Arabia (majority Muslim)—about their attitudes toward extreme, eternal punishment. All subsequent analysis includes only the 1,084 participants who met our inclusion criteria.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-94"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-94">[94]</a></sup></span></p><p>We assessed extreme retributivist attitudes through three questions:</p><ul><li>“If hell didn't exist, or if it stopped existing, we should create it to punish all evil people with extreme suffering forever." [“<strong>create hell</strong>”]</li><li>“If I could create a system that makes deserving people feel unbearable pain forever, I would.” [“<strong>would</strong> <strong>create system</strong>”]</li><li>“We should have a system in the world that makes deserving people feel unbearable pain forever.” [“<strong>want system</strong>”]</li></ul><p>We focus here on participants we call "<i>consistent and concerning</i>": those who agreed (≥5 on the 7-point scale) with at least one of the above, while also selecting "Forever" and at least “1%” on two independent questions:</p><ul><li>“For how long should the most evil or extremely immoral people be made to experience extreme, unbearable pain?” with options ranging from “Not at all”, “Minutes”, “Hours”, etc. to “Millions of years”, and “Forever”. [“<strong>duration</strong>”]</li><li>“Of all the people in the world, what proportion deserve unbearable pain <i>forever?”</i> (Use the slider: 0% to 100%) [“<strong>proportion</strong>”]</li></ul><p>11–14% in the US, UK, and Pakistan were consistent and concerning responders for <strong>create hell</strong>, rising to 19–25% in China, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey. Results for <strong>want system</strong> (8–11% and 19–24%) and <strong>would create system</strong> (7–11% and 16–23%) showed roughly similar patterns.<br />Looking beyond the conjunctive measure, when asked what <i>proportion</i> of humanity deserves unbearable pain forever, more than half of participants<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-95"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-95">[95]</a></sup></span>answered 1% or higher; a quarter answered 7% or higher.</p><p>Wanting hell to be created correlated at <i>ρ</i> = 0.25 with sadism (measured via the <a href="https://www.researchgate.net/publication/359195728_The_Revised_Assessment_of_Sadistic_Personality_ASP-8_Evidence_for_Validity_across_Four_Countries">ASP-8 scale</a>)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-96"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-96">[96]</a></sup></span>and with several of our items assessing ideological fanaticism (<i>ρ</i> = 0.26-0.37, all <i>p</i> &lt; 0.001).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-97"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-97">[97]</a></sup></span>This suggests that ideological fanatics and malevolent actors are indeed more likely to endorse extreme retributive attitudes.</p><p><strong>Caveats and limitations</strong><br />These are seemingly concerning results, but they need to be interpreted with caution.</p><p>The questions involved complex and abstract hypotheticals; responses to such questions are <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/vZ4kB8gpvkfHLfz8d/critique-of-macaskill-s-is-it-good-to-make-happy-people?commentId=mxm3d9FyWp4EGAxXJ">notoriously unreliable</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-98"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-98">[98]</a></sup></span>and hopefully don’t reflect genuine commitments participants would actually act upon.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-99"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-99">[99]</a></sup></span></p><p>One notable limitation is that many participants answered <i>inconsistently</i> across questions, which is why we focus on the conservative conjunctive measure above. In non-Western samples especially, responses for individual questions in isolation were much higher than the numbers we reported above.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-100"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-100">[100]</a></sup></span></p><p>Other limitations include potential differences in meaning across translations<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-101"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-101">[101]</a></sup></span>, the non-representative nature of online survey samples<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-102"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-102">[102]</a></sup></span>, and the inherent unreliability of crowdsourced surveys where participants may quickly click through questions without genuine reflection to maximize hourly earnings.</p><p>Nevertheless, these results are concerning. Substantial fractions of multiple populations seem to endorse extreme retributivist attitudes, even on conservative estimates, and seem to apply them broadly, not just to a few of history’s greatest villains.</p><h3>Religious fanatical retributivism</h3><p>Fanatical retributivist attitudes could translate into astronomical suffering through at least two pathways: religious and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Secular_fanatical_retributivism">secular</a> (discussed below). We focus primarily on the religious case as it seems overall more concerning.</p><p>To our knowledge, no serious theologian has ever advocated actively creating technological hells. Most would likely consider the idea a blasphemous misinterpretation. But such arguably confused views may nevertheless arise, partly because the concept of hell is central to the two largest religions: Christianity (2.4 billion followers) and Islam (2 billion followers). The Bible frequently discusses hell, with <a href="https://timothykeller.com/blog/2008/8/1/the-importance-of-hell">Jesus repeatedly</a> describing it <a href="https://www.thegospelcoalition.org/article/the-uncomfortable-subject-jesus-addressed-more-than-anyone-else/">in detail</a>. The concept of hell (<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahannam">Jahannam</a>) is also central to Islam, with the Quran containing at least 92 “significant passages” about hell, compared to 62 about paradise (Jones, <a href="https://real-j.mtak.hu/18424/1/The-Arabist_28_-_29_2008.pdf">2008, p. 110</a>).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-103"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-103">[103]</a></sup></span></p><p>Various scriptural passages and theological writings articulate doctrines that, when combined with fanatical misinterpretation and transformative technology, become concerning:</p><ol><li>Hell is a physical reality and morally necessary for divine justice (with certain passages indicating that those in heaven witness or even rejoice in divine judgment)</li><li>The suffering is eternal and its intensity far exceeds any earthly pain</li><li>A large fraction of humanity is destined for hell</li></ol><p>The following examples from foundational texts illustrate these doctrines: Thomas Aquinas, arguably Christianity's most influential theologian, wrote in his <i>Summa Theologica</i> that "the blessed will rejoice in the punishment of the wicked [...]. In order that the happiness of the saints may be more delightful to them [...] they are allowed to see perfectly the sufferings of the damned." The Quran states: “Surely those who reject our verses, we will cast them into the Fire. Whenever their skin is burnt completely, we will replace it so they will constantly taste the punishment.” (<a href="https://quran.com/4/56">4:56</a>). According to two hadiths in the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahih_al-Bukhari">Sahih al-Bukhari</a>, the second-most authoritative text after the Quran in Sunni Islam, the ratio of people going to hell compared to paradise will be 100:1 (<a href="https://sunnah.com/bukhari:6529">Book 81, Hadith 118</a>) or even 1000:1 (<a href="https://sunnah.com/bukhari:6530">Book 81, Hadith 119</a>).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-104"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-104">[104]</a></sup></span>See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1MdSEu-CTsCSWAZ4rY7aFU-ZYe9zFHg54rrICtJOOsZU/edit?tab=t.0">Appendix C</a> for further relevant quotes.</p><p>Importantly, many believers reject simplistic readings of holy texts as being incompatible with divine love, and much of contemporary theology tends to emphasize forgiveness and mercy. Within Christianity, doctrines like <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_universalism">universalism</a> (ultimate redemption of all souls) and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annihilationism">annihilationism</a> (the destruction of unredeemed souls at death rather than eternal torment) are popular among laypeople and widely supported by scholars of many denominations. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism">Sufism</a>, one of the oldest Islamic traditions, similarly emphasizes divine mercy over retribution.</p><p><strong>Why might religious fanatics create technological hells?</strong><br />Christianity and Islam unambiguously establish that God alone created hell and reserves judgment exclusively for himself. Most religious believers immediately recognize that human attempts to implement divine punishment on their own would amount to blasphemy and likely violate basic theological principles. The risk thus emerges primarily not from orthodox theology but from confusion or willful misinterpretation—for example, some may want to rationalize their sadistic preferences.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-105"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-105">[105]</a></sup></span>So, how could any religious believer possibly come to believe that they should create hell on their own, rather than leaving it to God?</p><p>Several pathways seem at least conceivable.</p><p><i>Making holy scriptures “come true”</i><br />As we have seen, religious texts describe heaven and hell as concrete realities. Some fanatics may aim to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#AI_alignment_may_not_solve_the_fanaticism_problem_either">reshape reality</a> to correspond to their pre-existing beliefs. The general practice of taking action to fulfill religious prophecies has broad historical precedent—from <a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/politics/wp/2018/05/14/half-of-evangelicals-support-israel-because-they-believe-it-is-important-for-fulfilling-end-times-prophecy/">Christian Zionists supporting Israeli territorial expansion</a> to fulfill end-times prophecy, to ISIS’s disproportionate focus on Dabiq because of a hadith declaring it the site of an apocalyptic battle.</p><p>We may thus speculate that some fanatics may be similarly motivated to immanentize their holy scriptures’ ideas about the afterlife. Concerningly, advanced technologies like transformative AI may allow them to actually create both a paradise where believers can dwell in eternal bliss and a hell where infidels and sinners suffer eternally. From this confused perspective, creating heaven and hell wouldn’t be blasphemy but an act of faith: maximizing the veracity of their religion by physically manifesting its claims.</p><p><i>Sycophantic AIs</i><br />It’s conceivable that an (possibly misaligned) artificial superintelligence might create hell without explicit instructions by their users, because of extreme sycophancy—not merely telling users what they want to hear, but <i>reshaping</i> <i>reality</i> so users experience what they want or expect to exist (or believe should exist).</p><p>Consider a superintelligent AI (semi-)aligned with a religious fanatic. As we explore in more detail <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#AI_alignment_may_not_solve_the_fanaticism_problem_either">below</a>, fanatics typically prefer vindication of existing beliefs over truth-seeking. Consequently, such an AI might aim to make religious scriptures literally true rather than helping its user discover what's actually real. Unless such an AI had strong principles against deception—the kinds of epistemic principles fanatics are not known for—it might autonomously create heaven and hell to validate its user's beliefs. The AI could make it appear that heaven and hell were created by God, or even present itself as God. Finding themselves in what appears to be the paradise described in their scriptures, fanatics might also expect to be able to witness the suffering of those damned to hell since their holy books describe this as a feature of paradise. An AI aiming to fulfill all expectations might thus create hell to "complete the experience".</p><p><i>Idiosyncratic interpretations and emerging technologies</i><br />Religious texts are open to a wide variety of interpretations. It seems hard to rule out—especially in light of the concerning empirical evidence discussed above—that some misguided fanatics would conclude that creating heaven and hell is somehow an act of devotion or their sacred duty.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-106"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-106">[106]</a></sup></span>Religious interpretations may also change over time, and other dynamics could further exacerbate fanatical tendencies (e.g., so-called <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#More_speculative_dynamics__purity_spirals_and_self_inflicted_suffering">purity spirals</a> which we’ll explore later).</p><p>Future technology like superintelligent AI or whole brain emulation may also interact with religious beliefs in ways we can't fully anticipate.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-107"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-107">[107]</a></sup></span>These could spawn entirely new religious movements, syncretic reinterpretations of existing faiths, or idiosyncratic religious beliefs held by powerful individuals.</p><h3>Secular fanatical retributivism</h3><p>As our survey data suggests, extreme retributivist intuitions may not be uncommon even among atheists and agnostics. The neuroscientist Robert Sapolsky, for instance, opens his book <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Behave-Robert-M-Sapolsky-audiobook/dp/B06XVYHXDV"><i>Behave</i></a> with a vivid description of his own retributivist fantasy about Hitler, culminating in wanting him to experience extreme suffering where "every moment feels like an infinity spent in the fires of hell."<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-108"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-108">[108]</a></sup></span>That even someone known for his compassionate approach to understanding human nature experiences such impulses suggests that retributivist intuitions are deeply embedded in human psychology.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-109"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-109">[109]</a></sup></span></p><p>As mentioned above, officially secular ideologies have produced their own torture systems. Future secular fanatics with access to advanced AI might create suffering systems justified by political rather than theological reasoning—punishing "traitors", "counterrevolutionaries," or whomever their ideology designates as irredeemably evil.</p><h2>Ideological fanaticism could undermine long-reflection-style frameworks and AI alignment</h2><p>Superintelligent AI could <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/KqCybin8rtfP3qztq/agi-and-lock-in">enable</a> individuals or <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/risks-of-stable-totalitarianism/">regimes</a> to permanently <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#is-global-lock-in-really-plausible-010837">lock-in their values</a>, potentially creating an unrecoverable dystopia (Ord, 2020, ch. 5.3). Misaligned AI could lead to human extinction or permanent disempowerment. Yet superintelligent AI could also enable truly <a href="https://nickbostrom.com/utopia">utopian outcomes</a>. The development of superintelligent AI may thus be the most pivotal event in the history of the universe (MacAskill, 2022, ch.4).</p><p>To avoid locking in undesirable values, a process akin to a <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/topics/long-reflection"><i>long reflection</i></a> may be helpful, where humanity carefully reflects on how to best achieve its long-term potential before rushing to take irreversible actions.</p><h3>Ideological fanaticism threatens collective moral deliberation</h3><p>The literal idea of a “long reflection” is probably unrealistic, but <a href="https://lukasfinnveden.substack.com/i/140338085/concrete-decision-making-proposals">more refined proposals</a>, like “viatopia”,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-110"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-110">[110]</a></sup></span>retain a similar emphasis on careful exploration and moral reflection. Whatever term we use, reaching truly utopian outcomes will likely require that major decisions—e.g., various “grand challenges” (MacAskill &amp; Moorhouse, <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/preparing-for-the-intelligence-explosion">2025</a>, section 4)—are at least partially guided by thoughtful deliberation (MacAskill &amp; Moorhouse, 2025a).</p><p>So, who should participate in the long reflection (or related post-AGI governance frameworks emphasizing collective moral deliberation), and under what rules? A natural Schelling point would be to give all living humans equal representation—an approach that is fair, democratic, and inclusive. (Note that participation and influence aren't necessarily binary: governance frameworks could include diverse voices while still constraining what outcomes are permissible. Moreover, decisions need not all be made at once; iterative approaches across smaller questions are perhaps more desirable.)</p><p>One line of argumentation for high inclusivity runs as follows. A wide range of viewpoints increases the chance of either discovering objective moral truth (if moral realism is true) or (if moral anti-realism is true) at least converging on robust moral principles that survive scrutiny from multiple perspectives, with the eventual outcome being at least broadly acceptable or even fairly attractive for many different value systems. Additionally, even if only a small fraction of agents participating in the long reflection converge on the best view, they may engage in <i>moral trade</i> with other value systems,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-111"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-111">[111]</a></sup></span>such that the ultimate outcome of the long reflection may not be substantially worse than the “optimal” outcome. Trade and compromise could be particularly important if the best futures constitute a narrow target that is hard to reach (MacAskill &amp; Moorhouse, 2025a).</p><p>But do these arguments extend to ideological fanatics? There are several reasons to think they don't. First, ideological fanatics, due to their absolute moral certainty, reflectively endorse locking in their values and beliefs, while eliminating dissent. Fanatics participating in the long reflection would seemingly make it <i>less</i> likely that we discover a hypothesized “correct moral view”, not more.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-112"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-112">[112]</a></sup></span></p><p>Second, gains from moral trade may be difficult to achieve when fanatics are at the bargaining table. When value systems are what MacAskill and Moorhouse (2025b, <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/convergence-and-compromise#31-trade-and-compromise">section 3</a>) call “resource-compatible”, the potential gains from trade could be enormous.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-113"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-113">[113]</a></sup></span>For instance, as they note, "hedonists might only care about bliss, and objective list theories might care primarily about wisdom; they might potentially agree to create a shared society where beings are both very blissful and very wise.” However, ideological fanaticism typically features highly resource-<i>incompatible</i> values. Nazi ideology, for instance, requires the elimination of all Jews and other ‘inferior’ races, so there are no hybrid arrangements that would satisfy both Nazis and Jews. Fanatics also often have <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_may_give_rise_to_extreme_optimization_and_insatiable_moral_desires">resource-hungry and insatiable</a> preferences (cf. Shulman <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/BasicAIDrives.pdf">2010</a>, p.4-6). What is more, fanatics may view any form of trade or compromise as morally reprehensible, equating it with betrayal of their sacred values. As a result, including fanatics in long reflection-like processes could actually prevent, rather than facilitate, moral trade among diverse value systems.</p><p>Ideological fanaticism could also harm other value systems more directly. First, fanatics tend to be highly intolerant and view an enormous range of behaviors and beliefs as immoral. For example, many religious fundamentalists oppose homosexuality, abortion, divorce, suicide, euthanasia, sex before marriage, and even music, singing, most clothes, most books, the Smurfs<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-114"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-114">[114]</a></sup></span>, etc.—see also the concept of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram#Forbidden_categories_of_actions">haram</a>, the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/613_commandments">613 commandments</a>, or the banning of large fractions of literature and art in <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_book_burnings">Nazi Germany</a>. In other words, fanatics may have extremely “<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/wE7KPnjZHBjxLKNno/ai-things-that-are-perhaps-as-important-as-human-controlled#Introduction1"><i>fussy</i></a>” preferences, which are <i>incompatible with the great majority of possible world states</i> and thus the fulfillment of most other value systems. From an upside-focused perspective, this could be extremely concerning. Fanatics might view extremely happy posthuman digital minds or hedonium as immoral abominations, and may thus oppose the creation of truly <a href="https://nickbostrom.com/utopia">utopian futures</a> filled with astronomical amounts of flourishing.</p><p>Second, fanatical ideologies may aim to create world states that are not only suboptimal but highly disvaluable according to most other value systems. One mechanism is <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Fanatical_retributivism_may_lead_to_astronomical_suffering">fanatical retributivism</a> discussed above; another is fanatics' plausibly greater propensity to use hostile bargaining tactics and engage in conflict.</p><h3>AI alignment may not solve the fanaticism problem either</h3><blockquote><p>"Are we going to ... create minds that help us seek the truth [or] create minds that have <i>whatever beliefs we want them to have</i>, <i>stick to those beliefs</i> and try to <i>shape the world around those beliefs</i>? [...] Some humans really ... will want to say, … 'This is the religion I follow. This is what I believe in. This is what I care about. And I am creating an AI to help me promote that religion, not to help me question it or revise it or make it better.'" —Holden Karnofsky, emphasis added (<a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/holden-karnofsky-how-ai-could-take-over-the-world/#misaligned-ai-might-not-kill-us-all-and-aligned-ai-could-be-catastrophic-002718">2023</a>)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-115"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-115">[115]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p>A crucial question in AI alignment is “aligned with whom or what” (e.g., Barnett, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/Hhtvwx2ka4pzoWg7e/ai-alignment-shouldn-t-be-conflated-with-ai-moral">2023</a>; Carlsmith, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cnv5g7jCWLw9LYKxa/an-even-deeper-atheism-3#Human_paperclippers_">2024</a>; Chen, <a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4684068">2023</a>)? Gabriel (<a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11023-020-09539-2">2020</a>) distinguishes six possible alignment targets, from literal instructions to moral values. For our purposes, these can be grouped into three categories:</p><ol><li><strong>Narrow intent-alignment</strong>: The AI does what the user currently wants—following their unreflected, surface-level preferences.</li><li><strong>Preference idealization</strong>: The AI does what the user would want if they knew more, reflected more, or were more like the person they wished they were.</li><li><strong>“Principles-based alignment”</strong>: The AI is aligned with certain values, principles, or moral frameworks, and not indexed to any particular user's preferences. For instance, Anthropic's Constitutional AI trains models to follow a written set of principles; more generally, AIs could be aligned with classical utilitarianism, a holy book, or broad values like “honesty”.</li></ol><p>(1) is obviously dangerous when the principals are fanatical (or malevolent). The more interesting question is whether (2) or (3) might help.</p><p><strong>Preference idealization won't necessarily deradicalize fanatics</strong><br />Yudkowsky's coherent extrapolated volition (<a href="https://intelligence.org/files/CEV.pdf">CEV</a>) is largely outdated, but it can illustrate the broader idea of preference idealization: that AI should serve not users' current, unreflected preferences but their <i>idealized</i> (extrapolated) preferences—what they would want if they knew more, reflected more, and were "more the people they wished they were."<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-116"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-116">[116]</a></sup></span>Similar ideas appear in various forms throughout the literature.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-117"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-117">[117]</a></sup></span>Would such preference idealization lead to good outcomes when the principals are fanatical?</p><p>Unfortunately, this seems unlikely.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-118"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-118">[118]</a></sup></span>Fanatics' deepest preference often appears to be vindication of existing beliefs rather than impartial, evidence-based truth-discovery.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-119"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-119">[119]</a></sup></span>When their beliefs conflict with reality, fanatics often attempt to <i>reshape reality</i> to correspond to their pre-existing beliefs, rather than update their beliefs to correspond to reality.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-120"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-120">[120]</a></sup></span>The Jewish fanatic <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yigal_Amir">Yigal Amir</a>, for instance, assassinated the compromise-oriented incumbent Prime Minister of Israel in 1995, in part because he apparently wanted to make Torah predictions come true.</p><p>Fundamentally, many fanatics may actively reject the core premise that 'knowing more' should change beliefs. A religious fanatic who believes in absolute divine revelation sees no room for improvement upon God's word—any change would be heresy. For many fanatics, being ‘more the people they wished they were’ may not mean being more reflective, rational, humble, or compassionate; they may wish to be more devout, more unwavering, and more zealous.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-121"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-121">[121]</a></sup></span>So even the idealized preferences of ideological fanatics might result in terrible outcomes.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-122"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-122">[122]</a></sup></span></p><p>For illustration, imagine that in 2040, the leader of a theocratic state obtained access to superintelligent AI. He has overseen torture and execution of thousands, sponsored terrorist organizations worldwide, and allowed female political prisoners to be raped before execution to <a href="https://wncri.org/2015/11/13/female-prisoners-virgins-raped/">ensure</a> their <a href="https://iranwire.com/en/news/117116-ex-official-virgin-prisoners-were-raped-to-prevent-them-going-to-paradise/">damnation</a>.</p><p>How should an AI aligned with his preferences act? Judging from his lifetime of choices, he seems to value enforcing his own religious interpretation above all else. He certainly doesn’t seem to place great value on truth-seeking or changing his mind when encountering new evidence. What is more, updating his beliefs to accurately reflect reality would be enormously painful, obliterating every shred of meaning, purpose, and accomplishment he once felt. The AI would need to convince him that his once cherished beliefs are grotesquely false, that his life's work amounted to a series of pointless atrocities, that his heroes were almost entirely wrong while his enemies were largely correct. Even psychologically healthy, non-fanatical people resist changing their minds about comparatively trivial matters because admitting mistakes is painful. For someone whose entire existence centers around their ideology, wouldn't an aligned AI (that is, one that truly has this guy’s best interests at heart) try to spare him all this misery and instead try to reshape reality to match his beliefs? Are we confident that what he <i>really</i> wants, deep down, is to hear the truth—especially in light of how he has lived his life so far?</p><p><strong>Principles-based alignment won't necessarily help either if fanatics are involved</strong><br />What about aligning AI with some external moral framework or set of principles, independent of any user's preferences?</p><p>This approach seems safer than relying on preference idealization alone and could indeed help, provided that reasonable people control the process and choose sensible principles. But it doesn't <i>automatically</i> solve the fanaticism problem. If fanatics have significant influence over which principles the AI is aligned with—if the alignment target becomes some holy book or "Mein Kampf"—we're back to disaster. In practice, decisions about alignment targets will be made by humans, including, potentially, fanatical humans.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-123"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-123">[123]</a></sup></span></p><h3>Prevalence of reality-denying, anti-pluralistic, and punitive worldviews</h3><p>The practical importance of these concerns—for both collective deliberation proposals and AI alignment approaches—depends in part on how prevalent such worldviews actually are. As <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Estimating_the_global_scale_of_ideological_fanaticism">discussed earlier</a>, a non-trivial fraction of humanity could reasonably be classified as ideological fanatics.</p><p>However, even many non-fanatical humans living in open societies don't seem to prioritize truth-seeking. Some explicitly acknowledge this: around 20% of people in Western societies do not think their beliefs should be based on evidence (Pennycook et al., <a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/judgment-and-decision-making/article/on-the-belief-that-beliefs-should-change-according-to-evidence-implications-for-conspiratorial-moral-paranormal-political-religious-and-science-beliefs/254C6EF93A5E037998EB7E1003627CB6">2020</a>)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-124"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-124">[124]</a></sup></span>. Similarly, 30% of US Americans (<a href="https://www.worldvaluessurvey.org/WVSOnline.jsp">WVS</a>) report that when science conflicts with their own religious beliefs they would stick with their religious beliefs.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-125"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-125">[125]</a></sup></span>More generally, most humans prioritize <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/people-embrace-beliefs-that-signal-354">socially adaptive beliefs</a>—i.e., those that make them look or feel good—over true beliefs (Williams, <a href="https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Daniel-Williams-22/publication/337275990_Socially_Adaptive_Belief/links/5dce7ba7299bf1b74b42cde7/Socially-Adaptive-Belief.pdf">2021</a>).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-126"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-126">[126]</a></sup></span>This makes including fanatics in the long reflection or related proposals even riskier, because we can't be confident that a clear majority of impartial truth-seekers will outweigh fanatical and non-truth-seeking voices.</p><p>Many of us may overestimate humanity's commitment to truth-seeking because of biases like wishful thinking, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/typical-mind-fallacy">typical mind fallacy</a> and unrepresentative sampling: Most longtermists and AI governance researchers grew up or work in WEIRD (cf. Henrich, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_WEIRDest_People_in_the_World">2021</a>)—Western, educated, industrialized, rich, and democratic—societies where support for evidence-based belief revision and science remains comparatively high while support for coercive ideology enforcement (such as death penalty for apostasy, theocratic governance, or extreme punishment of dissent) is rather low.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-127"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-127">[127]</a></sup></span>But elsewhere, support for such worldviews is much higher, sometimes even reaching majority levels: for instance, over 50% in Iran and over 90% in Pakistan (World Values Survey, <a href="https://www.worldvaluessurvey.org/WVSOnline.jsp">2017-2022</a>) believe that “whenever science and religion conflict, religion is always right” and that “the only acceptable religion is my religion”.</p><h2>Ideological fanaticism could worsen many other risks</h2><h3>Differential intellectual regress</h3><p>Ideological fanaticism may exacerbate most other risks by driving <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/differential-intellectual-progress-as-a-positive-sum-project"><i>differential intellectual</i> <i>regress</i></a>. Regimes governed by fanatical ideologies are often able to maintain or even advance technological capabilities, while systematically degrading <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EByDsY9S3EDhhfFzC/some-thoughts-on-metaphilosophy">philosophical sophistication</a>, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/XxBwnSt4BcBhpJJyX/essay-competition-on-the-automation-of-wisdom-and-philosophy">wisdom</a>, moral <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/TmPSYEdQ6Mrb9oMLA/ai-strategy-given-the-need-for-good-reflection">reflection</a>, reason, <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/improving-institutional-decision-making/">societal decision-making</a> and cooperation-conducive institutions. (This degradation happens through multiple reinforcing mechanisms, e.g., imposing <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China">censorship</a> and propaganda, replacing experts with <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trofim_Lysenko">ideological loyalists</a>, and rewarding conformist dogmatism over open discourse and evidence-based reasoning.)</p><p>This pattern is particularly concerning from a longtermist perspective. Humanity badly needs wisdom, rationality, and strong institutions to navigate <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/preparing-for-the-intelligence-explosion#4-grand-challenges">grand challenges</a> like the governance of AGI. Improving <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/improving-institutional-decision-making/">institutional decision-making</a> and <a href="https://www.openphilanthropy.org/focus/forecasting/">forecasting</a> are already considered important cause areas for this reason.</p><h3>Ideological fanaticism may give rise to extreme optimization and insatiable moral desires</h3><blockquote><p>Fanaticism never sleeps: it is never glutted: [...] it is never stopped by conscience; for it has pressed conscience into service. Avarice, lust, and vengeance, have piety, benevolence, honour; fanaticism has nothing to oppose it. —<a href="https://www.goodreads.com/quotes/1271188-if-a-man-happen-to-take-it-into-his-head">Jeremy Bentham</a></p></blockquote><p>Most non-fanatical humans tend to be <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satisficing">satisficers</a>. They compromise, are opportunistic, do what is convenient, and are happy to trade with others. In contrast, fanatics are more likely to <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/T975ydo3mx8onH3iS/ea-is-about-maximization-and-maximization-is-perilous"><i>maximize</i></a> by any means necessary, ultimately aiming to rearrange every atom in the universe to align with their ideology’s demands. Such <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EaLCAZ4bgycY6hFC8#2_3_2_Will_agents_optimize_extremely_intensely__and_does_this_matter_">extreme optimization</a> for an idiosyncratic value system is incompatible with the fulfillment of most other value systems.</p><p>Unfortunately, <i>moral preferences in general</i> seem more scope-sensitive and resource-hungry (Shulman, <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/BasicAIDrives.pdf">2010</a>, pp. 4-6) than selfish desires.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-128"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-128">[128]</a></sup></span>From a selfish perspective, most people would presumably be quite happy with only a galaxy or two; some especially frugal ones might even make do with a single solar system.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-129"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-129">[129]</a></sup></span>Uncolonized distant galaxies are meaningless to most egoists, but an “<a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/astronomical-waste/">astronomical waste</a>” to classical utilitarians. (To be clear, this scope-sensitivity isn't itself problematic; it's arguably a feature of genuine moral concern.)</p><p>Given their propensity to torture, murder, launch wars, and so on, fanatics are often thought of as being immoral. But this does <i>not</i> mean that fanatics lack moral conviction. In many ways, the dangers of ideological fanaticism arguably stem from <i>excessively strong</i> moral convictions. <a href="https://leeds-faculty.colorado.edu/mcgrawp/PDF/SkitkaBaumanSargis2005.pdf">Skitka et al. (2005)</a>, for instance, found that stronger moral conviction leads to less tolerance and cooperation. What fanatics lack is humility, moderation, concern for others’ well-being (even if they disagree), and moral constraints setting limits on what constitutes acceptable behavior in pursuit of one’s goals.</p><p>For fanatics, perhaps no victory is total enough, no punishment severe enough, no empire extensive enough. Ultimately, ideological fanaticism may end up removing all constraints on maximizing behavior—making their morality uniquely “<a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/y5jAuKqkShdjMNZab/morality-is-scary">scary</a>”.</p><h3>Apocalyptic terrorism</h3><p>Some fanatical groups have extremely conflict-conducive preferences. Some even believe that they need to <i>actively</i> <i>bring about the apocalypse</i>—involving enormous suffering and destruction—in order to usher in their conception of utopia. ISIS, for example, believes that they must defeat the armies of “Rome” (often interpreted as the US) at Dabiq, which will “initiate the countdown to the apocalypse” (Wood, <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2015/03/what-isis-really-wants/384980/">2015</a>). Other groups may want to kill literally everyone. Motivated by Christian, Hindu, Buddhist, and conspiratorial elements, the Aum Shinrikyo <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/kevin-esvelt-stealth-wildfire-pandemics/#omnicidal-actors-in-history-000710">doomsday cult</a> tried to create a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_subway_sarin_attack">global pandemic</a> in order to “redeem” humanity.</p><h3>S-risk-conducive propensities and reverse cooperative intelligence</h3><p>Taylor (<a href="https://longtermrisk.org/measurement-research-agenda/#21_Characterizing_s-risk-conducive_properties">2025</a>) uses the term <i>s-risk-conducive properties</i> to describe properties that, if possessed by powerful agents like transformative AIs, could contribute to s-risks, largely by initiating or escalating conflict. Taylor outlines the following broad categories:</p><ul><li>Tendencies to escalate conflict, make large threats when in conflict with other agents, and enact large punishments against wrongdoers (punitiveness).</li><li>Spite, vengefulness, and risk tolerance or risk-seeking behavior.</li><li>Absence of beneficial capabilities that allow actors to avoid or de-escalate conflict (absence of cooperative intelligence).</li></ul><p>It's notable that many ideological fanatics tend to exhibit essentially all of these characteristics. We could describe this general cluster of conflict-conducive traits as a form of ‘<i>reverse cooperative intelligence</i>’—essentially the opposite of what the <a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/d41586-021-01170-0">cooperative AI</a> research agenda seeks to develop. While cooperative intelligence involves engaging in dialogue, building trust, de-escalating conflict, and finding mutually beneficial compromise solutions, fanatics instead tend to demonize others over the slightest disagreement, treat compromise as intolerable betrayal, endorse brutal violence, and generally escalate conflict.</p><h3>More speculative dynamics: purity spirals and self-inflicted suffering</h3><p>Fanatical retributivism isn't the only source of astronomical suffering in “fanatical utopias”. The following admittedly far-fetched dynamics could create perpetual suffering even after ideological fanatics have achieved total control and eliminated all designated enemies.</p><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purity_spiral"><i>Purity spirals</i></a> (or <i>moral outbidding</i>), where members compete to demonstrate ideological commitment through ever more extreme measures, could amplify several risks discussed above. This dynamic—seen e.g. in the French Revolution's Reign of Terror or Mao’s Cultural Revolution—could intensify fanatical retributivism itself, with members competing to advocate ever crueler punishments for ideological opponents. Anyone suggesting mere execution rather than eternal torture might be branded as weak or traitorous. Purity spirals could also continuously expand the definition of "enemy" or “evil”, ensuring that there is always someone left to punish. Similar dynamics, fueled by resentment and hatred, may also lead fanatics to actively seek to instantiate the opposite of their enemies’ values.</p><p>Some ideological fanatics may also embrace <i>asceticism</i> or <i>self-inflicted suffering</i> for ideological reasons, viewing suffering as purifying or virtuous. Unlike retributivism, which targets enemies, this could mean imposing suffering on even the "pure" in-group—potentially forever. (These scenarios are explored further in <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/13zSCUf2j7KReWjcPAOBpR9XoqUJ9BP9UIqbdKaznCII/edit?tab=t.0#bookmark=id.289m8avvwlqj">Appendix D</a>.)</p><h3>Unknown unknowns and navigating exotic scenarios</h3><p>While any specific catastrophic scenario tends to be unlikely, the fundamental characteristics of ideological fanaticism (especially its dogmatism, bad epistemics, and blind hatred) make it more likely to cause harm across a wide range of potential scenarios, including ones we haven’t yet identified or foreseen. Actors who embody such traits seem also less likely to properly navigate exotic scenarios—<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/acausal-trade">acausal trade</a>, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/evidential-cooperation-in-large-worlds">evidential cooperation in large worlds</a>, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/updateless-decision-theory">updateless decision theory</a>, and so on. Fanatics seem therefore particularly worrisome from a perspective of unknown unknowns and deep uncertainty.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-130"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-130">[130]</a></sup></span></p><h1>Interventions</h1><p>We organize potential interventions into two broad categories. First, we discuss “conventional” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Societal_or_political_interventions">political and societal interventions</a> which appear useful across a wide spectrum of worldviews. Second, we discuss more directly <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Artificial_intelligence_related_interventions">artificial intelligence-related interventions</a> that tend to be more neglected and plausibly higher leverage. (This area is where we expect to focus the majority of our own work going forward.)</p><p>However, the boundary we draw between political/societal versus AI-related interventions is somewhat artificial and potentially misleading. Exclusive focus on a narrow conception of AI safety would risk neglecting political &amp; societal interventions that likely improve AI outcomes. If we want society to make reasonable decisions about the future of transformative AI, it would help to have reasonable people in positions of political power, including in various parts of the US government. Likewise, many AI-focused interventions rely on political will and governmental competence.</p><p>Most interventions discussed below are not novel and overlap with existing longtermist priorities. But the fanaticism lens could shift priorities and, especially from an s-risk perspective, make certain directions that previously seemed neutral or counterproductive appear more promising. We're especially excited about preventing AI-enabled coups, compute governance, making AIs themselves non-fanatical, and developing fanaticism-resistant AGI governance proposals. That said, most of our recommendations are tentative and some may prove misguided upon further investigation. Moreover, we’re likely not aware of the most promising anti-fanaticism interventions; hopefully some can be identified by further research.</p><h2>Societal or political interventions</h2><p>No intervention in this section scores exceptionally highly in terms of importance, tractability, and neglectedness. However, given that enormous sums are spent in this area, making these efforts even <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/o5LBbv9bfNjKxFeHm/marginally-more-effective-altruism">marginally more cost-effective could still be valuable</a>.</p><h3>Safeguarding democracy</h3><p>The US is the world’s most powerful country and leads in AI development. Consequently, safeguarding US democracy<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-131"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-131">[131]</a></sup></span>seems crucial to reduce many long-term risks, including those from malevolent and fanatical actors. Of course, other powerful democracies may also influence humanity's long-term future. Preventing democratic backsliding in countries like India and across Europe is therefore also important.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-132"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-132">[132]</a></sup></span></p><p>How can we prevent further democratic backsliding? Below, we focus on reducing polarization and strengthening anti-fanatical principles. ​​We emphasize these not because they are necessarily the most important interventions overall, but because they are most directly related to the long-term risks of ideological fanaticism.</p><h3>Reducing political polarization</h3><p>Excessive political polarization corrodes democratic norms and institutions, creates legislative gridlock, and increases intergroup hostility (Levitsky &amp; Ziblatt, 2018; Binder, 2004; Mason, 2018).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-133"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-133">[133]</a></sup></span>Most worryingly for our purposes, polarization seems to create the psychological and social conditions that exacerbate the core characteristics of ideological fanaticism: epistemic dogmatism (pushing people to choose between beliefs approved by their respective tribe, rather than following evidence), in-group loyalty (defending people on one’s side no matter what) and tribal hatred (all political opponents are viewed as existential enemies<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-134"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-134">[134]</a></sup></span>), and calls for extremist acts, including political violence.</p><p>This dynamic seems to play out through various vicious feedback loops: Extremists on both sides adopt increasingly irrational positions—sometimes <a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/mila.12392">embracing absurd beliefs</a> as costly signals of tribal loyalty. Each side's extremism in turn validates the other's worst fears, making people even more tribal and irrational. Meanwhile, moderate or nuanced positions become increasingly untenable, as everyone must choose a side or be attacked by both (even if they criticize <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/tribalism-corrupts-politics-even">one side</a> much more than the other).</p><p>Ultimately, such polarization spirals may give rise to two opposing fanatical ideologies.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-135"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-135">[135]</a></sup></span>(In the academic literature, this is studied as <i>competitive extremism</i> or <i>mutual radicalization</i>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-136"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-136">[136]</a></sup></span>) Historical examples include Weimar Germany, where Communists and Nazis together commanded just 13% of votes in 1928 but surged to 56% by 1933, their street violence feeding off each other, or 1930s Spain, where far-left anarchists and far-right fascists escalated toward civil war. In each case, extremists had a paradoxically symbiotic relationship where each side's excesses were used to justify the other's apocalyptic narratives and increasingly extremist actions.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-137"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-137">[137]</a></sup></span>While contemporary Western politics is not near the severity of these historical examples, milder versions of these dynamics seem to be present, especially in certain countries.</p><p>Beyond fueling ideological fanaticism, polarization diminishes society's epistemics and ability to address complex problems. This becomes particularly dangerous as we approach AGI and its associated <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/preparing-for-the-intelligence-explosion#4-grand-challenges">grand challenges</a> which, even more so than ordinary political issues, demand wise, evidence-based deliberation. Polarization also erodes social trust and increases the risk of conflict, itself a <a href="https://centerforreducingsuffering.org/research/risk-factors-for-s-risks/#Conflict_and_hostility">major risk factor for s-risks</a>. Breaking the polarization spiral is thus not just valuable for near-term democratic stability, but also for ensuring humanity can navigate its future wisely.</p><p>What can be done? The best path forward likely requires cultural changes and institutional and structural reforms.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-138"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-138">[138]</a></sup></span>The political scientist Lee Drutman (<a href="https://www.newamerica.org/political-reform/reports/more-parties-better-parties/">2023a</a>) argues that a major cause of US polarization is its rigid two-party system, resulting from its <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting">first-past-the-post</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-member_district">single-member district</a> electoral system. Drutman sees <a href="https://protectdemocracy.org/work/fusion-voting-explained/">fusion voting</a> and <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bqWwV3xk9Qk&amp;ab_channel=Vox">proportional representation</a> as the two most promising ways of reducing this "<a href="https://www.amazon.com/Breaking-Two-Party-Doom-Loop-Multiparty/dp/0190913851">two-party doom loop</a>" of hyper-partisan polarization.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-139"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-139">[139]</a></sup></span>Proportional representation in particular disincentivizes outgroup demonization and refusal to compromise, so typical of ideological fanatics—calling all other parties irredeemably evil makes finding coalition partners difficult and thus limits paths to power.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-140"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-140">[140]</a></sup></span>Others have argued for <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/aaron-hamlin-voting-reform/">approval voting</a>, <a href="https://www.newamerica.org/political-reform/blog/how-i-updated-my-views-on-ranked-choice-voting/">ranked choice voting</a>, <a href="https://protectdemocracy.org/work/how-did-we-get-here-primaries-polarization-and-party-control/">open primaries</a>, and <a href="https://www.vox.com/2015/3/2/8120063/american-democracy-doomed">parliamentarism</a> (instead of presidentialism).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-141"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-141">[141]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-142"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-142">[142]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-143"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-143">[143]</a></sup></span></p><h3>Promoting anti-fanatical values: classical liberalism and Enlightenment principles</h3><p>Arguably the most foundational intervention against ideological fanaticism is to promote values, norms, and principles that actively counteract it. We see <a href="https://www.notesfromthecircus.com/p/what-is-liberalism"><i>classical liberalism</i></a> and <i>Enlightenment principles</i> (e.g., Pinker, <a href="https://www.goodreads.com/book/show/35696171-enlightenment-now">2018</a>)—terms we use interchangeably here—as time-tested bulwarks that stand almost directly opposed to ideological fanaticism. They provide an institutional framework for managing disagreement, as well as the substantive commitments that directly counter ideological fanaticism:</p><ul><li>Instead of dogmatic certainty in any single authority's possession of all truth and virtue, they promote reason, evidence, the scientific method, <a href="https://harpers.org/a-letter-on-justice-and-open-debate/">open debate</a>, and skepticism of traditional authority.</li><li>Instead of tribalistic loyalty and hatred, they advocate for universal humanism (and sometimes even moral consideration for other sentient beings), individual liberty, equality before the law, and tolerance.</li><li>Instead of totalitarian “any means necessary” concentration of power in one supreme authority, they stand for procedural justice, separation of powers, and the rule of law.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-144"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-144">[144]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p>These aren't arbitrary preferences, but rather mutually reinforcing principles that create both the <i>values</i> and the <i>institutions</i> necessary to prevent ideological fanaticism from running amok. Recognizing that no one has privileged access to absolute truth, classical liberalism doesn't require consensus on ultimate truths, only agreement on procedural rules that allow peaceful coexistence (cf. Rawls’ <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/rawls/#ReaPluPubPolCul">reasonable pluralism</a>). This epistemic humility creates open societies that can admit their own limitations and <a href="https://www.persuasion.community/p/gradual-change-is-fing-awesomeand">gradually evolve</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-145"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-145">[145]</a></sup></span>through elections and open debate rather than violence and revolutions. However, this requires both philosophical commitments (reason, rights, tolerance) and institutional architecture (democracy, constitutions, independent courts, free speech) working together.</p><p>Unfortunately, classical liberalism and Enlightenment principles are facing intensifying attacks from illiberal movements, such as right-wing and left-wing extremism as well as religious fundamentalism. How can we defend these principles? The most general approach is creating content for wide audiences that exemplifies Enlightenment principles and helps society think more sensibly. Many mainstream intellectuals, journalists, publications, and organizations already do relevant work here.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-146"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-146">[146]</a></sup></span>Other avenues for bolstering classical liberalism over fanatical ideologies include legal advocacy for equal protection, free speech, and other fundamental rights. Governments are already involved in providing <a href="https://youtu.be/oKY600o3CXw?si=5M9LUd8JAJGPhiWf">education on classical liberal values</a> and <a href="https://www.counterterrorism.police.uk/what-we-do/prevent/">preventing radicalization</a>, and have historically supported efforts like <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty">Radio Free Europe</a> and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America">Voice of America</a>, which helped weaken totalitarian ideologies. Preserving and defending such existing infrastructure may be as important as creating new or more cost-effective initiatives.</p><p>From a longtermist perspective, it might seem myopic to get caught up in the fray of today's political and cultural battles. Looking back millennia from now, won't the pendulum-swings of political sentiment mostly average out to insignificance? But if transformative AI arrives within the next decade, the political and epistemic conditions of our time may non-trivially influence humanity’s long-term trajectory.</p><h3>Growing the influence of liberal democracies</h3><p>We might try to reduce the expected influence of fanatical regimes by strengthening the defenses and influence of more liberal democratic regimes. Of course, democratic governments already pour enormous resources into improving their industrial might, technology, and military power, but they could likely do it better in various ways.</p><p>One idea, largely to illustrate the general point, is for democratic countries to admit a higher number of (high-skilled) immigrants, encouraging what economists call “<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight#:~:text=The%20net%20benefits%20of%20human,as%20a%20%22brain%20drain%22.">brain gain</a>”.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-147"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-147">[147]</a></sup></span>Already, the most educated citizens in authoritarian countries <a href="https://fiia.fi/en/publication/brain-drain-from-russia">tend</a> to be the ones most eager to leave—if these people had an easier time moving to democratic countries, they would not only make democratic countries grow stronger, but make authoritarian countries weaker.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-148"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-148">[148]</a></sup></span>A few <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Billion_Americans">targeted policy changes</a> on the part of the United States or <a href="https://www.dougsaunders.net/maximum-canada/">other liberal democracies</a> could greatly accelerate that ongoing process.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-149"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-149">[149]</a></sup></span></p><p>Another promising approach would be promoting economic growth and innovation in Europe, especially regarding AI. In many ways, Europe is a stronghold of classical-liberal principles, yet it is <a href="https://www.atlanticcouncil.org/blogs/new-atlanticist/the-draghi-report-grabbed-europes-attention-now-its-time-for-the-eu-to-put-it-into-action/">underperforming its economic potential</a>. Boosting growth in liberal democracies would lift their relative power (and may also reduce vulnerability to fanaticism<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-150"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-150">[150]</a></sup></span>). In particular, advanced AI will likely <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/carl-shulman-economy-agi/">bestow</a> vast economic and <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/the-free-world-must-prevail/#Whoever_leads_on_superintelligence_will_have_a_decisive_military_advantage">military</a> benefits. Setting up liberal democracies to successfully develop and harness AI capabilities is therefore very important (while mitigating various risks).</p><p>Of course, you only want to pursue these kinds of interventions if you're confident that you're shifting the balance of power in a robustly positive direction. Given that the US is already the world's strongest country, the marginal value of further strengthening may be lower than <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Safeguarding_democracy">safeguarding its democratic institutions</a>. Similarly, strengthening other liberal democracies, particularly in Europe, could meaningfully improve the overall position of the free world.</p><h3>Encouraging reform in illiberal countries</h3><p>In the 1980s, many European states were under the control of Soviet-aligned communist regimes. By the 1990s, most had transitioned to democracy, a shift accelerated by deliberate efforts to reform these regimes.</p><p>Similar efforts today may reduce fanatical regimes' influence. Opportunities include supporting opposition movements and regime-critical media, conditioning development aid or EU/WTO membership on democratic standards, poaching top talent through emigration, or implementing economic sanctions.</p><p>However, this area is both prone to backfire and non-neglected: The US has historically engaged in numerous efforts to reform and weaken illiberal countries—often with negative consequences. Generally, we should be cautious with adversarial interventions and focus on cooperative solutions where possible.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-151"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-151">[151]</a></sup></span></p><h3>Promoting international cooperation</h3><p>Promoting international cooperation seems beneficial partly because it can reduce the risk of <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/great-power-conflict/">great power conflicts</a>, such as between the US and China, which <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Ideological_fanaticism_increases_the_risk_of_war_and_conflict">increase s-risks and x-risks in various ways</a>. Great power conflicts may also create pathways for fanatics to gain power:<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-152"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-152">[152]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li><strong>War reinforces dangerous "enemy of my enemy" dynamics that can empower fanatics</strong>. When facing an existential threat, nations are more likely to ally with anyone, including fanatics, against their primary adversary. For example, to bleed the Soviet Union in Afghanistan, the United States backed the Islamist <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen">Mujahideen</a>, only to see those fighters later form the Taliban and Al-Qaeda. A similar logic is at play in the emerging loose alliance of CRINK. China, Russia, Iran, and North Korea share little ideological common ground beyond a mutual opposition to the United States. Consequently, reducing tensions between the US and China may also reduce the strength of these alliances.</li><li><strong>Technology sharing increases.</strong> In desperate times such as wartime, a nation may be more likely to share its most advanced technologies with allies, including potentially fanatical ones. As the tide of World War II turned against them, the Nazis shared <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-2_rocket">rocket</a> and <a href="https://nationalinterest.org/blog/buzz/could-japanese-jet-fighter-have-won-the-pacific-war-24030">jet fighter</a> <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanagi_missions#Reciprocal_voyages">designs</a>, and even attempted to share <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_submarine_U-234">uranium with</a> Imperial Japan. Similarly, in a potential future US-China conflict over AI supremacy, the loser, perhaps partly out of desperation or spite, could share its AI capabilities with its allies.</li><li><strong>Strained information security</strong>. Wartime increases both the number of people requiring access to sensitive technologies and adversaries' incentives to attempt infiltration. Security measures may intensify, but often not proportionally. The Manhattan Project, despite strong precautions, was compromised by <a href="https://ahf.nuclearmuseum.org/ahf/profile/klaus-fuchs/">Klaus Fuchs</a>, who passed comprehensive nuclear designs to the Soviets. Wartime urgency may also pressure organizations to accept risks they'd otherwise reject. In an AI context, rapid scaling of compute infrastructure, emergency partnerships, and rushed hiring could create vulnerabilities.</li><li><strong>Exacerbating political extremism.</strong> War creates fertile ground for the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#What_do_we_mean_by_ideological_fanaticism_">fanatical mindset</a>. Wartime propaganda would likely increase tribalistic nationalism (any criticism of one's country is branded as treachery), degrade epistemics, and normalize violence. Defeat or national humiliation may generate (potentially justified) resentment which can empower extremist movements (cf. China’s “<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_of_humiliation">century of humiliation</a>” narrative, or the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stab-in-the-back_myth">"stab-in-the-back" myth</a> after Germany's WW1 defeat fueling Hitler’s rise to power).</li><li><strong>Democratic backsliding and rising authoritarianism.</strong> Conflict is often used as a pretext for would-be authoritarians to consolidate power. For instance, if a war with China breaks out, some may favor invoking <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_the_United_States">martial law</a> and suspending democratic processes.</li><li><strong>State collapse and revolutions</strong>*.* Great power conflict seems to be a major driver of revolutions (cf. Skocpol, 1979)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-153"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-153">[153]</a></sup></span>. A <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1fQbXXX1lScqgiBBQzg7rCA6h4zuFClLMiwzHBuFRUj0/edit?tab=t.0">crude analysis</a> suggests that more than 70% of major revolutions (between 1900 and 2010) occurred during or as direct results of great power conflicts. Currently, there exist only a few truly fanatical regimes, so new revolutions may make things worse in expectation, potentially resulting in new fanatical regimes in previously stable regions. The costs and chaos of war may also increase the risk of (partial) state collapse, allowing fanatical groups to seize critical resources. While in the past this meant conventional weapons (as when <a href="https://www.amnesty.org.uk/how-isis-islamic-state-isil-got-its-weapons-iraq-syria">ISIS captured U.S.-supplied military equipment</a> in Iraq), in the future it could mean AI-critical infrastructure.</li></ul><p><strong>Risks from increased cooperation</strong><br />Cooperation of some kinds could <i>increase</i> the risk of ideological fanatics gaining power. For instance, some types of cooperation on AI could reduce the chance of the US gaining a decisive advantage. An obvious example would be the US removing export controls on compute to China—very “cooperative” in a sense. Historical examples like Chamberlain’s failed appeasement strategy with Hitler and the “<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wandel_durch_Handel">Wandel durch Handel</a>” (change through trade) policy with Russia demonstrate that naive cooperation can have undesirable outcomes.</p><p><strong>Interventions</strong><br />It’s <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/great-power-conflict/#tractability">difficult to say</a> what sorts of interventions might be effective for increasing international cooperation <i>in general</i>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-154"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-154">[154]</a></sup></span>It may be more tractable to work toward international agreements targeted at defusing specific geopolitical flashpoints or governing potentially destabilizing technologies like AI.</p><p>The <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Non-Proliferation_of_Nuclear_Weapons">Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty</a> might serve as a general model for agreements governing emerging technologies. With this treaty, ideological enemies worked together to prevent nuclear chaos because the alternative was a threat to all. As the catastrophic potential of AI exceeds even that of nuclear weapons, such pragmatic cooperation arguably becomes even more essential.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-155"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-155">[155]</a></sup></span></p><p>Promising existing work includes creating frameworks for US-China AI safety coordination, as promoted by organizations like the <a href="https://saif.org/">Safe AI Forum</a> (including its project <a href="https://idais.ai/">International Dialogues on AI Safety</a>), and the <a href="https://www.simoninstitute.ch/">Simon Institute for Longterm Governance</a>.
