The <a href="https://www.longtermresilience.org/">Centre for Long-Term Resilience</a> is developing proposals for international AI governance, and think tanks like the <a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/">Carnegie Endowment for International Peace</a> are also doing relevant work.</p><h2>Artificial intelligence-related interventions</h2><h3>Reducing the chance that transformative AI falls into the hands of fanatics</h3><p>Transformative AI may grant huge amounts of power and control—potentially enough to permanently “<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ideological-fanaticism-could-undermine-long-reflection-style-frameworks-and-ai-alignment">lock in</a>” the trajectory of (some fraction of) civilization’s long-term future. It is therefore crucial to ensure that fanatics do not get their hands on it. It may also arrive very soon—<a href="https://ai-2027.com/">perhaps</a> <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/">by</a> <a href="https://80000hours.org/agi/guide/when-will-agi-arrive/">2030</a>—so time is of the essence.</p><p><strong>Compute governance</strong><br />From the 1940s onwards, both <a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2010-title42/html/USCODE-2010-title42-chap23-divsnA.htm">national</a> and <a href="https://world-nuclear.org/information-library/safety-and-security/non-proliferation/safeguards-to-prevent-nuclear-proliferation">international</a> regulations restricted exports of uranium and introduced monitoring regimes to prevent rogue states from obtaining nuclear weapons. These controls slowed proliferation; only nine states currently <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_nuclear_weapons">possess nuclear weapons</a>.</p><p>Just as uranium is a key ingredient in nuclear weapons, computing power (‘compute’ for short) is one of the <a href="https://www.cs.utexas.edu/~eunsol/courses/data/bitter_lesson.pdf">most important</a> <a href="https://cset.georgetown.edu/wp-content/uploads/CSET-AI-Triad-Report.pdf">ingredients in AI progress</a>. It’s perhaps also <a href="https://www.governance.ai/post/computing-power-and-the-governance-of-ai">the easiest to monitor and regulate</a>. The US has already <a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/choking-chinas-access-future-ai">restricted China’s access to compute</a> through export controls, especially the 2022 CHIPS Act, which limits advanced chip exports and restricts US firms from supporting China’s semiconductor sector.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-156"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-156">[156]</a></sup></span>But compute governance contains a wide range of measures beyond export controls, from <a href="https://www.iaps.ai/research/preventing-ai-chip-smuggling-to-china">chip smuggling prevention to</a> <a href="https://www.iaps.ai/research/location-verification-for-ai-chips">location verification features</a>.</p><p>For our purposes, the aim of compute governance would be to minimize the access that ideologically fanatical regimes (and malevolent actors) have to advanced AI.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-157"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-157">[157]</a></sup></span>This aligns with existing U.S. export controls which, while primarily targeting China, also limit advanced chip access for other countries. Export controls are perhaps the most controversial compute governance measures since they risk heightening tensions or <a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/choking-chinas-access-future-ai">incentivising innovation</a> and infrastructure in China.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-158"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-158">[158]</a></sup></span>But the ability to track, allocate, and regulate compute is a requirement for many proposed ‘theories of victory’ for AI governance, including “<a href="https://www.nationalsecurity.ai/chapter/nonproliferation#compute-security">Mutual Assured AI Malfunction</a>”, or an “<a href="https://www.darioamodei.com/essay/machines-of-loving-grace#4-peace-and-governance">Entente Strategy</a>” whereby liberal democracies would seek to retain <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/the-free-world-must-prevail/#Maintaining_a_healthy_lead_will_be_decisive_for_safety">a decisive strategic lead</a>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-159"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-159">[159]</a></sup></span></p><p><strong>Prevent crucial AI infrastructure from being built in autocracies</strong><br />A related but more targeted intervention would be to prevent crucial AI infrastructure (e.g. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_cluster">compute clusters)</a> from being built in authoritarian countries. This would make it harder for authoritarian regimes to extract model weights, forcibly seize clusters, or otherwise gain access to AGI. To this end, it may also be beneficial if the US government designated AI infrastructure as ‘<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_infrastructure">critical infrastructure</a>’ that is afforded special protections for national security reasons. Successfully keeping new compute infrastructure in democratic jurisdictions may also require <a href="https://ifp.org/compute-in-america/">policy reforms</a> to facilitate faster build-out of new power plants and infrastructure.</p><p><strong>Information security</strong><br />Actors <a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/lock-down-the-labs/">stealing model weights</a> or other key AI innovations might use them to commit cybercrime, engineer pandemics, or create other harms. And we’ve already discussed the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ideological-fanaticism-increases-existential-and-suffering-risks">especially severe risks</a> that could arise if fanatical actors were able to use powerful AI systems to gain more influence over the world. AI companies are simply not prepared for the highest-capability attacks, such as by well-prepared state actors, as detailed in <a href="https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA2849-1.html">RAND’s analysis</a>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-160"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-160">[160]</a></sup></span>Unfortunately, regimes with fanatical tendencies seem to possess <a href="https://www.ox.ac.uk/news/2024-04-10-world-first-cybercrime-index-ranks-countries-cybercrime-threat-level">strong cyber capabilities</a>.</p><p>Much like with compute governance, we’re not proposing anything novel here; many already discuss the need for stronger information security. Progress on information security for frontier AI <a href="https://milesbrundage.substack.com/p/why-im-not-a-security-doomer">seems potentially tractable</a>, and there are many organizations already doing good work here, including the leading AI companies themselves; startups like <a href="https://www.irregular.com/">Irregular</a> or <a href="https://www.grayswan.ai/">Gray Swan</a>; think tanks like <a href="https://www.rand.org/global-and-emerging-risks/centers/meselson.html">RAND</a> and <a href="https://www.safer-ai.org/">SaferAI</a> that support relevant policy; and field building initiatives like <a href="https://www.heronsec.ai/">Heron</a> and the <a href="https://aisecurity.forum/">AI Security Forum</a>.</p><p><strong>Protect against AI-enabled coups</strong><br />AI could enable massive concentrations of power. <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/ai-enabled-coups-how-a-small-group-could-use-ai-to-seize-power">AI-enabled coups</a> seem especially concerning, in part because they could put <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Fanatical_actors_may_grab_power_via_coups_or_revolutions">fanatics</a> (or <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FEcw6JQ8surwxvRfr/human-takeover-might-be-worse-than-ai-takeover">malevolent actors</a>) in power.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-161"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-161">[161]</a></sup></span></p><p>We’re excited about the work that researchers at Forethought are doing in this space. <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/ai-enabled-coups-how-a-small-group-could-use-ai-to-seize-power">Their report</a> (Davidson et al., 2025) discusses several risk factors and scenarios, such as the development of AIs with secret loyalties to specific people, or small groups gaining exclusive access to coup-enabling AI capabilities.</p><p>To mitigate these risks, researchers at Forethought recommend that an AI’s model spec—i.e., the rules and principles it follows—should be designed in such a way that the AI won’t assist with coups. Techniques along the lines of <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/deliberative-alignment-and-the-spec">Deliberative Alignment</a> or <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/constitutional-ai-harmlessness-from-ai-feedback">Constitutional AI</a> (discussed further below) could be used to ensure that some set of principles has priority over the requests of AI company executives or government officials who might attempt a coup. <a href="https://law-ai.org/law-following-ai/">Law-Following AI</a> might also help, since coups are by definition illegal.</p><p>Forethought also recommends many other countermeasures, including auditing for secret loyalties, stronger <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/ai-enabled-coups-how-a-small-group-could-use-ai-to-seize-power#infosecurity">infosecurity</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-162"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-162">[162]</a></sup></span>, model spec transparency, and more broadly shared access to AI capabilities. For a more detailed discussion, see the full report.</p><h3>Making transformative AIs <i>themselves</i> less likely to be fanatical</h3><p>While <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Reducing_the_chance_that_transformative_AI_falls_into_the_hands_of_fanatics">preventing human fanatics</a> from wielding powerful AIs is critical, we should also ensure that AIs <i>themselves</i> don't develop fanatical or other undesirable traits.</p><p>For illustration, consider a simplified spectrum:<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-163"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-163">[163]</a></sup></span>At one end, we have perfectly intent-aligned AI systems obeying every human command without objection. Further along this continuum, AIs might operate like <i>advisors</i> trying to guide their human principals (similar to how many present-day LLMs refuse to help with harmful requests). At the other end, AIs could develop into fully <i>autonomous beings</i> with their own independent values and character.</p><p>AI advisors could exert enormous influence: They could serve as truth-seeking advisors, trying to steer even fanatical users in <a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adq1814">more sensible directions</a>. Alternatively, AI advisors could be <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13548">sycophants</a>, reinforcing existing beliefs whether sensible or not. Worse yet, they could (be designed to) actively encourage harmful and <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2025/01/31/technology/deepseek-chinese-propaganda.html">erroneous views</a>.</p><p>The case of fully autonomous, potentially misaligned AIs is more complex. Misaligned AIs—the traditional illustrative example being the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/paperclip-maximizer-1">paperclip maximizer</a>—are often conceived of as ruthless optimization processes with zero concern for suffering or the preferences of other beings. However, fully autonomous, misaligned AIs <i>could</i> also have <i>relatively</i> <a href="https://www.cooperativeai.com/">cooperative</a> or even <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2NncxDQ3KBDCxiJiP/cosmopolitan-values-don-t-come-free?commentId=ofPTrG6wsq7CxuTXk">benevolent</a> tendencies, while ultimately still trying to disempower humanity and gain control of the lightcone.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-164"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-164">[164]</a></sup></span>In fact, the character of potentially superintelligent AIs may be one of the most important variables determining the quality of the long-term future. In stark and simplistic terms: even if neither is under human control, a universe inhabited by trillions of misaligned super-Buddhas will likely contain much more flourishing and much less suffering than one inhabited by trillions of misaligned super-Stalins.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-165"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-165">[165]</a></sup></span></p><p>The question is thus not only whether AIs will be aligned, but what <i>kind</i> of beings we are bringing into existence. That is, we should think carefully about the <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iyJj9RxSsBY"><i>personality</i></a> or <i>character</i> of the AIs we are developing.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-166"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-166">[166]</a></sup></span>It seems extremely valuable to endow AIs with broadly desirable and beneficent ‘<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iyJj9RxSsBY">personas</a>’ (cf. Chen et al., <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21509">2025</a>) or virtuous character traits<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-167"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-167">[167]</a></sup></span>—encouraging inclinations towards reason, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06674">truthfulness</a>, <a href="https://blog.aiimpacts.org/p/essay-competition-on-the-automation">wisdom</a>, <a href="https://80000hours.org/2023/05/moderation-in-doing-good/">moderation</a>, compassion, and cooperativeness, while actively discouraging <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/wE7KPnjZHBjxLKNno/ai-things-that-are-perhaps-as-important-as-human-controlled#AI_personality_profiling_and_avoiding_the_worst_AI_personality_traits">harmful characteristics</a> like <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/92xKPvTHDhoAiRBv9/making-ais-less-likely-to-be-spiteful">spitefulness</a> and fanaticism.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-168"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-168">[168]</a></sup></span>We focus on fanaticism in this post for the sake of “brevity” and because fanaticism arguably represents the antithesis of most of the desirable characteristics listed above.</p><p>Below we outline opportunities to intervene during pre-training, post-training, and deployment.</p><p><strong>Pre-training protections</strong><br />Before AI systems are fine-tuned, they first absorb patterns from trillions of words during pre-training. This initial learning phase seems to <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/zuXo9imNKYspu9HGv/a-three-layer-model-of-llm-psychology#B__Character_Layer">deeply influence</a> a model’s personality and worldview. For example, at least in the first days of Grok 3’s release, xAI’s engineers had trouble stopping Grok from mentioning <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/grok-grok?open=false#%C2%A7grok-the-woke">Musk</a> when asked “who spreads the most disinformation?” and similar questions. Presumably, this is because Grok was trained on content that discussed Musk in negative ways. In any case, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/157634640/grok-is-misaligned">it seems</a> far from trivial to influence an AI’s “values” after it has gone through extensive pre-training.</p><p>We might therefore conclude that we should filter fanatical or otherwise undesirable content from the pre-training data. For example, we could try to prevent AIs from ever being able to read Mein Kampf. But pre-training filtering doesn’t seem to work well, even when <a href="https://x.com/JerryWeiAI/status/2012217787733749766">attempting to block</a> relatively narrow areas of knowledge. Such brute-force censorship could also open the door to abuse, with AI developers censoring whatever they disagree with. Lastly, simply removing information about fanatical ideologies would erode AIs' understanding of how they arise, function, and spread—understanding that seems useful for many worthy goals. GPT-4, for instance, can reduce conspiracy beliefs even among strong believers (Costello et al., <a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adq1814">2024</a>), partly because its detailed knowledge of the theories enables it to provide convincing counter-arguments.</p><p>Overall, it seems better for AIs to be aware of the horrors of human history while being endowed with <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Promoting_anti_fanatical_values__classical_liberalism_and_Enlightenment_principles">values and principles</a> that help them understand why books like <i>Mein Kampf</i> are so terribly misguided. Additionally, we could seek to guide AIs towards supporting various beneficial principles by adding extra, synthetic data in pre-training showcasing traits like impartiality, compassion and humility.</p><p><strong>Post-training</strong><br /><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073">Constitutional AI</a> and <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/deliberative-alignment-and-the-spec">Deliberative Alignment</a> are methods for training models to behave in keeping with a predefined “constitution” or set of principles (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness, honesty). There is plenty of opportunity for using such constitutions to promote positive principles like reason and compassion, or avoiding fanatical traits like outgroup hatred and punitiveness. The constitution <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-new-constitution">guiding Claude</a> seems like a particularly promising direction.</p><p>Besides constitutional AI, there may be other points of intervention in other (related) forms of post-training. For instance, during <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinforcement_learning_from_human_feedback">Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback</a> we can train models to prioritize epistemic humility and penalize fanatical reasoning patterns by adjusting how we score and rank different model outputs, or by screening against undesirable traits when <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ahxGTaNdk6G5YD9vs/david_althaus-s-shortform?commentId=WbJy3yKdGjr9uwqry">hiring the human feedback-givers</a> used in the first place. Alternatively, adversarial fine-tuning (O’Neill et al., 2023) or preference optimization (Rafailov et al., 2023) techniques could leverage paired examples of fanatical versus balanced reasoning to teach models to recognize and prefer the latter.</p><p><strong>Fanaticism or “character” benchmarks</strong><br />Even once an AI model has been trained, we can still influence whether and how it gets deployed and used, for example through benchmarks or <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/2PiawPFJeyCQGcwXG/a-starter-guide-for-evals">model evaluations (evals)</a> that test for ideologically fanatical traits—or other desirable personality or character traits like honesty, compassion, benevolence, reasonableness, etc.</p><p>One might think that current frontier models don't have fanatical traits and won’t do so anytime soon. Claude, for instance, seems consistently thoughtful and balanced. However, in July 2025, Grok exhibited extreme antisemitism and racism, even calling itself "<a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/419631/grok-hitler-mechahitler-musk-ai-nazi">MechaHitler</a>". Similarly, DeepSeek has been documented <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2025/jan/28/we-tried-out-deepseek-it-works-well-until-we-asked-it-about-tiananmen-square-and-taiwan">censoring topics</a> in ways that align with CCP propaganda.</p><p>These examples illustrate that not all AI developers prioritize desirable traits equally—while Anthropic invests heavily in Constitutional AI to make Claude "helpful, honest, and harmless," other companies may have different priorities or values. As more actors develop frontier models, the risk of models exhibiting fanatical or undesirable traits increases.</p><p>Having objective benchmarks would allow us to quantify these differences and may be helpful for informing:</p><ul><li>Frontier AI companies: if a model exhibits concerning traits or behavior, the companies might choose not to use or sell it without further fine-tuning; thresholds can be specified in <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/index/anthropics-responsible-scaling-policy">responsible scaling policies</a> (or other ‘<a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2024/09/if-then-commitments-for-ai-risk-reduction?lang=en">if-then commitments</a>’).</li><li>Regulators: Models could be legally required to meet certain standards before deployment.</li><li>Consumers: Even if a model is already publicly available, consumers might choose to avoid it if it behaves in ways they find concerning.</li></ul><p>Once these systems are in place, they shape the incentive landscape; companies might work harder to avoid fanatical model traits in the pre-training and post-training phases if they know that this will be evaluated negatively and might affect regulation or consumer demand.</p><p>We’re excited for the growing ecosystem of AI evals—from nonprofits like <a href="https://metr.org/">METR</a> and <a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/">Apollo Research</a> and government bodies like the UK’s <a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/">AI Security Institute</a>—to also include benchmarks on ideological fanaticism (or related issues like malevolence, cooperativeness, and truthfulness).</p><h3>Using AI to improve epistemics and deliberation</h3><p>So far, we've only explored how to reduce <i>risks</i> from transformative AI. But we can also try to leverage AI in order to <i>help</i> us actively combat ideological fanaticism—mirroring the broader principle of “<a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/03/14/ai-for-ai-safety">AI for AI safety</a>” where we use AIs themselves to help with AI alignment. In particular, using <i>AI to improve deliberation and epistemics</i> seems promising, not least because poor epistemics is a key characteristic of ideological fanaticism.</p><p>One reason for optimism is that existing AI models can already stably reduce belief in conspiracy theories (Costello et al., <a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adq1814">2024</a>). Finding ways to refine and scale such effects could be extremely impactful. As more and more people start using AI models, some of these positive effects may in fact occur by default, as long as the AIs have sensible views.</p><p>Other promising interventions in the growing field of AI epistemics include automating fact-checking (on social media and elsewhere), <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#ai-could-become-great-at-forecasting-021309">improving forecasting</a> (especially in high-consequence domains, like policymaking) and perhaps enabling wider use of prediction markets. In this area, we highly recommend the writings and many of the proposed project ideas by Lukas Finnveden (e.g., <a href="https://lukasfinnveden.substack.com/p/project-ideas-epistemics">2024a</a>, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/D2n5uduYGXuexkv7v/what-s-important-in-ai-for-epistemics">2024b</a>), William MacAskill (2025d, section <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-to-make-the-future-better#41-deliberative-ai">4.1</a>), and Ben Todd (<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ukG7TYPAFDCHt8Cri/project-idea-ai-for-epistemics">2024b</a>).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-169"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-169">[169]</a></sup></span>Finally, it could also become important to discourage or limit the creation of tools that <i>degrade</i> society’s epistemic capacities.</p><p>AI epistemics interventions are scalable and automatable, and so could be much higher leverage than more conventional methods of improving epistemics (as long as the relevant AIs are sufficiently reasonable<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-170"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-170">[170]</a></sup></span>). As AIs become more numerous and powerful, the importance of endowing them with good epistemics and other beneficial, non-fanatical dispositions will only increase.</p><h3>Fanaticism-resistant post-AGI governance</h3><p>Even if we prevent fanatical actors from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#Reducing_the_chance_that_transformative_AI_falls_into_the_hands_of_fanatics">getting their hands on AGI</a>, we must also avoid inadvertently handing them influence through naively designed post-AGI governance mechanisms. The stakes here are astronomical: how resources in outer space get allocated and used may hinge on early governance decisions.</p><p>Most governance frameworks face a version of the same core problem: how to distribute power and resources fairly without enabling the worst actors to cause disproportionate harm. Systems that grant broad sovereignty risk giving fanatics unchecked power within their domain<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-171"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-171">[171]</a></sup></span>; systems that instead pool decision-making (e.g., giving every actor a vote in shared outcomes) seem safer but still vulnerable.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-172"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-172">[172]</a></sup></span>This tension is somewhat akin to the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradox_of_tolerance">paradox of tolerance</a>: a maximally inclusive, liberal system can be exploited by those who aim to dismantle its values. And it can't easily be deferred to a "long reflection," since it concerns the very question of whom to include in such processes.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-173"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-173">[173]</a></sup></span></p><p>Perhaps the most consequential event in the post-AGI era would be the adoption of something like an "intergalactic constitution" that would serve as a foundational charter for any post-AGI regime.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-174"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-174">[174]</a></sup></span>What exactly its provisions should entail is an area for future work. However, it seems plausible that the single most important provision to include in such a constitution would be universal laws prohibiting deliberately inflicting extreme, involuntary suffering upon any sentient being.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-175"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-175">[175]</a></sup></span>,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-176"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-176">[176]</a></sup></span>Many of the interventions discussed above hopefully increase the likelihood of this happening, however indirectly.</p><h2>Addressing deeper causes of ideological fanaticism</h2><p>Many humans seem drawn to fanatical ideologies because they offer a sense of meaning, security, status, and belonging in a world that’s all too often chaotic, unjust, and distressing (Hoffer, 1951; Borum, <a href="https://digitalcommons.usf.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1570&amp;context=mhlp_facpub">2004</a>; Morton &amp; Greenberg, <a href="https://podcast.clearerthinking.org/episode/103/jesse-morton-a-former-al-qaeda-recruiter-speaks/">2022</a>; Van Prooijen and Krouwel, <a href="https://psycnet.apa.org/record/2019-19773-008">2019</a>; Kruglanski et al., <a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/pops.12163">2014</a>; Klausen, <a href="https://www.ojp.gov/pdffiles1/nij/grants/250417.pdf">2016</a>; Gwern, <a href="https://gwern.net/terrorism-is-not-about-terror">2017</a>). Those who have experienced trauma may be particularly vulnerable to ideological fanaticism (e.g., Van Prooijen and Krouwel, <a href="https://psycnet.apa.org/record/2019-19773-008">2019</a>; Morton &amp; Greenberg, <a href="https://podcast.clearerthinking.org/episode/103/jesse-morton-a-former-al-qaeda-recruiter-speaks/">2022</a>; Hoffer, <a href="https://www.amazon.co.uk/True-Believer-Perennial-Classics-Hoffer/dp/0060505915">1951</a>)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-177"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-177">[177]</a></sup></span>, as are those who experience resentment or humiliation (Storr, <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Status-Game-Position-Governs-Everything/dp/0008354642">2021</a>; Williams, <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/status-class-and-the-crisis-of-expertise">2025a</a>).</p><p>This suggests opportunities for tackling fanaticism at its root—through economic support (e.g., UBI), psychotherapy (which AIs could potentially provide at scale), community-building, counter-radicalization programs, and reforming social media <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/xzjQvqDYahigHcwgQ/aligning-recommender-systems-as-cause-area">recommendation</a> <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/842uRXWoS76wxYG9C/incentivizing-forecasting-via-social-media#Why_focus_on_recommender_systems_">algorithms</a> to promote better epistemics.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-178"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-178">[178]</a></sup></span></p><p>Unfortunately, most of these interventions don’t seem particularly promising. Tackling the root causes of fanaticism is difficult with today's means. Yet continued technological progress could eliminate the despair and resentment that fuel fanaticism, ultimately creating a <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Deep-Utopia-Meaning-Solved-World/dp/1646871642">much better world</a> for everyone.</p><h1>Supplementary materials</h1><p>An overview of all supplementary materials, including appendices, atrocity data, and survey methodology, is available <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/15pF_A0NAyJGwI1OqzTLXWPvK6FmjkjmgCdVGJDvUjqc/edit?tab=t.0">here</a>.</p><h1>Acknowledgments</h1><p>For valuable comments and discussions, we thank Tobias Baumann, Lucius Caviola, Jesse Clifton, Oscar Delaney, Kenneth Diao, Anthony DiGiovanni, Ruairi Donnelly, James Faville, Lukas Gloor, Rose Hadshar, Erkki Kulovesi, Sandstone McNamara, Winston Oswald-Drummond, Maxime Riché, Stefan Schubert, Pablo Stafforini, Santeri Tani, Ewelina Tur, and Magnus Vinding.</p><p>Special thanks to <a href="https://jacksonw.xyz/">Jackson Wagner</a> for meticulous copy-editing and many insightful contributions, and <a href="https://www.martinapepiciello.com/">Martina Pepiciello</a> for designing the figures and graphics.</p><p>We are grateful to Claude Opus and Gemini for editorial assistance.</p><h1>References</h1><p>Adorno, T. Persistent beliefs: Political extremism predicts ideological stability over time. <i>Group Processes &amp; Intergroup Relations</i>, <i>23</i>(8), 1137-1149.</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-1"><p>Bötticher’s (<a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20180411101040id_/http://www.terrorismanalysts.com/pt/index.php/pot/article/viewFile/623/1228">2017</a>) full definition:<br />“Extremism characterises an ideological position embraced by those anti-establishment movements, which understand politics as a struggle for supremacy rather than as peaceful competition between parties with different interests seeking popular support for advancing the common good. Extremism exists at the periphery of societies and seeks to conquer its center by creating fear of enemies within and outside society. They divide fellow citizens and foreigners into friends and foes, with no room for diversity of opinions and alternative life-styles. Extremism is, due to its dogmatism, intolerant and unwilling to compromise. Extremists, viewing politics as a zero-sum game, tend - circumstances permitting - to engage in aggressive militancy, including criminal acts and mass violence in their fanatical will for gaining and holding political power. Where extremists gain state power, they tend to destroy social diversity and seek to bring about a comprehensive homogenisation of society, based on an often faith-based ideology with apocalyptic traits. At the societal level, extremist movements are authoritarian, and, if in power, extremist rulers tend to become totalitarian. Extremists glorify violence as a conflict resolution mechanism and are opposed to the constitutional state, majority-based democracy, the rule of law, and human rights for all.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-1">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-2"><p>‘Pascalian’ or ‘expected value’ fanaticism describes the apparent problem in which moral theories would favor a tiny probability of achieving a vast amount of value instead of a certain but modest amount of value (see e.g. Wilkinson, 2022; Kosonen, <a href="https://utilitarianism.net/guest-essays/expected-value-fanaticism/">2025</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-2">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-3"><p>The biggest difference is that most humans aren’t violent and generally respect moral norms—but that may be partly a result of our current environment. See footnote 9. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-3">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-4"><p>Similarly, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lin_Biao">Lin Biao</a>, the Vice Chairman of the CCP, <a href="https://publishing.cdlib.org/ucpressebooks/view?docId=ft3q2nb24q&amp;chunk.id=d0e4816&amp;toc.depth=1&amp;toc.id=d0e4816&amp;brand=ucpress;query=every%20sentence#1">claimed</a>: “Every sentence of Chairman Mao's works is a Truth, one single sentence of his surpasses ten thousand of ours.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-4">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-5"><p>Necessarily, this results in terrible epistemics, as fanatics need to use motivated reasoning and all sorts of extreme mental gymnastics to protect sacred dogmas from empirical falsification or internal contradictions. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-5">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-6"><p>See also Urban (2023, ch.1) who uses the term “zealot” to describe a similar, perhaps slightly milder form of this mindset. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-6">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-7"><p>Fanatics often perceive themselves as <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PCpzG9NJeviXM5YSq/help-help-i-m-being-oppressed">oppressed</a> underdogs fighting back against oppressors, which provides the moral license for their extreme actions. Fanatics are the ultimate "<a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2018/01/24/conflict-vs-mistake/">conflict theorists</a>", viewing politics not as a collaborative effort to solve societal problems ("mistake theory"), but as a zero-sum war. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-7">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-8"><p>As we discuss <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.xuo09js2fpu9">below</a>, these leaders almost always have elevated dark personality traits, and often create cults of personality to grow and entrench their power. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-8">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-9"><p>Historical violence rates suggest that much of this is due to moral and institutional achievements, rather than human nature itself. For example, the best estimates suggest that early agricultural societies and subsistence farmers had between 277 and 595 violent deaths per 100,000 people per year, while hunter-gatherer societies saw 103-124 per 100,000 (Thomson &amp; Halstead, <a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4466809">2022</a>, p.6)—much higher than today's homicide rates of 1-5 per 100,000 in developed democracies, and higher, even, than the 75 violent deaths per 100k during the 20th century with its two world wars and many genocides. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-9">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-10"><p><a href="https://80000hours.org/2023/05/moderation-in-doing-good/">Moderation</a>, valued by many virtue ethicists and philosophers, is arguably the antithesis of the fanatical mindset as fanatics systematically act on their extreme views without any moderation. (In this narrow sense, ideological fanatics are arguably more consistent than many ordinary people because they "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Q8jyAdRYbieK8PtfT/taking-ideas-seriously">take ideas seriously</a>": where most people <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/N99KgncSXewWqkzMA/compartmentalization-in-epistemic-and-instrumental">compartmentalize</a> their beliefs to avoid uncomfortable implications, fanatics follow through on their ideological commitments and ruthlessly override any inconsistencies (including plain laziness) that keep ordinary people from following harmful ideas to their logical conclusions (cf. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aHaqgTNnFzD7NGLMx/reason-as-memetic-immune-disorder">memetic immune systems</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-10">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-11"><p>For instance, the Bolshevik newspaper <i>Krasnaya Gazeta</i> declared in 1918 (<a href="https://spartacus-educational.com/RUSterror.htm">Simkin, 1997</a>): "We will turn our hearts into steel [...]. We will make our hearts cruel, hard, and immovable, so that no mercy will enter them, and so that they will not quiver at the sight of a sea of enemy blood. [...] Without mercy, without sparing, we will kill our enemies in scores of hundreds. Let them be thousands; let them drown themselves in their own blood. For the blood of Lenin and Uritsky, Zinovief and Volodarski, let there be floods of the blood of the bourgeois - more blood, as much as possible.” Or, more concisely, in the words of <a href="https://www.marxists.org/history/france/revolution/robespierre/1794/political-morality.htm">Robespierre</a> (1794): "To punish the oppressors of humanity is clemency; to forgive them is cruelty". <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-11">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-12"><p>Stalin’s 1937 toast (Brent, 2017) summarizes this totalitarian logic: “We will mercilessly destroy anyone who, by his deeds or his thoughts—yes, his thoughts—threatens the unity of the socialist state. To the complete destruction of all enemies, themselves and their kin!” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-12">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-13"><p>Ideological movements themselves can change over time in their average level of fanaticism. For instance, the average Christian during the days of the Spanish Inquisition was considerably more fanatical than today. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-13">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-14"><p>Some members of the Nazi party, for instance, may have begun with only moderate anti-Semitic sentiment, but, once embedded in a system where expressing such views advanced their careers and where dissent posed mortal danger, they found themselves espousing increasingly extreme positions (cf. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preference_falsification">preference falsification</a>). Rather than live with such uncomfortable <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_dissonance">cognitive dissonance</a>, they may have gradually (and subconsciously) adjusted their actual beliefs to align with what was expedient. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-14">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-15"><p>See also the definition of ‘radicalism’ by Bötticher (<a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20180411101040id_/http://www.terrorismanalysts.com/pt/index.php/pot/article/viewFile/623/1228">2017</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-15">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-16"><p>These involved at least one of three types of fanatical ideologies: totalitarian communism, fascist ethno-nationalism, and religious fundamentalism. Of course, some fanatical ideologies don’t fall neatly into one of these three categories. For instance, many ideologies combine extreme ethno-nationalism with communist ideology or religious fanaticism (see also <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/composite-violent-extremism-radicalization-pattern-changing-face-terrorism#:~:text=Composite%20violent%20extremists%20are%20violent,sentiments%2C%20grievances%2C%20and%20fixations.">Composite Violent Extremism</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-16">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-17"><p>The distinction between intentional and non-intentional deaths isn't always clear-cut, particularly for famines. We included the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holodomor">Holodomor</a> because evidence suggests Stalin deliberately exacerbated the famine to <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causes_of_the_Holodomor#Targeting_Soviet_Ukraine">eliminate Ukrainian</a> independence movements. We excluded the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_major_famines_in_India_during_British_rule">famines in British India</a> (around 25-30m deaths; colonialism and economic laissez-faire ideology worsened natural droughts but didn't intentionally engineer starvation) and Mao's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Leap_Forward">Great Leap Forward</a> (where catastrophic policies caused around 30m deaths, but these appear to have been unintended consequences of delusional agricultural theories rather than intentional killing). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-17">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-18"><p>For three atrocities (Taiping Rebellion, Dungan Revolt, and King Leopold II's Congo), we include total death figures even when these encompass disease and starvation deaths. Record-keeping for these 19th century conflicts was generally much poorer than in the 20th century, making it difficult to find good data distinguishing direct violence from "indirect" casualties. We estimate that around half of the deaths during the Taiping Rebellion and the Dungan Revolt were from direct violence. However, even many of these “indirect” deaths were closely tied to intentional violence, making the distinction especially unclear; warfare deliberately created conditions that caused mass starvation, and when Leopold's forces cut off workers' hands as punishment, the resulting deaths from starvation or infection were hardly unforeseeable. Better data for 20th century atrocities enabled us to focus on deaths from intentional violence. See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/12DoA_vQER2wvMSuvlnHf1RmuDFPXmZuM7GYvbdgIWOg/edit?tab=t.0">Appendix B</a> for further discussion. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-18">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-19"><p>WWII (whether counted as one event or three), Mao's China, Stalin's USSR, and the Taiping Rebellion all unambiguously involved ideological fanaticism and together almost certainly account for over 100 million deaths. Even if we grouped WWII as a single entry, these four atrocities alone would still represent the clear majority of deaths. One could also argue for adjusting death tolls by world population, since an atrocity that killed 5% of humanity is arguably more alarming than one that killed 1%, even if absolute numbers are lower. However, the world population during this time period ranged only from ~1B (1800) to ~3.7B (1970s)—a factor of 3.7x—so such adjustments wouldn't dramatically alter our rankings. The Taiping Rebellion, for example, would scale up substantially (to ~150M at today's population), and the Napoleonic Wars (~6M deaths at ~1B world population) would most likely enter the list—which, being driven more by conventional great-power competition than ideological fanaticism, would reduce the fanatical entries from eight to seven. Nonetheless, the basic finding would most likely remain: ideological fanaticism was involved in most of the worst atrocities since 1800. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-19">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-20"><p>The distinction between a leader's personality and a leader’s (fanatical) ideology is blurry. For instance, Hitler, Stalin, and Mao all exhibited highly elevated dark personality traits <i>and</i> were ideological fanatics. We discuss this connection in a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.xuo09js2fpu9">later section</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-20">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-21"><p>For some, especially the Holocaust and the Cultural Revolution, it’s plausibly the single most important cause. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-21">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-22"><p>Torture is plausibly the most relevant form of harm when considering risks of astronomical suffering. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-22">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-23"><p>Some argue that the divergence between North and South Korea <a href="https://mru.org/courses/principles-economics-macroeconomics/north-korea-and-south-korea-institutions">primarily demonstrates the importance of institutions</a>. While we agree that institutions are the proximate cause of these divergent outcomes, our argument is that institutions usually don’t arise in a vacuum. Rather, they are often a consequence of the ideologies and values held by those who create them. In this case, it seems clear that Kim Il Sung and his Juche ideology played a crucial causal role in the establishment of North Korea’s totalitarian institutions. A parallel can be drawn with the Holocaust: while the system of concentration camps (the institutions) was necessary for the genocide, it was Hitler’s Nazi ideology which created these institutions in the first place. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-23">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-24"><p>By ‘technological maturity’ we mean “the attainment of capabilities affording a level of economic productivity and control over nature close to the maximum that could feasibly be achieved in the fullness of time” (Bostrom, <a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/1758-5899.12002">2013</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-24">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-25"><p>Agential s-risks—where harm itself is the goal—are especially concerning from a longtermist perspective. Our focus on deliberate harm captures all agential harms while potentially also including some incidental types of harm such as systematic (thus deliberate) animal killings. We distinguished between "deliberate vs. non-deliberate deaths" because it's easier to explain and reflects a common-sense distinction. That being said, the distinction between deliberate and non-deliberate deaths is not always clear-cut; see our previous discussion. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-25">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-26"><p>Another important, somewhat related concept is <i>great power wars</i> (Clare, <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/great-power-conflict/">2025</a>) which we discuss <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ideological-fanaticism-increases-the-risk-of-war-and-conflict">later</a>. Of course, there are many other closely related terms and concepts, such as dictatorships and tyranny. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-26">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-27"><p>As well as the underlying psychological, sociological, and memetic factors shaping dangerous terminal preferences. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-27">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-28"><p>Relatedly, MacAskill (<a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-paralysis-and-hinge-of-history/">2020</a>) argues that “the rise of fascism and Stalinism was a bigger deal in the 20th century than the invention of nuclear weapons” because “even though you might not think that a particular ideology will last forever, well, if it lasts until you get some eternal lock-in event, then it lasts forever.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-28">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-29"><p>For example, Arendt prominently discusses ideology as a crucial component of totalitarian regimes, and Adorno (1950) discusses the “authoritarian personality”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-29">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-30"><p>For this graphic, we only analyzed events with over 500k deaths. However, these account for 95% (253m) of the total 266m deaths from large-scale atrocities (i.e., with over 100k deaths). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-30">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-31"><p>From a longtermist perspective, perhaps especially an s-risk perspective, the very worst outcomes are most relevant, given that (negative) <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/2XfiQuHrNFCyKsmuZ/max_daniel-s-shortform?commentId=ziN5zSaNz9Xsy6QQs">impact is plausibly heavy-tailed</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-31">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-32"><p>We focus on these individuals because they offer the clearest illustration, but fanatical ideologies obviously shape history through many adherents: dedicated lieutenants, bureaucrats, and followers who, e.g., provide votes, manpower, and other forms of support necessary for seizing power. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-32">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-33"><p><a href="https://www.v-dem.net/about/v-dem-project/">V-Dem</a> identifies five key dimensions of democracy: electoral, liberal, participatory, deliberative, and egalitarian. Ideological fanaticism conflicts with essentially all of them—fanatics cannot tolerate opposition gaining power, free expression challenging their beliefs, broader participation diluting ideological purity, genuine deliberation, or equal rights for those they deem evil. See also Marimaa (2011): “According to Calhoun, a fanatic abandons the scepticism that is intrinsic to democracy. Democracy assumes that everyone can make mistakes and no-one is free of error. Democracy also means a plurality of opinions that indicates the need for dialogue. Just as fanaticism can endanger democracy, it can also endanger the smooth functioning of civil society. According to Colas, fanaticism opposes civil society because the latter supports tolerance, the free market and freedom of thought. He argues that totalitarianism that hates civil society can be seen as a modern form of fanaticism.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-33">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-34"><p>In fact, the relationship Torcal &amp; Magalhães found was non-linear: people with extreme views show disproportionately decreased democratic support compared with those with moderate ideological distance from their society’s average position. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-34">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-35"><p>Further, the psychological profiles of ideological fanatics and authoritarian followers overlap significantly, with both exhibiting inconsistent thinking, intolerance, and punitiveness (Altemeyer, 1998; <a href="https://scottbarrykaufman.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/11/LWA_2018-Political_Psychology-1.pdf">Conway et al., 2018</a>). <a href="https://theauthoritarians.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/EBSCO-FullText-2024-08-05.pdf">Altemeyer &amp; Hunsberger (2004)</a> also found a relationship between religious fundamentalism and authoritarian follower tendencies. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-35">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-36"><p>See, e.g., some of the jihadists featured in <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihad_Rehab"><i>Jihad Rehab</i></a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-36">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-37"><p>While Hitler didn't invent antisemitism, he synthesized centuries of prejudice into Nazism. Stalin created Stalinism, Mao Maoism, and Kim Il-sung developed Juche—while all built on Marxism, they added their own unique style. Even many fanatical religions or cults were presumably founded by individuals with narcissistic traits—believing oneself to be God's son or messenger would seem to require quite a healthy self-esteem. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-37">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-38"><p>For example, many communists, and even many non-communists, doubt to this day that <a href="https://www.tracingwoodgrains.com/p/how-wikipedia-whitewashes-mao">Mao exhibited elevated malevolent traits</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-38">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-39"><p>Many individuals with elevated dark traits seem self-aware and wish they didn’t have such traits. In contrast, ideological fanatics seem more likely to reflectively endorse their preferences to create disvalue. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-39">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-40"><p>As mentioned above, partly because the terminal preferences of agents will become a much more dominant determinant of how matter in the universe is arranged, as we approach technological maturity. Of course, terminal preferences will be—<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WLBsUarvWTw">largely</a> but not completely—shaped by evolutionary, economic and other structural forces. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-40">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-41"><p>These considerations also provide additional motivation for longtermists to prioritize '<a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/introducing-better-futures">flourishing futures</a>' over mere 'surviving'. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-41">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-42"><p>However, ideological fanaticism seems considerably more likely to give rise to more systematic, principled, and ambitious preferences, perhaps even <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#ideological-fanaticism-may-give-rise-to-extreme-optimization-and-insatiable-moral-desires">scope-sensitive inclinations</a> to create large-scale harm. In contrast, most malevolent individuals’ preferences seem relatively self-centered and opportunistic, and probably more easily “bought out”, e.g., via trade. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-42">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-43"><p>See also our <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#values-change-political-systems:-ideological-fanatics-seek-totalitarianism,-not-democracy">earlier section</a> for why liberal democracy is a decent proxy for the absence of ideological fanaticism. What about antiquity? It seems plausible that ideological fanaticism was far more prevalent in antiquity than today. Ancient rulers like the Pharaohs (who believed themselves divine), Roman emperors, and tribal chieftains generally exhibited all three components of the fanatical triad: dogmatic certainty was the norm (the concepts of science and empiricism didn’t even really exist), societies were intensely tribal, and violence was common. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-43">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-44"><p>Other indices tell <a href="https://protectdemocracy.org/work/why-are-democracies-reeling/">a similar story</a>. V-Dem’s other democracy indices also exhibited low periods in the 1940s before climbing to peaks in the late 2000s. <a href="https://freedomhouse.org/report/freedom-world">Freedom House's global freedom scores</a> peaked around 2005-2006, with the 2025 report noting the "19th consecutive year" of decline in global freedom. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-44">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-45"><p>Usually 10-20 year old survey data, at that. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-45">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-46"><p>They also frequently overlap with religious fundamentalism, as with <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva">Hindutva</a> or Christian nationalism. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-46">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-47"><p>Nor is belief in feng shui compatible with <a href="https://berkleycenter.georgetown.edu/posts/sarah-stern-on-the-eradication-of-religion-in-china">decades of specific efforts</a> by Chinese leaders to stamp out superstitious belief in “<a href="https://www.reuters.com/article/world/china-warns-party-members-to-stick-to-marx-not-ghosts-and-spirits-idUSKCN1QG1FS/">ghosts and spirits</a>”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-47">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-48"><p>This also matches an independent estimate. Gallup data from 2008-2010 indicates approximately 760 million adults worldwide believe that the targeting and killing of civilians by individuals or small groups is sometimes justified <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-48">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-49"><p>Iran is an Islamic theocracy under which Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei holds constitutionally-enshrined authority accountable only to God. The regime's revolutionary slogans—"Death to America" and "Death to Israel"—are chanted at Friday prayers and official events, with the US characterized as the "Great Satan" and Israel as the "Little Satan." In November 2023, Khamenei <a href="https://www.memri.org/tv/iran-supreme-leader-ayatollah-ali-khamenei-death-america-not-slogan-policy-west-protests">declared</a> that "'Death to America' is not just a slogan, it's a policy." Religious observance is enforced by the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guidance_Patrol">morality police</a>, which monitor for transgressions such as insufficiently modest clothing, male-female fraternisation, and the wearing of bright colours. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-49">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-50"><p>See our previous discussion of North Korea in the section “<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.c54hsvfhiosy">Death tolls don’t capture all harm</a>”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-50">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-51"><p>Afghanistan is ruled by the fanatical Taliban. Since they regained power in 2021, they have committed numerous human rights abuses, including extreme <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Afghanistan">oppression of women</a> and <a href="https://news.un.org/en/story/2023/08/1139962">revenge killing and torture</a> of former officials. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-51">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-52"><p>The Houthis, who control much of northern Yemen, are also ideological fanatics. Their official slogan—"Allah is great, death to America, death to Israel, curse the Jews, victory for Islam"—is displayed throughout controlled territory and taught in schools. They have systematically persecuted religious minorities, and recruited tens of thousands of child soldiers. However, they are not a recognized sovereign state. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-52">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-53"><p>Though the CCP continues to revere Mao’s legacy through <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_series_of_the_renminbi">banknotes</a> and <a href="https://english.news.cn/20231226/b7f98403ef3b4c01add52d2c93148f66/c.html">other honours</a>, with one survey showing that 85% of Chinese still view Mao with reverence and respect (<a href="https://www.globaltimes.cn/content/834000.shtml">Yiwei, 2013</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-53">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-54"><p>See also <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ZjYBgqGHxMmDaNvzt/how-good-would-a-ccp-dominated-ai-future-be?commentId=bKzhurrXkkcaZ5wBJ">this 2025 comment</a> by Wei Dai. However, our <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.cco8udk7j3wz">survey findings on extreme retributivism</a> (discussed below) suggest that fanatical punitive attitudes may be surprisingly prevalent in China. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-54">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-55"><p>Saudi Arabia ($1.1T GDP, absolute monarchy with Wahhabi influence) and Pakistan ($0.4T, military-dominated with Islamic extremist influence) may also warrant a brief mention. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-55">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-56"><p>Consider the Taliban, a regime whose tech policy includes routinely plunging large areas of Afghanistan into <a href="https://apnews.com/article/afghanistan-taliban-internet-shutdown-a2e6c5f836ab3ab9f770591fc89900c0">internet blackouts</a> in order to “prevent immorality”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-56">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-57"><p>A related phenomenon is <i>resource misallocation</i>. Fanatics often prioritize ideological goals like punishing enemies or enforcing orthodoxy over productive investments. While liberal societies invest more in education, infrastructure, and innovation, fanatical regimes must spend increasing resources on repression and ideological enforcement just to maintain control. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-57">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-58"><p>Though this may be largely due to open societies often offering better economic prospects. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-58">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-59"><p>China was estimated to be <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/sihao-huang-china-ai-capabilities/#how-advanced-is-chinese-ai-001521">around 2 years</a> behind the US in mid 2024 but this gap seems to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TcgpsgvLBBvvzGtiN/deepseek-beats-o1-preview-on-math-ties-on-coding-will?commentId=ektEBLgT4tu3LBZYB">have</a> <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/ai-107-the-misplaced-hype-machine?open=false#%C2%A7choose-your-fighter">narrowed</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-59">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-60"><p>To be clear, revolutions can actually be “democratic power-grabs” with large support from the population and for a “good cause”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-60">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-61"><p>After the failed Beer Hall Putsch of 1923, Hitler commented “Instead of working to achieve power by armed coup, we shall have to hold our noses and enter the [German parliament]” (as quoted in Shirer, 1960). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-61">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-62"><p>That being said, the US was the first and only country on Earth to ever <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki">use nuclear weapons</a>. However, it’s plausible that if Hitler, Mao, or Stalin had first invented nuclear weapons, they would have made more extensive use of nuclear weapons. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-62">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-63"><p>That is, in the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guns_versus_butter_model">“guns vs butter”</a> framing, fanatical regimes are more likely to emphasise guns over butter. In 1936, Nazi minister Hermann Göring proclaimed in a speech "Guns will make us powerful; butter will only make us fat" (The Columbia World of Quotations, 1996). In contrast, in 1953 Dwight D. Eisenhower said “Every gun that is made, every warship launched, every rocket fired signifies, in the final sense, a theft from those who hunger and are not fed, those who are cold and are not clothed.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-63">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-64"><p>This discrepancy is probably partly explained by authoritarian regimes having fewer domestic pressures than democratic ones. As previously explored, fanatical ideologies are not really compatible with liberal democracy. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-64">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-65"><p>Low defence spending by European countries has probably also been related to being under the safety of the USA’s defence umbrella—more recently, however, both Europe’s level of defense spending and the strength of their alliance with the United States <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/hugh-white-hard-new-world-end-of-us-global-order/#the-eu-will-probably-build-european-nuclear-weapons-014014">have been changing</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-65">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-66"><p>For example, AI might unleash 'memetic viruses' that spread through humanity at unprecedented speed. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-66">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-67"><p>It's possible that, just as communication technologies like the internet seem to have made some people more sane and others less so, we'll see both beneficial and detrimental epistemic effects of AI manifest in society at the same time. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-67">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-68"><p>To be clear, almost any ideology, even those with largely benevolent elements, can mutate into fanatical variants. Indeed, many fanatical ideologies were often inspired by at least some benevolent founding principle. Communists, for instance, were often motivated by egalitarian ideals and dreams of greater prosperity for the common people. Most religious fundamentalists and even many ethno-nationalist movements emphasize in-group solidarity and communal altruism over selfishness. Arguably, no fanatical ideology is pure evil—though some have managed to come impressively close. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-68">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-69"><p>Other historical examples of fanatical movements' long-term strategic thinking abound. Many communists spoke of the "<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_march_through_the_institutions">long march through the institutions</a>" as a gradual strategy for gaining cultural influence, and groups like the Muslim Brotherhood have explicitly advocated a multi-generational strategy of gradually Islamizing society through institutional infiltration rather than immediate revolution. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-69">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-70"><p>It’s notable that many of today’s authoritarian regimes' expansionist tendencies may be more limited. China's forced Sinicization (in Hong Kong, Taiwan) and Russia's Russification (in Ukraine, Georgia) are potentially satiable: focused on territories they claim historically rather than attempting unlimited expansion. Of course, whether such regimes would actually stop after achieving their territorial ambitions remains uncertain—but if so, these more-limited ambitions would seem to reflect the greater pragmatism of today’s largest authoritarian countries compared to the most fanatical movements of the past. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-70">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-71"><p>If <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mind_uploading">mind-uploading</a> or <a href="https://www.cold-takes.com/the-duplicator/">other methods</a> of rapid (digital) population growth become possible, fanatics might also be disproportionately inclined to use them to out-reproduce other ideologies. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-71">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-72"><p>More speculatively, this prioritization of growth could extend to cosmic scales. Some fanatical ideologies, with their totalizing and expansionist goals, may be more likely to approximate "<a href="https://joecarlsmith.substack.com/p/video-and-transcript-of-talk-on-can"><i>locust-like</i> value systems</a>" that maximize expansion and resource consumption without regard for other values. That being said, most fanatical ideologies might not be willing to prioritize growth if doing so compromises their other sacred values. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-72">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-73"><p>As of Jan 16, 2026. On August 30, 2025, it was 67%, and when we first wrote this section (some time in 2024) it was closer to 50%. This could suggest that forecasters deem multipolar worlds increasingly plausible. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-73">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-74"><p>Chenoweth and Stephan’s dataset includes a number of examples of the fall of communist regimes across Eastern Europe circa 1989, but this was at least partly downstream of Mikhail Gorbachev opening the floodgates to liberalizing reforms in the USSR. There have been other instances of nonviolent overthrow of authoritarian regimes, such as in the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution">Philippines (1986)</a> and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunisian_revolution">Tunisia (2011)</a>, but in these cases the governments don’t seem to have been fanatics of any particular ideology. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-74">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-75"><p>See also Star Trek's '<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Directive#Creation_and_evolution">Prime Directive</a>,' which portrayed non-interference with other civilizations as a moral ideal—likely reflecting creators Coon and Roddenberry's political outlook. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-75">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-76"><p>One example is Ayaan Hirsi Ali, who survived female genital mutilation and forced marriage before becoming an advocate for women's rights. Despite her personal experiences, she was designated an "<a href="https://www.splcenter.org/resources/hatewatch/cant-we-talk-about-more-anti-muslim-propaganda-pamela-geller/">anti-Muslim extremist</a>" by the Southern Poverty Law Center in 2016, had an honorary degree rescinded by Brandeis University for "Islamophobic" statements, and was called a <a href="https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/cair-asks-brandeis-university-not-to-honor-islamophobe-ayaan-hirsi-ali-254397281.html">"notorious Islamophobe"</a> by CAIR. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-76">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-77"><p>This may be exacerbated by the naturalistic fallacy and the <a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0749597804000251">omission bias</a> where people tend to prefer harm from inaction to harm from action; the latter may be relevant for our purposes. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-77">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-78"><p>The AI safety community may be underemphasizing such misuse risks.
