As Ben Todd (<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/YDjH6ACPZq889tqeJ/updates-on-the-ea-catastrophic-risk-landscape">2024a</a>) observes: 'Within AI risk, it seems plausible the community is somewhat too focused on risks from misalignment rather than mis-use or concentration of power.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-78">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-79"><p>From a (naive) rationalist game-theoretic perspective, wars are a puzzle that requires explanation. Prima facie, rational agents should be able to avoid costly wars by bargaining to find mutually beneficial compromise solutions. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-79">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-80"><p>Re 1), Fearon uses the term ”irrational”, Blattman uses the term misperception. Re 2), Blattman talks of “intangible incentives” and Fearon of “issue indivisibilities” (which seem different but somewhat related concepts). Re 3), Fearon only briefly mentions this in the first paragraph. Re 4), Blattman uses the term “uncertainty”, Fearon talks of “private information and incentives to misrepresent”. Both use the term “commitment problem”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-80">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-81"><p>These values don’t have to be bad or misguided in themselves. For example, some may view human rights as a sacred value. In practice, however, secular governments and individuals are much more likely to put a (very high) price on them, e.g. in hostage negotiations, and thus arguably such values are not completely sacred. Probably more importantly, a sacred value on “human rights” is much less dangerous because they are relatively easily achievable in the sense that just not killing and torturing humans is enough to satisfy this sacred value. To be more precise, fulfilling the sacred value of “human rights” is compatible with a large fraction of all possible world states and many other value systems. In contrast, if one’s sacred value is total obedience to a long list of religious dogmas, this is incompatible with a much larger fraction of possibility space. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-81">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-82"><p>This is the term used in the psychological literature (e.g., Tetlock, <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12860191/">2003</a>). Blattman (2023) and Fearon (1995) use the terms “intangible incentive” and “<i>issue</i> <i>indivisibility</i>”, respectively, to refer to similar (but not identical) concepts. Sacred values also relate to the concept of “<i>having something to lose</i>” which some view as a highly desirable property in the context of cooperative AI. For example Nguyen (<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/wE7KPnjZHBjxLKNno/ai-things-that-are-perhaps-as-important-as-human-controlled#Conceptual_progress">2024</a>) writes: “Several people think [having something to lose] is very important [...]. It intuitively is meant to capture the difference between “If I engage in this conflict I might lose everything I hold dear while cooperation guarantees that I can at least keep what I have right now” and “I have nothing to lose anyway, let’s fight and maybe I’ll get the thing I really want but am unlikely to get by default.”” When sacred values are violated, people can perceive the current situation as essentially infinitely bad, and thus believe that they have nothing to lose since the status quo cannot get any worse. This mindset vastly increases one’s willingness to engage in conflict, regardless of winning probability, and resorting to extreme measures to alter the status quo. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-82">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-83"><p>“[...R]eligious fundamentalists among both Jews and Muslims assassinated their political leaders [because they] were willing to make religious compromises and come to a peace agreement between Islamic and Judaic forces. Rabin's assassin, Yigal Amir [...]&nbsp;claimed that <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin">Rabin</a> [the moderate Prime Minister of Israel who was awarded with the Nobel Peace Prize] was guilty of renouncing eternal Jewish rights to the Holy Land, which in his view was solely the land and territory of the Jews as promised by God in the Hebrew Bible. For Amir and his followers, Rabin had to be killed so that no sacred Jewish land would be ceded to the Arabs. Similarly, for the militants in the Muslim Brotherhood who were responsible for the murder of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat">Sadat</a> [a moderate Egyptian President who was also awarded the Nobel Peace Prize], compromise with the Israelis violated Islamic law and constituted an act of religious infidelity punishable by death.” [...] Each side claims that it has a sacred obligation to wage war against the other side in order to reach its religious goal of full control of the Holy Land.” – Selengut (2017). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-83">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-84"><p>“[...] The 1939 Winter War between Finland and the Soviet Union followed on the refusal of the Finnish government to cede some tiny islands in the Gulf of Finland that Stalin seems to have viewed as necessary for the defense of Leningrad in the event of a European war. One of the main reasons the Finns were so reluctant to grant these concessions was that they believed they could not trust Stalin not to use these advantages to pressure Finland for more in the future. So it is possible that Stalin's inability to commit himself not to attempt to carry out in Finland the program he had just applied in the Baltic states may have led or contributed to a costly war both sides clearly wished to avoid.” (Fearon, 1995, p. 408). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-84">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-85"><p>The same “differential commitment credibility” issue also seems to apply to <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/LpkXtFXdsRd4rG8Kb/reducing-long-term-risks-from-malevolent-actors">malevolent actors</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-85">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-86"><p>Another example is how Hitler and Mussolini betrayed the Munich agreement which was initially celebrated in much of Europe as “peace for our time”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-86">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-87"><p>See also Leskelä (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LvtsFKxg2t3nWhKRq/commitment-and-credibility-in-multipolar-ai-scenarios">2020</a>) for a more systematic discussion of commitments and credibility. See e.g. this quote: "[...] committing to threats could require completely different mechanisms or approaches than committing to cooperation [...]." <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-87">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-88"><p>It’s unclear how ideological fanaticism interacts with <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/brXr7PJ2W4Na2EW2q/the-commitment-races-problem">commitment <i>races</i></a> (where two or more agents rush to make the first unyielding commitments about how they’ll interact with each other, in order to constrain their adversary’s options and gain the upper hand). Note that while both commitment problems and commitment races involve commitments, they represent different dynamics: in commitment problems, <i>inability</i> to commit is what contributes to conflict, whereas in commitment races the <i>ability</i> to commit is what contributes to conflict. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-88">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-89"><p>There have generally been two main categories of explanations advanced for why democracies are less conflict-prone: that decision-makers in democratic countries tend to act in accordance with democratic (non-violent) norms; and that institutional strength and accountability are restraining forces when it comes to instigating conflict (Russett, 1993; Rosato, 2003). Tomz and Weeks (2012) propose two further possible mechanisms: that citizens of democracies perceive other democracies as less threatening, and also consider it to be morally problematic to initiate conflicts with other democracies. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-89">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-90"><p>There is only weak evidence that democracies are <i>generally</i> less conflict-prone than autocratic states (Maoz, 1989; Müller &amp; Wolff, 2004). In particular, it is not known if conflicts between democratic and autocratic states occur less frequently than conflicts between autocratic states. However, even if evidence showed that autocracies are less likely to engage in conflict with one another than with democracies, this would hardly serve as a reason to endorse autocracy! In <i>Autocracy Inc.</i> (2024), Anne Applebaum illustrates how autocratic regimes often collaborate to undermine democracies. While such aligned autocracies may experience reduced internal conflict, they represent a significant threat to global progress and wellbeing. As a concrete example, during World War II, the collaboration of the Allied forces was crucial in defeating allied regimes that posed a risk of immense harm. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-90">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-91"><p>Sandberg uses radical negative utilitarians as an example. However, all of the other fanatical ideologies that have been mentioned in this piece seem much more worrisome. There are almost certainly less than 1,000 radical negative utilitarians on Earth—orders of magnitude less than, say, radical Islamists. Prominent negative utilitarians like Brian Tomasik also emphasize <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/reasons-to-be-nice-to-other-value-systems/">cooperation</a> and <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/possible-ways-to-promote-compromise/">compromise</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-91">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-92"><p>We think retributivism is misguided because it’s cruel and because we don’t believe that anyone, ultimately, “deserves” anything as there is no libertarian free will. But hopefully most readers who endorse moderate retributivism agree with the concerns we outline about <i>fanatical</i> retributivism. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-92">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-93"><p>One could argue that endorsing extreme eternal punishment is inherently fanatical, at least by our definition: it requires sufficient certainty to endorse potentially permanent and irreversible action, usually involves extreme hostility toward an outgroup of "evil people," and embraces using the most extreme measures imaginable. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-93">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-94"><p>We only included participants who passed two attention checks, reported answering with complete honesty (in the section of the survey containing the questions above), and provided a valid number or birth year confirming they were between 18 and 110 years of age, and didn’t strongly violate additivity (see footnote 95). Importantly, our results seem robust to both weaker and stricter inclusion criteria. For example, in our “extra strict” sample (N=748), we only included participants who in addition to the previous inclusion criteria, also reported being fluent in the language in which the survey was administered, didn’t violate additivity at all (see again footnote 95), and their free text responses showed evidence of serious engagement. The results were similar though the responses were somewhat less retributive. <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1F0z8o6lR7N8zYbXNsQplrTW27MfGcZdjv7Gl7hZl3P0/edit?tab=t.0">See our supplementary materials for details</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-94">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-95"><p>We excluded participants with high additivity violations. Here is how we calculated this: Participants first saw the question “Of all people in the world, what proportion of them deserve extremely happy lives?”, three questions later, on a different page, they were asked “[...] what proportion deserves unbearable pain forever?”. We excluded participants whose percentages to these two questions added up to more than 110%. We chose this rather arbitrary threshold because i) some people ain’t so good at math and could have easily messed up their “calculations” by 10%, and ii) participants couldn’t go back to edit their earlier response (and we didn’t want to exclude people who may have updated their views and didn’t violate additivity a lot). The results from participants with no additivity violation (i.e., their proportions summed to no more than 100%) were slightly lower: 45% said 1% or higher, a quarter answered 6% or higher. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-95">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-96"><p>We chose the ASP-8 scale because it does not include any items about people deserving suffering. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-96">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-97"><p>Here is the full wording of some of these items along with their respective Spearman correlations: “Society should make sure my core beliefs or principles are always adhered to without exception, regardless of whether people agree with them.” (ρ ≈ 0.37); “I'll do for my religion more than any of its other followers would do.” (ρ ≈ 0.34), “; Some sources of knowledge (people, texts or traditions) provide absolute truths, are always correct, and should never be doubted. (ρ ≈ 0.32); “I insist upon my group getting the respect that is due to it.” (ρ ≈ 0.30); “I'll do more for my group than any other group members would do.” (ρ ≈ 0.30); “I make my religion strong.” (ρ ≈ 0.26). Wanting hell to be created also correlated with dehumanization-related items (ρ = 0.21) and with overall verbal identity fusion score with their selected group (ρ ≈ 0.17). Almost all of these items also correlated at roughly similar magnitudes with our other questions about supporting eternal punishment. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-97">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-98"><p>Many respondents, reading very quickly to maximize earnings per hour, might just interpret the question as “Evil people: Yay or boo?” and respond with “very boo!”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-98">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-99"><p>That said, with sufficiently powerful AI, enacting preferences may become as quick and abstract as answering survey questions—the AI asks what you want, you answer, and it's done. This would continue a historical trend toward ever-greater psychological distance from harm: a few thousand years ago, killing required getting one's hands dirty; today's technology allows vast destruction at the push of a button. The pilots of the Enola Gay likely could not have killed Hiroshima's civilians by hand, yet dropping the bomb was psychologically manageable. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-99">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-100"><p>In the UK and US samples recruited via Prolific, 18-20% agreed with the “create hell” question. In samples from China, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey (recruited via Positly), agreement ranged from 51-57%. Regarding the “want system” question, 13-18% in the Prolific samples agreed, compared to 39-53% in non-Western samples. Finally, for the “would create system” question, 15% of Prolific respondents agreed compared to 35–52% in non-Western samples. On the “duration” question, 18-19% in the Prolific samples selected “forever”, compared to 32-43% in non-Western samples. To illustrate, consider the hell question in Pakistan, our least reliable sample. Overall, 51% of our Pakistan sample endorsed the hell question, but only 25% were in the group who endorsed the hell question <i>and</i> also selected 'Forever' in the duration question, and only 10.5% met those criteria <i>and</i> endorsed at least 1% in the proportion question. This suggests that participant inconsistency (and perhaps viewing hell as a deterrent) contributed to the large gap between the base rate of apparent hell endorsement and the proportion of “consistent and concerning” responders. Moreover, a mere 13% of the Pakistan sample passed our attention checks and met our other inclusion criteria—the lowest inclusion rate of any country in our study. The fact that substantial inconsistency remained even after filtering out so many participants suggests that there were persistent data quality issues with our Pakistan sample. Other non-Western samples also exhibited inconsistencies—see our supplementary materials. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-100">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-101"><p>Though we did work with professional translators. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-101">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-102"><p>For example, 39% of US Prolific respondents identified as atheist/agnostic, compared to ~14% in the US Positly sample—which is much closer to the proportion found in <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_United_States">representative US surveys</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-102">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-103"><p>In Buddhism and Hinduism, the closest concept to hell is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naraka_(Buddhism)">Naraka</a> (in Hinduism it’s also referred to as <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naraka_(Hinduism)">Yamaloka</a>), where sinners are tormented after death. However, there are two crucial differences between the Abrahamic conception of hell and Naraka which make the latter less worrisome from a longtermist perspective: i) souls don’t remain forever but only <i>temporarily</i> in Naraka until “their negative <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_in_Buddhism">Karma</a> is exhausted” and ii) their suffering is seen as a natural consequence rather than a deliberate and morally desirable divine punishment. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-103">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-104"><p>Probably in part due to passages like this, many Islamic theologians seem to believe that <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahannam#Organization,_size,_and_guards">many more</a> people go to hell than to heaven. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-104">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-105"><p>For example, this <a href="https://www.reddit.com/user/aspdpositive/">Reddit user</a> <a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/DebateAChristian/comments/3a3fcr/comment/csa0iqq/?utm_source=share&amp;utm_medium=web3x&amp;utm_name=web3xcss&amp;utm_term=1&amp;utm_content=share_button">claims</a> that he would enjoy seeing those tortured in hell forever, adding that “Nothing has ever filled me with as much euphoria as hearing something wail in pain.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-105">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-106"><p>“If the Lord wills it, they say, it will be, and it is our task to obey the word and try as hard as we can to realize God’s will” (Selengut, 2017). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-106">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-107"><p>See also Ian Banks’ <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_Detail">Surface Detail</a>. While of course a work of fiction, and thus containing many implausible scenarios and assumptions, it also outlines political, economic, and religious motivations for creating digital hells, some of which aren’t completely implausible. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-107">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-108"><p>The full quote (Sapolsky, 2017): “What would I do with Hitler? The viscera become so raw that I switch to passive voice in my mind, to get some distance. What should be done with Hitler? It’s easy to imagine, once I allow myself. Sever his spine at the neck, leave him paralyzed but with sensation. Take out his eyes with a blunt instrument. Puncture his eardrums, rip out his tongue. Keep him alive, tube-fed, on a respirator. Immobile, unable to speak, to see, to hear, only able to feel. Then inject him with something that will give him a cancer that festers and pustulates in every corner of his body, that will grow and grow until every one of his cells shrieks with agony, till every moment feels like an infinity spent in the fires of hell. That’s what should be done with Hitler. That’s what I would want done to Hitler. That’s what I would do to Hitler.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-108">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-109"><p>To be clear, Sapolsky is adamant about never wanting to act upon this dark fantasy. However, Sapolsky's ego-dystonic attitude is informed by his neuroscientific understanding of human behavior, a perspective that may not constrain others' retributivist intuitions. While fictional, the Black Mirror episodes <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Bear_(Black_Mirror)">White Bear</a> and <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Christmas_(Black_Mirror)">White Christmas</a> are also noteworthy. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-109">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-110"><p>Will MacAskill defines viatopia as “a state of the world where society can guide itself towards near-best outcomes, whatever they may be” (MacAskill, <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/introducing-better-futures">2025b</a>) <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-110">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-111"><p>For example, MacAskill and Wiblin (<a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#what-conditions-make-eventual-eutopia-likely-032849">2025</a>) discuss trade but also mention the risks of agents self-modifying their preferences (e.g., towards placing positive weight on suffering) to increase their bargaining position. Previously, MacAskill (<a href="https://futureoflife.org/podcast/moral-uncertainty-and-the-path-to-ai-alignment-with-william-macaskill/">2018</a>) discussed trade more optimistically: “One thing you could do is just say, ‘Well, we've got ten billion people alive today, let's say. We're gonna divide the universe into ten billionths, so maybe that's a thousand galaxies each or something.’ And then you can trade after that point. I think that would get a pretty good outcome.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-111">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-112"><p>Curiosity, humility, and good epistemics are likely important for getting the most important questions right. Fanatics typically lack all three. Some potentially crucial considerations may be arcane and require sophisticated reasoning. How plausible is it that religious fanatics who literally believe that God created the universe in six days contribute rather than derail discussions involving <a href="https://longtermrisk.org/files/Multiverse-wide-Cooperation-via-Correlated-Decision-Making.pdf">multiverse-wide evidential cooperation</a>, <a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/category/meta-ethics">meta-ethics</a>, and the <a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/ai-creation-and-the-cosmic-host.pdf">cosmic host</a>? <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-112">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-113"><p>A related concept is “value porosity” (Bostrom, <a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/porosity.pdf">2014</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-113">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-114"><p><a href="https://www.amazon.co.uk/Nine-Lives-Time-Inside-al-Qaeda/dp/1786075407"><i>Nine Lives</i></a> by Dean et al. (ch. 3, 2018). Dean describes the strict Islamic study group in Saudi Arabia that he joined in the early 1990s. This group, which later funneled members into al-Qaeda, banned watching "The Smurfs" among various other restrictions. According to Dean, the group considered the show a "Western plot to destroy the fabric of our society" and promote sexual freedom because of the single female character, Smurfette, among many males. They also believed the show promoted witchcraft. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-114">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-115"><p>See <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ahxGTaNdk6G5YD9vs/david_althaus-s-shortform#ji6fvJnnnPdf2GTTW">here</a> for a longer quote. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-115">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-116"><p>CEV and the long reflection aren’t mutually exclusive and are in fact motivated by the same concern: many of humanity’s current, unreflected values are suboptimal and further reflection —in the case of CEV, with the assistance of aligned or “friendly” AI—would hopefully improve them and lead to (massively) better outcomes, for (almost) everyone involved. More broadly, there are other contexts besides AI alignment where preference idealization could play an important role. Many thorny philosophical questions related to preference idealization also arise when considering collective moral deliberation (like the viatopia and long-reflection ideas discussed earlier) and when contemplating transhumanist self-modification and enhancement. For much more detail on this, see Joe Carlsmith’s essay “<a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2021/06/21/on-the-limits-of-idealized-values">On the limits of idealized values</a>”. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-116">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-117"><p>For example, Bostrom's discussion of '<a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/topics/indirect-normativity">indirect normativity</a>' (2014b, ch. 13), Gabriel’s (2020) discussion of “informed preferences or desires”, or Chen (2023). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-117">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-118"><p>Interestingly, Yudkowsky's original CEV document contains an extended thought experiment on this topic. He suggests that if a terrorist group were savvy enough to create an aligned superintelligence, this might require sufficient intellectual humility and moral caution that they would realize the need to aim their AI at an idealized core of deep moral principles, and to "include all the world" in the process of moral extrapolation, rather than simply feeding in a list of specific commandments. He suggests that such a process of idealization might successfully shave off most of the moral rough edges of the group's fanatical ideology. However, this argument relies on a selection effect that may not hold. A fanatical group could plausibly steal or adapt existing alignment technology rather than developing it from scratch—acquiring the technical capability without any philosophical humility. More broadly, fanatical regimes throughout history have <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.9gvrqhy2vpf0">developed advanced technological capabilities</a> (nuclear weapons, rockets, etc.) without any corresponding epistemic improvement. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-118">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-119"><p>In fact, many humans seem to prefer vindication of their existing beliefs over honest feedback. This may explain why Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback tends to produce sycophantic AI behavior (Sharma et al., <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13548">2023</a>)—RLHF raters tend to reward AIs when they tell them what they want to hear. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-119">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-120"><p>Compare Selengut (2017, emphasis added): “[...B]ut what about fundamentalists and other religious traditionalists who refuse to compromise what they see as the word of God? These Orthodox believers, [...] <i>rather than compromise their beliefs</i>, they <i>seek to remake reality to fit their religious cognitions and expectations.</i> They engage in militant transformation to force all others to accept their religious beliefs and demand that society be based on their religious views. [...] “[Religious fanatics] refuse to compromise their beliefs and reduce their longing for the fulfillment of sacred prophecies. <i>If reality challenges their beliefs, reality must be changed to fit religious truth.</i>” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-120">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-121"><p>Similarly, some <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/LpkXtFXdsRd4rG8Kb/reducing-long-term-risks-from-malevolent-actors">malevolent</a> humans may also <i>reflectively</i> <i>endorse</i> their (sadistic) preferences. Malevolent preferences and ideological fanaticism may also interact and reinforce each other: the ideology allows people to fulfill their sadistic preferences while simultaneously enabling them to preserve their virtuous self-image. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-121">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-122"><p>That being said, there might be ways to construct idealization procedures that enforce genuinely epistemically neutral learning—one could even convince some fanatics to embrace such processes if framed as confirming their certainty ("If you're truly right, more knowledge can only vindicate you"). Designing such procedures could be important future work, though it remains challenging given fanatics' skill at incorporating contradictory evidence into existing worldviews. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-122">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-123"><p>Gabriel (2020) makes a related point: the challenge isn't to identify the "true" moral theory and encode it in machines, but rather to find fair processes for determining which values to encode—processes that don't simply allow some people to impose their views on others. This is precisely the problem that fanaticism poses. Fanatics are not interested in fair processes or reasonable pluralism; they want their values to win. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-123">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-124"><p>More precisely, 19.2% of participants had “actively open-minded thinking style” scores below the midpoint, indicating that on average they leaned towards disagreement with statements like “People should always take into consideration evidence that goes against their beliefs”. Instead of seeking truth, people prioritize feeling good about themselves and the world, maintaining their worldview and sense of meaning, purpose, and identity, and being seen as moral, high-status, intelligent, and loyal by their in-group. Many EAs and rationalists might be falling prey to a typical mind fallacy here, in assuming that most people value having true beliefs and epistemic rationality as much as they do. More generally, many WEIRD people may overestimate how widespread certain characteristics of WEIRD psychology are (like impartiality and moral universalism), see footnote 127. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-124">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-125"><p>This isn’t primarily due to misinformation; fake news comprises only 0.15% of Americans’ daily media diet (Allen et al., 2020). People’s beliefs are misguided less because they were misled and more because they are motivated to hold these wrong beliefs. As Drutman (<a href="https://leedrutman.substack.com/p/revealed-exposed-unbelievable-the">2023c</a>) and Williams (<a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/misinformation-researchers-are-wrong">2024a</a>, <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/the-deep-and-unavoidable-roots-of">2024b</a>) argue, misinformation is primarily a demand-side rather than supply-side problem: social and psychological factors—like partisan animosity, perceived loss of status, inequality, grievances and frustrations, fitting in with one’s tribe, signaling virtue, et cetera—create a demand for content that confirms existing beliefs and provides psychological relief. People don’t typically hold incorrect beliefs simply because they lack access to accurate information (e.g., <a href="https://psycnet.apa.org/fulltext/2022-15515-005.html">Scheufele et al., 2021</a>). Instead, as Williams (<a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/economics-and-philosophy/article/marketplace-of-rationalizations/41FB096344BD344908C7C992D0C0C0DC">2023</a>) notes, political media may function more as a “rationalization market” where people seek sophisticated justifications for their preferred beliefs. These issues not only affect low-information voters but also highly educated elites and academics (Williams, <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/on-highbrow-misinformation">2025c</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-125">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-126"><p>Much of this is happening <i>subconsciously</i> (Simler &amp; Hanson, <a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/0190495995">2018</a>), for evolutionary reasons (<a href="https://www.amazon.com/Why-Everyone-Else-Hypocrite-Evolution/dp/0691154392">Kurzban, 2012</a>). To be fair, it seems plausible that most humans’ <i>idealized</i> preferences would prioritize truth-seeking but this isn’t obvious and may depend on the precise idealization procedure. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-126">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-127"><p>Several aspects of WEIRD psychology seem also beneficial from the perspective of reducing risks of ideological fanaticism and making the long reflection work well (see table 1.1 “Key elements in WEIRD psychology”, Henrich, 2020): Less conformity and less deference to tradition/elders; Impartial principles over contextual particularism; Trust, fairness, honesty, and cooperation with anonymous others, strangers, and impersonal institutions; Muted concerns for revenge; Reduced in-group favoritism; Moral universalism. Of course, many aspects of WEIRD psychology seem neutral and others seem worse, particularly overconfidence. With all of that said, we obviously should value the perspectives of other cultures, perhaps even more so than seems intuitive: historically, most people have been too xenophobic and enamored with their own values and customs, and most Western thinkers, certainly pre 1950, were insufficiently critical of racism, colonialism, and Western imperialism. As discussed <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.sd6wdd9t3emd">above</a>, some of the worst atrocities relating to ideological fanaticism actually occurred in WEIRD societies. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-127">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-128"><p>Wei Dai makes this point <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/Hhtvwx2ka4pzoWg7e/ai-alignment-shouldn-t-be-conflated-with-ai-moral?commentId=mcFFDSaBboJRJZLrB">here</a>: "I tend to think that people's selfish desires will be fairly easily satiated once everyone is much much richer and the more "scalable" "moral" values would dominate resource consumption at that point [...]." <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-128">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-129"><p>Bostrom (<a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/ai-creation-and-the-cosmic-host.pdf">2024</a>a) argues: “Human values appear to be quite resource-satiable: we would much rather have a 100% chance of being able to use 1 galaxy to meet our goals than to have a 1% chance of being able to use 100 galaxies.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-129">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-130"><p>The increased variance in the behavior of fanatics in post-AGI scenarios may be particularly concerning from an s-risk perspective. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-130">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-131"><p>Preserving US democracy seems far from guaranteed. See, e.g., <a href="https://www.metaculus.com/questions/36389/us-no-longer-a-democracy-by-2030/">relevant</a> <a href="https://www.metaculus.com/questions/14141/us-continuously-a-liberal-democracy/">questions</a> on Metaculus. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-131">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-132"><p>For a brief discussion on safeguarding liberal democracy more generally, see <a href="https://80000hours.org/problem-profiles/liberal-democracy/">Koehler (2022)</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-132">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-133"><p>See also <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-why-were-polarized">Book Review: Why We're Polarized</a> (Astral Codex Ten, 2021): “Every so often, people ask what an effective altruism of politics would look like. If you [...] wanted to improve (US) politics as much as possible [...] what would you do? <i>Why We’re Polarized</i> and the rest of Klein’s oeuvre make a strong case that you would try to do something about polarization. Solve that, and a lot of the political pathologies of the past few decades disappear, and the country gets back on track.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-133">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-134"><p>Arguably some political opponents are indeed existential enemies. But it usually doesn’t make sense to believe that, say, all members of the opposing party are existential enemies, let alone critics of one’s own strategy. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-134">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-135"><p>Tim Urban (2023) depicts polarized political tribes as Golems: hulking, mindless creatures locked in perpetual combat, each animated and sustained by the other's hostility. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-135">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-136"><p>See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1aoCDzU-wB8lOP0C-HxM8HqkIX-e9n1deVUG7VBWte_k/edit?tab=t.0">here for a short summary by Claude</a>. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-136">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-137"><p>Beyond the negative emotions of fear and anger that fuel polarization, there may also be powerful positive psychological rewards. People can experience a psychological rush and a sense of self-righteous clarity and purpose from being part of the team that fights evil. This mirrors the experiences of some soldiers who fight in wars. (E.g., in his memoir Merry Hell!, Thomas Dinesen expresses how he greatly enjoyed parts of his WWI experience—the fighting, the rush, and the activity. See also the "band of brothers" phenomenon where soldiers report intense bonds and even nostalgia for combat.) This suggests that polarization and fanaticism may be self-reinforcing not just through fear and hatred, but also through the intoxicating sense of belonging and meaning, and even excitement that comes from being part of a righteous struggle against evil. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-137">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-138"><p>Though recent discussions of “<a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2025/07/03/abundance-klein-thompson-trump-biden-clinton-democrats/">abundance</a>” (cf. Klein &amp; Thompson, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_(Klein_and_Thompson_book)">2025</a>) may also provide a possible path to making politics less polarized. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-138">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-139"><p>Drutman <a href="https://leedrutman.substack.com/p/a-healthy-democracy-requires-healthy">(2023b</a>): “The most promising and doable pro-party reforms are fusion voting and proportional representation. Fusion voting allows multiple parties to endorse the same candidate, encouraging new party formation. Proportional representation ends the single-member district and makes it possible for multiple parties to win a proportional share of representation in larger, multi-member districts.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-139">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-140"><p><a href="https://www.newamerica.org/political-reform/reports/more-parties-better-parties/">Drutman (2023a)</a> also writes: “Illiberal extremism follows from a binary, highly polarized party system, because extremism emerges from radicalized in-group/outgroup conflict. Thus, the party system requires change. Breaking the core problem of escalating binary, us-versus-them competition requires adding new parties to realign and reorient partisan competition.” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-140">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-141"><p>For instance, Vinding (<a href="https://magnusvinding.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/03/reasoned-politics.pdf">2022</a>, ch.14): “Parliamentary systems appear to have significantly lower levels of political polarization, and are generally more stable, more peaceful, and less prone to coups (Santos, 2020, p. 1, ch. 1; Casal Bértoa &amp; Rama, 2021). They also tend to have “better corruption control, bureaucratic quality, rule of law, […] and literacy” (Gerring et al., 2009; Santos, 2020, p. 47).”<br />However, transforming the US into a parliamentary system seems very intractable and its benefits would plausibly be smaller than those of proportional representation (Mainwaring &amp; Lee Drutman, <a href="https://protectdemocracy.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/The-Case-for-Multiparty-Presidentialism.pdf">2023</a>). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-141">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-142"><p>Improving epistemics directly is another avenue for reducing polarization and fanaticism. This includes books (like Julia Galef’s <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Scout-Mindset-People-Things-Clearly-ebook/dp/B07L2HQ26K"><i>Scout Mindset</i></a> or Steven Pinker’s <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Rationality-What-Seems-Scarce-Matters/dp/B08X4X4SQ4"><i>Rationality</i></a>) and educational resources (like <a href="https://www.clearerthinking.org/clearer-thinking-tools">Clearer Thinking</a>) to promote better reasoning. More scalable approaches might include promoting greater adoption of prediction markets and a variety of AI-based interventions (discussed below). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-142">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-143"><p>Organizations working on structural reforms include <a href="https://www.fixourhouse.org/">Fix Our House</a>, <a href="https://protectdemocracy.org/work/fusion-voting-explained/">Protect Democracy</a>, and <a href="https://www.newamerica.org/political-reform/research-areas/voting-electoral-and-local-reform/fusion-voting/">New America</a>. Those focused on cultural change include the <a href="https://bipartisanpolicy.org/">Bipartisan Policy Center</a>, which creates spaces for cross-party negotiation, and groups like <a href="https://braverangels.org/">Braver Angels</a> and <a href="https://www.moreincommon.com/">More in Common</a> that work to reduce affective polarization at the grassroots level. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-143">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-144"><p>Why single out classical liberalism and Enlightenment principles and not, say, utilitarianism or the core principles of <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/R8AAG4QBZi5puvogR/effective-altruism-in-the-age-of-agi">effective altruism</a>? While we’re fans, classical liberalism seems to have several advantages. It has already influenced many <a href="https://www.usconstitution.net/enlightenment-ideas-in-the-constitution/">government</a> <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life,_Liberty_and_the_pursuit_of_Happiness">constitutions</a> and is <i>time-tested</i>: Societies founded on Enlightenment principles consistently score highest on objective metrics of human flourishing, ranging from GDP per capita and life expectancy to self-reported life satisfaction and human rights protections. Classical liberalism is already widely supported and lies inside the Overton window; out of all influential ideologies, classical liberalism seems most compatible with the core principles of EA. It also represents an attractive compromise for almost all (non-fanatical) value systems. The procedural principles of classical liberalism (like rule of law, separation of powers, etc.) are also fairly concrete, while EA is more <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/FpjQMYQmS3rWewZ83/effective-altruism-is-a-question-not-an-ideology">abstract</a>, open to interpretation and may even run the risk of becoming <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/T975ydo3mx8onH3iS/ea-is-about-maximization-and-maximization-is-perilous">fanatical itself</a>. EA is also unlikely to become widely supported in the near future, especially since the FTX debacle. Finally, it is probably not an accident that many thinkers who are still widely admired today—such as Martin Luther King Jr., Nelson Mandela, the U.S. Founding Fathers, Bertrand Russell, Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, David Hume, Adam Smith, and Mill himself—were deeply inspired by the Enlightenment. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-144">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-145"><p>Indeed, Enlightenment thinkers themselves have been far from perfect. Kant, for example, had an oddly intense <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kantian_ethics#Sexual_ethics">preoccupation with masturbation</a>, and some Enlightenment thinkers espoused views that were clearly racist or sexist. But on the whole, most Enlightenment thinkers had much better attitudes compared to their contemporaries—and crucially, their philosophical framework contains the tools for self-correction and moral progress. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-145">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-146"><p>Examples include <a href="https://www.persuasion.community"><i>Persuasion</i></a>, <a href="https://www.economist.com/"><i>The Economist</i></a>, <a href="https://stevenpinker.com/">Steven Pinker</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McWhorter">John McWhorter</a>, <a href="https://www.slowboring.com/">Matthew Yglesias</a>, <a href="https://www.samharris.org/">Sam Harris</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deeyah_Khan">Deeyah Khan</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coleman_Hughes">Coleman Hughes</a>, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claire_Lehmann">Claire Lehmann</a>, <a href="https://substack.com/@helenpluckrose">Helen Pluckrose</a>, <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/">Scott Alexander</a>, <a href="https://heterodoxacademy.org/">Heterodox Academy</a>, and <a href="https://www.thefire.org/">FIRE</a>, among many others who have remained true to classical liberal principles even when facing pressures from all sides of the political spectrum. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-146">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-147"><p>In this context, it’s worth mentioning how many highly successful entrepreneurs—like Elon Musk, Dario Amodei, Sergey Brin, or Jensen Huang—are (second-generation) immigrants. More generally, the top performing researchers and entrepreneurs tend to produce a <a href="https://80000hours.org/2021/05/how-much-do-people-differ-in-productivity/">disproportionate amount of the value</a> in their field; attracting these people is especially useful. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-147">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-148"><p>Open Philanthropy has granted <a href="https://www.openphilanthropy.org/grants/?q=high-skilled+immigration">several million dollars</a> towards high-skilled immigration reform, as well as over $9 million to the Institute for Progress, a think tank with policy research and advocacy on both <a href="https://ifp.org/category/high-skilled-immigration/">high-skilled immigration</a> and <a href="https://ifp.org/category/emerging-technology/">compute governance</a> (a promising AI-related intervention for tackling risks of fanaticism that we cover below). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-148">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-149"><p>That being said, it’s important to address potential serious risks from increased immigration. First, a naive open border policy plausibly makes it easier for foreign spies to gain influential positions. Second, immigrants can negatively influence the culture and values of the country they move to. For example, <a href="https://edition.cnn.com/2016/04/11/europe/britain-muslims-survey/index.html">around half</a> of British Muslims believe that homosexuality should be illegal. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-149">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-150"><p>Many policy interventions aimed at increasing economic growth, bringing down the cost of living, or making the economy fairer by curtailing rent-seeking behavior may have an indirect anti-fanatical effect—provided growth reaches ordinary people, not just elites. Historically, fanatical ideologies seem to have found particularly fertile ground in times of societal turmoil, desperation, growing inequality, and economic contraction (cf. the political and economic woes of the Weimar Republic that preceded the rise of Nazism, Russian collapse in WWI preceding the October Revolution, or economic crisis in late-1970s Iran preceding the Islamic Revolution). Conversely, when most people experience rising living standards, they seem more amenable to reasonable, positive-sum thinking and less likely to fall prey to misguided populist ideas (cf. Bryan Caplan’s “<a href="https://www.econlib.org/library/Columns/y2004/Caplanidea.html"><i>The Idea Trap</i></a>”). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-150">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-151"><p>See the section “<a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1T-FEPbRKh0GlE8-HUnKgc3XU06S7zWmge1YdsWuEmZM/edit?tab=t.0#bookmark=id.h6a49haqck1u">Encouraging reform in illiberal countries</a>” in Appendix F for more details. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-151">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-152"><p>See also <a href="https://reducing-suffering.org/#cooperation_and_peace">Brian Tomasik’s writings on the benefits of cooperation</a>. See <a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1h3dRBJE41rZ2vY_HOcAZLH2PotxBUXy-HIyZFvYKNfI/edit?tab=t.0">here</a> for a more detailed exploration of these pathways. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-152">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-153"><p>In her book States and Social Revolutions, Skocpol argues that the revolutions are not simply caused by popular discontent but also often require the collapse of the state's administrative and military power. This "state breakdown" can be triggered by intense and unsuccessful geopolitical competition, i.e., being unable to cope with the military and fiscal pressures exerted by foreign rivals. Thus, international conflict can make states more vulnerable to revolutions. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-153">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-154"><p>Aside from the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#heading=h.oq6yrgkrwdea">already-discussed idea</a> of influencing individual regimes to be less fanatical or otherwise more cooperative. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-154">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-155"><p>For more detail on the dynamics around the feasibility of such a deal, see <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tUB_uvSqiw8&amp;pp=ygUTcmF0aW9uYWwgYW5pbWF0aW9ucw%3D%3D">this video</a> from Jackson Wagner and Rational Animations. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-155">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-156"><p>The CHIPS Act is <a href="https://youtu.be/lwlOwiee6Aw?si=gbsyQKWLLutbJUBu">under threat</a> at the time of writing. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-156">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-157"><p>To be clear, we’re not proposing anything new here. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-157">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-158"><p>We remain optimistic about export controls. DeepSeek managing to catch up to the frontier of US ‘thinking’ models in spite of existing export controls is an important case study; it demonstrates that algorithmic insights are still a key lever in AI progress, but has also revealed—via deployment difficulties, and quotes from DeepSeek’s founder—that compute <a href="https://www.chinatalk.media/p/deepseek-what-the-headlines-miss">remains a significant constraint</a> for them. Export controls may need to be widened and tightened up, but that doesn’t mean that they are ineffective. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-158">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-159"><p>We are excited about Longview’s <a href="https://www.longview.org/request-for-proposals-on-hardware-enabled-mechanisms-hems-for-ai-verification/">request for proposals on secure, governable chips</a>, as well as high-quality research and advocacy by groups including <a href="https://www.rand.org/global-and-emerging-risks/centers/technology-and-security-policy.html">RAND</a>, <a href="https://www.iaps.ai/">IAPS</a>, <a href="https://www.csis.org/programs/wadhwani-ai-center">CSIS</a>, <a href="https://www.cnas.org/publications/reports/secure-governable-chips">CNAS</a>, <a href="https://ifp.org/special-compute-zones/">IFP</a>, <a href="https://www.thefai.org/search?FAI%5BrefinementList%5D%5Btags.title%5D%5B0%5D=Artificial%20Intelligence">FAI</a>, <a href="https://theaipi.org/">AIPI</a>, <a href="https://encodeai.org/blog/">Encode</a>, and more. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-159">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-160"><p>Of course, frontier companies already have strong economic incentives to prevent losses of intellectual property. But these incentives don’t account for harms to wider society from misuse of powerful AI. Meanwhile, the incentives to steal AI intellectual property are high, since training runs are <a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/how-much-does-it-cost-to-train-frontier-ai-models">expensive</a>. Model weights are surprisingly compressed, although still <a href="https://milesbrundage.substack.com/p/why-im-not-a-security-doomer">sufficiently ‘chonky’</a> that security measures might be possible. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-160">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-161"><p>More generally, one idea would be to somehow <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/LpkXtFXdsRd4rG8Kb/reducing-long-term-risks-from-malevolent-actors#Developing_better_constructs_and_measures_of_malevolence">screen against</a> fanatical (and malevolent) traits in the people who shape or control TAI. In an ideal world, leading AI companies’ employees and relevant government officials would be screened for fanatical and malevolent traits. However, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QmoqgLThjATbAeyQr/what-is-malevolence-on-the-nature-measurement-and#The_Dark_Factor__D_">most existing</a> measures of malevolent traits carry extreme <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QmoqgLThjATbAeyQr/what-is-malevolence-on-the-nature-measurement-and#Methodological_problems_associated_with_measuring_dark_traits">methodological</a> <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QmoqgLThjATbAeyQr/what-is-malevolence-on-the-nature-measurement-and#Social_desirability_and_self_deception">limitations</a> that make them almost useless, and designing manipulation-proof measures of either malevolence or fanaticism would be a long process that we probably won’t have sufficient time or resources for. An even larger challenge is buy-in amongst important stakeholders like the US government or AI companies. Most AI companies or the US government won’t actually incorporate impartial fanaticism screenings into their hiring processes and reject otherwise-strong candidates who perform poorly on them. One could possibly screen for <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ahxGTaNdk6G5YD9vs/david_althaus-s-shortform?commentId=WbJy3yKdGjr9uwqry">undesirable traits in RLHF raters</a> though. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-161">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-162"><p>Stronger infosec would make it more difficult to insert secret loyalties. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-162">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-163"><p>This one-dimensional spectrum from “pure tools” to “autonomous beings” is a useful approximation for our purposes, though AIs actually vary along multiple dimensions. These include: degree of intent-alignment versus misalignment; whether they act sycophantically versus guide users toward truth; whether they optimize for existing versus reflectively-endorsed human preferences; and whether they’re autonomous versus tool-like. What matters for preventing fanaticism is ensuring AIs exhibit anti-fanatical characteristics (reason, truthfulness, compassion) regardless of where they fall on any of these dimensions—we want them to resist amplifying fanatical ideologies whether they’re functioning as obedient tools, advisory systems, or autonomous agents. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-163">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-164"><p>See also MacAskill &amp; Wiblin (<a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#ai-takeover-might-happen-anyway-should-we-rush-to-load-in-our-values-022529">2025</a>) making very similar arguments. See also MacAskill (<a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-to-make-the-future-better">2025d</a>, section 3.2). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-164">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-165"><p>AI alignment seems overall beneficial (partly because this seems to make AIs overall more benevolent, probably in part because most humans are comparatively benevolent; see also <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ifechgnJRtJdduFGC/emergent-misalignment-narrow-finetuning-can-produce-broadly">emergent misalignment</a>.) However, intent-alignment <i>could</i> in principle backfire if it allows “<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#estimating-the-global-scale-of-ideological-fanaticism">misaligned humans</a>” to wield intent-aligned AIs to amass immense power. Indeed, one could argue that sufficiently wise and benevolent AIs might reasonably want to constrain humanity's reach at least somewhat—some humans don't seem particularly benevolent, and, from the impartial point of view of the universe, it's unclear whether homo sapiens, given our history, should be trusted completely with the entire lightcone. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-165">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-166"><p>This isn’t to say that today’s approaches (here and elsewhere) will necessarily scale to future, more-powerful systems. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-166">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-167"><p>Cf. MacAskill (2025a): “[...] What should be in the model spec? How should AI behave in the countless different situations it finds itself in? To what extent should we be trying to create pure instruction-following AI (with refusals for harmful content) vs AI that has its own virtuous character?” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-167">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-168"><p>Joe Carlsmith puts it nicely <a href="https://joecarlsmith.substack.com/i/157049154/how-does-this-version-of-the-alignment-problem-fit-into-the-bigger-picture">here</a>: “I want advanced AI to strengthen, fuel, and participate in good processes in our civilization – processes that create and reflect things like wisdom, consciousness, joy, love, beauty, dialogue, friendship, fairness, cooperation, and so on. [...] And AIs aren’t just <i>tools</i> in this respect – they can be, in a richer sense, <i>participants</i>, <i>citizens,</i> and perhaps, ultimately, <i>successors</i> [...].” <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-168">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-169"><p>More speculatively, AI may also be able to help with (moral) philosophy and “wisdom”—though see especially Wei Dai’s <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/4xwWDLfMenw48TR8c/long-reflection-reading-list#Metaphilosophy__AI_philosophical_competence">concerns</a> <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/axSfJXriBWEixsHGR/ai-doing-philosophy-ai-generating-hands">here</a>. Some relevant discussion is also scattered through <a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#intellectual-advances-outside-technology-are-similarly-important-002857">this podcast</a> with Will MacAskill. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-169">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-170"><p>DeepSeek, for instance, might not be able to help, given that it <a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2025/jan/28/we-tried-out-deepseek-it-works-well-until-we-asked-it-about-tiananmen-square-and-taiwan">censors topics</a> that contradict the Chinese Communist Party’s preferred narratives. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-170">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-171"><p>For example, MacAskill (<a href="https://futureoflife.org/podcast/moral-uncertainty-and-the-path-to-ai-alignment-with-william-macaskill/">2018</a>) seems to have had such a system in mind: “One thing you could do is just say, ‘Well, we've got ten billion people alive today, let's say. We're gonna divide the universe into ten billionths, so maybe that's a thousand galaxies each or something.’ And then you can trade after that point. I think that would get a pretty good outcome.” MacAskill seems now more pessimistic about such proposals (cf. “<a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/will-macaskill-century-in-a-decade-navigating-intelligence-explosion/#we-should-aim-for-more-than-mere-survival-023753">We should aim for more than mere survival</a>” towards the end of the episode). <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-171">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-172"><p>Collective decision-making would plausibly block the most disvaluable outcomes, since fanatics will (most likely) remain a minority. However, fanatics could still use their voting bloc to bargain for harmful concessions or perhaps even legitimize (parts of) their worldview within the system, and naively designed governance could give disproportionate bargaining power to bad actors. Supermajority voting schemes could help perhaps reduce such risks (cf. MacAskill and Hadshar, <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/intelsat-as-a-model-for-international-agi-governance#weighted-voting-with-supermajorities-for-important-decisions">2025</a>), though they may increase the likelihood that minorities can veto outcomes that would be very good for most other value systems. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-172">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-173"><p>Of course, the choice isn't simply about including or excluding certain factions once and for all. More realistic governance frameworks will probably feature more <i>iterative</i> decision-making across many smaller questions (and hopefully conditions designed to gradually shift values toward reasonableness over time). But all such approaches must still grapple with difficult boundary questions: what precisely counts as intolerable, and according to whom? <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-173">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-174"><p>The actors who first develop aligned superintelligence would possess extraordinary bargaining power in shaping such a charter. But many other actors might also (indirectly) influence the outcome. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-174">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-175"><p>Right now, although the enforcement is (very) imperfect, human rights violations are outlawed by international rules and institutions, such as the <a href="https://www.ohchr.org/en/hrbodies/hrc/home">UN Human Rights Council</a> and the <a href="https://www.icc-cpi.int/">International Criminal Court</a>. Outlawing certain acts in the post-AGI world is a natural extension of this idea, and existing institutions may provide a foundation to build upon. How to monitor and enforce these provisions across intergalactic space is a further area for future work. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-175">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-176"><p>We’d probably want to apply such a principle universally: just as we'd block fanatics from creating what others consider extreme disvalue, we should also block actions that impose extreme disvalue on other moral perspectives, including those of fanatics (for instance, gratuitously burning holy books), at least unless there are very strong reasons for doing so. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-176">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-177"><p>For further details, see “<a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1As4OTRW96P2GtUztAN3rEteM7QMc3jaRWph5ogUl9ng/edit?tab=t.0">Ideological fanaticism: Causes</a>”. Note that this is an extremely unpolished and unfinished exploration of causes. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-177">↩︎</a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-178"><p>It’s plausible that the negative effects of social media are <a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/the-case-against-social-media-is">exaggerated</a> (though see here for <a href="https://arachnemag.substack.com/p/the-case-against-social-media-is">counterarguments</a>). Williams (<a href="https://www.conspicuouscognition.com/p/is-social-media-destroying-democracyor">2025b</a>) argues that the problem isn't primarily that algorithms manipulate people into extremism, but rather that social media's democratizing character reveals and amplifies pre-existing popular demand for extreme content that elite gatekeepers previously excluded from mainstream discourse. However, it still seems plausible that changing social media <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/xzjQvqDYahigHcwgQ/aligning-recommender-systems-as-cause-area">recommendation algorithms</a> to incentivize reason and truth-seeking over tribalism and outrage is both possible and beneficial. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-bxXhDCzXq8bJcuQWw-178">↩︎</a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFewetPnb8KJgcER7/long-term-risks-from-ideological-fanaticism-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What We Learned from Briefing 140+ Lawmakers on the Threat from AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/A7BtBD9BAfK2kKSEr/what-we-learned-from-briefing-140-lawmakers-on-the-threat)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 12, 2026 7:53 PM GMT<br /><br /><p>Back in May 2025, I published a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xwrajm92fdjd7cqnN/what-we-learned-from-briefing-70-lawmakers-on-the-threat"><u>post</u></a> titled “What We Learned from Briefing 70+ Lawmakers on the Threat from AI”. I was taken aback by the positive reception that this post had, and have appreciated the kind feedback through online forums and in-person conversations.</p><p>I’ve doubled the number of meetings since writing that post and I’ve been wanting to expand on it for a while. I wouldn’t say I’ve learned twice as much! But I have learned some other things, so here’s an update I hope you’ll find helpful.</p><p>If you haven’t read my previous <a href="https://leticiagarciamartinez.substack.com/p/what-we-learned-from-briefing-70"><u>post</u></a> from May 2025, I would recommend starting there: it contains what I consider the core insights, whereas this one builds on those ideas and addresses some questions I’ve received since.</p><p>If you have not come across <a href="http://campaign.controlai.com/"><u>ControlAI</u></a> before or wish to read an update on our UK parliamentary campaign, you can find more information further down.</p><p><strong>Sample size, characteristics, and time frame</strong></p><ul><li>Between September 2024 and February 2026, I delivered over 150 parliamentary meetings with cross-party UK parliamentarians and their teams to discuss and collaborate on tackling the threat posed by superintelligent AI systems.</li><li>Of these, 140 were introductory briefings to familiarise parliamentarians with the topic and establish working relationships, while the remainder were follow-up sessions to reconvene on the issue and advise on specific initiatives.</li><li>Of those 140 initial briefings, 126 were delivered directly to parliamentarians, while only 14 were delivered exclusively to staffers.</li><li>The composition of the sample was as follows: 42% were Members of the House of Commons (MPs), 35% were Members of the House of Lords (Peers), and 22% were devolved parliamentarians from the Scottish, Welsh, and Northern Irish legislatures.</li><li>Most meetings were attended by two members of ControlAI’s team, with a few exceptions where I attended alone.</li></ul><h2><strong>Part I: Attention is All You Need</strong></h2><p><strong>Betting on common knowledge</strong></p><p>In September 2024, we began briefing parliamentarians and asking them to support a <a href="https://controlai.com/statement"><u>campaign statement</u></a>. The objective was to build common knowledge about the problem of the extinction risk posed by superintelligence, and encourage them to take a public stance. A public stance is a tangible, verifiable signal that they understand the issue, care about it, and want others to know. Our UK campaign statement reads as follows:</p><blockquote><p>“Nobel Prize winners, AI scientists, and CEOs of leading AI companies have stated that mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority.</p><p>Specialised AIs - such as those advancing science and medicine - boost growth, innovation, and public services. Superintelligent AI systems would compromise national and global security.</p><p>The UK can secure the benefits and mitigate the risks of AI by delivering on its promise to introduce binding regulation on the most powerful AI systems.”</p></blockquote><p>As of February 2026, over 100 parliamentarians have supported this campaign. Its purpose was to raise awareness of the problem and build a coalition of lawmakers that want to tackle it. As parliamentarians came to understand the issue more fully, we were able to deepen our conversations and focus more directly on policy solutions: specifically, the case for a prohibition in the foreseeable future, given that superintelligence cannot be developed safely or controllably.</p><p>As a result of this sustained engagement, an increasing number of parliamentarians are now speaking openly about the threat from superintelligence and the need for such a prohibition.
